PSG wants to extend Achraf Hakimi but…

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

PSG shows its muscles. With a very overhauled team, the French champion managed last night to score a 9th consecutive victory in all competitions. This time, it was Le Havre which was no match. Despite Josh Maja’s opener, the capital club never panicked, confident in its strengths to take 5 (5-1 victory) against the Normans, more than ever last in the Ligue 1 standings. Captain of the evening in the absence of Marquinhos, Achraf Hakimi again produced a great performance, in line with his start to the season.

With a hat-trick and two assists, the Moroccan was undoubtedly named man of the match by our editorial team (10/10). “It’s incredible for a full-back! My training? It’s that of a full-back. Afterwards the coach helped me a lot, by giving me freedom, to take space, to cover me when I am in attackhe reacted yesterday at the microphone of League 1+. I am happy and comfortable on the pitch. I feel very good. I feel comfortable playing with these teammates, it’s easy. On the pitch, I am well in front of the goal. I try to have fun.»

His trainer is also won over, he who has made him one of his base men. “We know its quality perfectly. He’s an attacker. He is higher than the attackers sometimes. He is the best right back in the world. I’m not excited about his three goals. What I like is to see him all the time at a very high level“, he explained at a press conference. No surprise then to know that PSG wishes to extend its contract, which currently runs until 2029, reveals The Team. It doesn’t matter what legal case the Moroccan is facing.

As a reminder, his trial for rape should be held in 2027, after the Court of Cassation confirmed his referral to justice. Presumed innocent, the side has always firmly contested these accusations. His club does not seem any more worried than that either, showing its desire to extend it. Except that according to the sports daily, Hakimi’s entourage has somewhat slowed down the enthusiasm of the Parisian management. He believes that there is still time before thinking about what comes next, especially since the last extension is only two years old. Simple temperance or first signs of a possible departure? Hakimi has never hidden behind the scenes his desire to one day return to Real Madrid…

Sheffield Wednesday vs Plymouth Argyle predictions with opinion split over Dave Challinor’s first League One game

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Sheffield Wednesday face Plymouth Argyle in Dave Challinor‘s first league match in charge of the club. The SWFC boss is tasked with righting the ship, with a four-game winless run in the league leaving them 9th in the table and six points off the automatic promotion spots.

The Owls were beaten in the FA Cup by Oldham Athletic in Challinor’s first game, before a youthful side lost 4-0 to Accrington Stanley in the EFL Trophy on Wednesday night. The new boss will be determined to get off the mark in an important game in the third tier.

As for Plymouth Argyle, they are three points better off than the Owls having played one more game. They sit up in 5th as it stands, winning seven of their 14 League One matches to date.

Unlike their upcoming opponents, the Pilgrims were victorious in their cup matches with wins over Alvechurch and Swindon Town in the FA Cup and EFL Trophy respectively. In their last league game, Tom Cleverley‘s men played out a 0-0 draw against Reading.

“Dave Challinor has led two Sheffield Wednesday games so far, but this feels like the first one that really means something. Points are up for grabs in the bid to climb back up the League One table and after poor results in the cups, he will be determined to get a tune out of his side and put a first win on the board.

“The SWFC boss played a side strong enough to beat Oldham Athletic and he was not impressed as they came up short. It means you have to anticipate changes for a tricky game against Plymouth Argyle.

“The Pilgrims are a good side, but they’ve got some notable absentees through injury. Aribim Pepple and Alex Mitchell are major doubts for the trip to Hillsborough and they will be big misses against an Owls team hungry for all three points.

“Argyle’s away record is strong though, with four wins in the last five League One games on the road. However, I do think their injuries will weaken them here, so I lean towards a valuable win for Challinor and Wednesday.”

“Dave Challinor needs to get off to a winning start with Sheffield Wednesday at Hillsborough this weekend. If SWFC lose, he will be on the back foot already after back-to-back cup losses to Oldham Athletic and Accrington Stanley. In fairness, he played a weakened team against the latter.

“The Owls’ recent form has been dire and their new head coach has a big job on his hands as he looks to get them on track. They have won only one in their last eight matches in all competitions.

“Plymouth Argyle should fancy their chances of getting something in South Yorkshire. PAFC are unbeaten in their last four and will see their upcoming opponents as a promotion rival.

“It is important that the Pilgrims don’t lose on Saturday and they need to be solid at the back. I think Tom Cleverley’s side will have enough to bag themselves a point.”

Faulty Wedding Cake pavilion to be dismantled at Waddesdon Manor

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Photography by Chris Lacey and Merriman Photography

A 12-metre-high pavilion shaped like a wedding cake is set to be dismantled at Waddesdon Manor in Buckinghamshire, UK, after it was closed over health and safety concerns.

A spokesperson for the Grade I-listed country house told the BBC that the pavilion, which was installed in 2023, was not “open for quite as long as hoped” due to faults in its ceramic tiles.

The sculptural pavilion, simply named Wedding Cake, was designed by Portuguese artist Joana Vasconcelos as a permanent garden pavilion for the grounds of Waddesdon Manor.

Its three tiers are wrapped in glazed, pastel-toned ceramic tiles made by the historic Portuguese tile manufacturer Viúva Lamego and intended to look like cake icing. They are now faulty, forcing the structure to close because of concerns about their safety.

According to the BBC, a report by a Buckinghamshire Council officer found that no viable solution to repair the faulty tiles had been identified.

As such, the council has approved a change to the listed building consent, allowing the sculpture to be removed. As the manor and its grounds are protected sites, permission must be obtained to carry out such work.

The structure must now be removed no later than January 2036. However, the Rothschild Foundation, which manages the estate, wants the sculpture removed as soon as possible, according to the BBC.

The revised consent also requires the surrounding landscape to be returned to grass within six months of its removal.

When the pavilion was completed, its designer Vasconcelos told Dezeen it was her “most ambitious project to date”.

Her use of tiles reflected her ambition to use ceramics and textiles to challenge traditional hierarchies of what she called “noble materials”, such as marble, in grand architectural settings.

The manufacturer Viúva Lamego’s standard 14-by-14 centimetre tiles determined the size of the overall structure. It has an 11-metre diameter, which was the smallest that could be achieved using the tiles.

The pavilion is the fourth project by Vasconcelos created for the estate, which was built in the 1870s by Baron Ferdinand de Rothschild to entertain guests and showcase his ceramic collections.

A spokesperson for the manor said it plans to continue to commission artwork for its grounds.

“We are hoping to continue to showcase art in the landscape, including plans for a new contemporary sculpture trail in the grounds of Waddesdon Manor, which will open in July 2027,” they said.

Other projects by Vasconcelos featured on Dezeen include a 24-metre-long textile installation for Dior’s Autumn Winter 2023 catwalk show.

Elsewhere, we recently reported on the dismantling of Rike Concert Hall, designed by Italian practice Studio Fuksas. It is being demolished less than 15 years after it was completed, and before ever opening to the public.

Photography by Chris Lacey and Merriman Photography

This is a rig for 1440p gaming. This CyberPowerPC machine has an AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D, an Nvidia GeForce RX 9070 GPU, 32GB of DDR5 5600 RAM, and a 1TB SSD. It ships with Wi-Fi 6 connectivity.

It's all held together by a B650 motherboard, and ships inside one of CyberPower's more impressive cases, along with fans aplenty. You also get Wi-Fi 6, Bluetooth, lots of USB ports, HDMI and DisplayPort connectivity, and a liquid cooler for the CPU, as well as an 850-watt power supply. And, of course, a copy of Windows 11.

With Black Friday still a couple of weeks away, this is one of the best gaming PC deals going right now and one not to be missed.

Manchester United sack Michael Carrick 10 days before Liverpool showdown

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Manchester United have sacked Michael Carrick as head coach 10 days before their next fixture, which is against Liverpool at Anfield.

As was widely expected, the 45-year-old has paid the price for the Red Devils’ atrocious start to the season, with the Old Trafford outfit winning just one of their first 10 Premier League games and sitting in the relegation zone during the November international break.

Fabrizio Romano reported the news via X on Thursday morning, with other media outlets then confirming it before an official announcement from the club was communicated.

As per Sky Sports, Mauricio Pochettino is expected to be named as Carrick’s successor, with Man United having an agreement in place with the Argentine – currently in charge of the USA men’s national team – to take over after their CONCACAF Nations League in the coming week.

Should that impending appointment be completed in the anticipated timeframe, the former Tottenham and Chelsea boss would be in place in time for the Red Devils’ visit to Anfield on Sunday 22 November.

Want more Empire of the Kop coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

Although the practice of ‘new manager bounce’ isn’t exactly a scientific formula, a myriad of case studies – a few of which involve Man United – may have injected trepidation into Liverpool fans ahead of the grudge match in L4 on Sunday week.

Carrick himself took over a Red Devils team in turmoil last January and, in his first two games, oversaw wins over Manchester City and Arsenal, who ultimately finished second and first in the Premier League.

Eight years ago, after Jose Mourinho got the sack at Old Trafford following a comprehensive defeat at Anfield, Ole Gunnar Solskjaer triggered an immediate upturn in form which saw them pick up 25 points from a possible 27 in the top flight.

Managerial changes aside, Man United have an annoying habit of pulling out a result against Liverpool when it’s least expected. In January 2025, they were 14th in the table when they faced the table-topping Reds on Merseyside and came away with a 2-2 draw.

Iraola has already tasted defeat twice at Anfield this season (against Man City and Arsenal). Another on Sunday week would rankle the most of all, especially when our arch-rivals have been so abysmal over the past three months but conveniently trigger a change just in time for their visit to L4.

Ariana Grande Brings Her Two Great Loves—Ricky Álvarez and Rare Vintage—to the Red Carpet

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Ariana Grande brought two of her greatest loves to the red carpet last night: boyfriend Ricky Álvarez and a very, very good piece of vintage.

At the New York premiere of Focker-in-Law, the singer and actor made her first red carpet appearance with Álvarez since rekindling their romance earlier this year. And if their constant embraces, adoring glances, and kisses were anything to go by, the pair are very much still in the honeymoon phase.

Stylist Law Roach selected a vintage 1990s Badgley Mischka gown for the occasion. The delicate, spaghetti-strapped design featured a pale silver-gray windowpane check, a fitted bodice scattered with tiny beaded flowers, and a voluminous floor-skimming skirt. She accessorized with Swarovski Matrix hoop earrings—a recent favorite—and traded the flipped bob she’s been sporting of late for a sleek micro ponytail, tied with a little ivory ribbon. Makeup was also her go-to style of glowy and highlight-dominant.

Roach is on a particularly impressive archival streak of late. He’s been mining the fashion vaults for Kim Kardashian’s All's Fair press tour, pulling everything from 1990s Dolce & Gabbana to Paco Rabanne couture. Just last weekend, he dressed Anya Taylor-Joy in a spectacularly ruffled spring 1997 Pierre Cardin ensemble for the LACMA Art+Film Gala.

Grande has long been a devotee of fashion’s more whimsical, romantic history. Her love of vintage reached particularly fanciful heights during the Wicked press tours, but the singer has since held firmly to her soft, ultra-feminine aesthetic with 1950s silhouettes and diaphanous fabrics. At one point, her dedication to the look was so pronounced that some wondered whether she was quietly auditioning for a rumored Audrey Hepburn biopic. Roach revealed that he’d bought the Badgley Mischka gown months earlier, drawn to its connection with the era of the original Meet the Parents film.

Álvarez, for his part, kept things considerably more low-key. The dancer and photographer wore a relaxed black blazer over an open-neck white shirt, tucked into dark indigo jeans with rolled cuffs, a leather belt, and brown shoes. Some classic easy-going boyfriend dressing. He spent much of the evening with an arm around Grande’s waist, while she leaned into him, held his hands, and beamed up at him for the cameras.

Of course, the couple’s love story has its own history. Grande and Álvarez first met when he worked as a backup dancer on her Honeymoon Tour in 2015. They dated for around a year before splitting in 2016, and he later received a memorable name-check in her 2018 breakup anthem, “thank u, next.” Almost a decade later, however, things have come full circle. Rumors of a reunion began circulating this summer when the pair were spotted together in Austin, Texas, during Grande’s Eternal Sunshine tour, before more sightings followed in Florida and New York. By August, they were Instagram official, and Grande had debuted a new tattoo reading “I do love you”, seemingly matching a similar inscription on Álvarez’s collarbone. The two were also spotted arm-in-arm around London, where Grande completed her 10-night residency at the O2. She even began rewriting the Ricky lyrics in “thank u, next” onstage. At one Brooklyn show, she sang, “Wrote some songs about Ricky / We always find our way back.” By the tour’s September finale in London, the sentiment had become considerably more definitive: “I’m tryna make him my last.”

More recently, they’ve been enjoying some more ordinary couple activities, including cheering on runners at the New York City Marathon earlier this month. Grande has also enlisted Álvarez for a particularly kiss-heavy R.E.M. Beauty lip-product guessing game on Instagram. The adoration and affection is showing no signs of slowing down.

Between a decade-old romance finding new life and a beautiful dress pulled from the archives, Ariana Grande is making a persuasive case for revisiting the past.

Singapore Airlines Reconsiders New Business Class Seat Design After Backlash

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Last week, Singapore Airlines unveiled all-new cabins, with the most exciting developments being a new first class and new business class. The new cabins look great (nothing revolutionary, but a huge improvement), with one exception — the new business class seat colors. Not only do they look cheap, but they just seem completely mismatched to the rest of the cabin.

While the airline vehemently defended the color choices for several days, it seems that they’re at least now reconsidering it, though it remains to be seen if it’s too late to make a change…

On Wednesday, November 11, 2026, Singapore Airlines CEO Goh Choon Phong had a town hall with employees, as reported by The Business Times. During this, he was asked about the negative feedback the airline has received about the new business class seat design.

In response to this, he reportedly said “we’ll look at it, we’ll see what’s feasible.” So that signals some willingness to make changes, though admittedly with how far out seats are ordered, that’s not necessarily so straightforward, so I wouldn’t count on anything happening. He did, however, emphasize that customers will come to like the seats after experiencing its comforts for themselves.

At the town hall, Singapore Airlines Chief Commercial Officer Lee Lik Hsin said this was a good learning point for the airline, but indicated that he hopes customers will mind the colors less after experiencing the seats. He also reportedly said “I suppose, you know, it’s the 80-20 rule, you can’t please them all.”

At the time of launch, Singapore Airlines VP of Product Experience, Ong Wah Li, explained the new design was in response to customer feedback, and described it as “modern, fresh, yet elegant and refined, that’s suited to business travel.”

BMW Designworks was behind the design of the cabin, and CEO Julia de Bono defended the color choices at the launch event, describing them as “more refreshing and more progressive.” As she viewed it, “what I see when I look at it, it’s definitely like a nice suit, with the white and the metal really popping out, you know, it’s more like a suit with a sneaker, so a bit more modern and fresh.”

To state the obvious, the trim and finishes on a new business class seat are hardly the most pressing issues facing the world. However, Singapore Airlines prides itself in being perhaps the most detail oriented airline in the world, because management has an obsessive attention to even the smallest things.

So I can’t help but be a bit confused by all of this:

It also just feels to me like the mandate for the design of this product was off, as shared by the CEO of BMW Designworks. Premium travel demand is through the roof, and a lot of people in premium cabins aren’t traveling for business. For that matter, these cabins will be operating the world’s longest flights, so this isn’t a case of a business traveler hopping on a plane in a suit, and going straight to a meeting (at least that doesn’t make up a meaningful percentage of the traffic here).

Is “suit with a sneaker” really the vibe you want to go for with a new business class product that’s on an airline that’s known for its incredible warmth? It’s almost like the airline views warmth and modernity as being at odds with one another, which I don’t at all agree with.

For example, reader Sam asked AI to take the new seat, but preserve the old color concept, and below is what it spit out.

That looks so much more on brand for Singapore Airlines than what the airline actually arrived at.

I would’ve loved to be a fly on the wall when this design was finalized. Was everyone immediately onboard with this, and thought it looked great? Or was there some skepticism, but someone said “trust me, people will love this, this is what a modern cabin looks like?”

I just don’t understand how you can commit to investing in a project that costs billions of dollars, reportedly get feedback from hundreds of customers, and still end up so far off the pulse of public sentiment.

Last week, Singapore Airlines unveiled its new cabins, which is an exciting development. Everything looked pretty great, except the new business class seat finishes, which seem terribly mismatched to the brand. The airline claimed it wanted something modern and progressive, but I’m not sure “suit with sneakers” is really the aesthetic that the public wanted (or that the chosen finishes actually reflect that).

Singapore Airlines leadership claims it’s now looking into possibly updating finishes, but I wouldn’t count on anything coming of that, given how far along this is.

What do you make of this Singapore Airlines business class design fiasco? And I’m still waiting for someone to please tell me that they love the design, and think it’s great!

Xabi Alonso ready to let Liverpool transfer target leave Chelsea

Chelsea manager Xabi Alonso has reportedly decided that Malo Gusto is not in his first-team plans amid interest from Liverpool.

The France international has mostly shone during his time at Stamford Bridge, but so far this season he’s seen a reduced role under new manager Alonso.

The Spanish tactician took over at Chelsea in the summer and his use of wing-backs might not leave much room for Gusto, who has previously been linked with Liverpool by Simon Phillips.

Now a new report from Phillips’ Substack today states that Alonso has advised Gusto to find a new club as he is not in his plans as a regular starter.

It remains to be seen if this will definitely lead to Liverpool making a move for the 23-year-old, but it could be a move that makes sense for all parties.

Want more CaughtOffside coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

Gusto might not be every Liverpool fan’s dream signing, but the Merseyside giants could do with making a change in the right-back position.

That area of their squad has been an issue since Trent Alexander-Arnold left, with Jeremie Frimpong struggling since joining from Bayer Leverkusen.

Ronald Araujo, normally a centre-back, is now filling in pretty well on the right since joining Liverpool on loan from Barcelona, but that probably isn’t the best long-term solution.

Gusto could therefore be a good option for LFC to consider if he is indeed set to be available.

While this perhaps seems unlikely now, could Chelsea boss Alonso push for a swap deal involving Gusto and Frimpong?

Frimpong was superb in Alonso’s system at Leverkusen, so it could be a good fit, even if he’s struggled in English football so far.

Perhaps Liverpool wasn’t the right move for him, and a return to a system like Alonso’s could get his career back on track.

'I've seen him crying' - Sam Clucas reveals Nathan Jones truth at Stoke City

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Charlton Athletic boss Nathan Jones has never been one to hide his true emotions, with the Welsh boss wearing his heart on his sleeve, as former Stoke City midfielder Sam Clucas has revealed exactly what he is like behind the scenes.

Nathan Jones has cultivated an excellent coaching career for himself, working his way up the ranks at Yeovil Town, Charlton, and Brighton & Hove Albion before taking the top job at Luton Town, guiding the Hatters from League Two to League One.

He'd leave Kenilworth Road for Stoke City in January 2019, with Luton sat 2nd in the third-tier table at the time and the Potters struggling in the Championship following relegation from the Premier League.

His time at the bet365 Stadium went far from planned, but not for lack of trying, as revealed by former midfielder Sam Clucas.

Football League World has asked AI to rank the top 25 most demanding fanbases in British football right now

Clucas, 36, is now without a club as he enters the twilight of his playing career, but having worked his way up from the National League with Hereford United to Hull City in the Premier League, he joined Swansea City in the summer of 2017 for £16.5 million.

His time in South Wales didn't go as he had hoped, and 12 months later, he joined Stoke in the Championship for £6 million, initially under former Derby County boss Gary Rowett. Six months later, Jones was appointed as the Potters' new head coach, and Clucas has revealed exactly what he was like behind the scenes.

Speaking to The Football Lowdown podcast, Clucas said: "I loved him, to be honest with you.

"I think you'll see from a lot of his interviews that he's definitely a heart-on-his-sleeve type of guy, and believe me, that's not just for show.

"I've seen him crying in dressing rooms after games and stuff; a really emotional guy, but he puts his heart and soul into everything every day in training, his meetings, everything.

"I wanted it to work out so badly for him, to be honest with you, at Stoke, because you could see how much work he was putting in, and I believe he actually slept over at the training ground a few times."

Having succeeded Rowett at the helm in the Potteries, Jones would guide Stoke to 16th in the Championship that year, and over the summer the likes of Nick Powell, Jordan Cousins, Adam Davies, and Liam Lindsay joined under his watch.

However, the Welshman failed to get a tune out of his side, winning two of his first 14 league games in charge the following campaign and was swiftly dismissed.

With a win percentage of just 15.79 per cent, only Paul Lambert and Chris Kamara have a worse win percentage than Jones for Stoke, and of managers to have undertaken 20 games or more for the Potters, he is statistically the worst of all time.

Whenever he and Stoke meet, there is no love between the Welshman and the Potters' supporters, as despite his best efforts, he ultimately flopped in North Staffordshire.

Since leaving Stoke, Jones has returned to Luton, and followed that up with a brief spell with Southampton and now Charlton, with whom he has helped to establish in the Championship after winning promotion with the Addicks back in 2025.

Jones has tended to thrive as an underdog during his coaching career, and taking the Stoke job with the Potters just having been relegated from the Premier League, it was a disastrous fit for all parties.

The Welshman is certainly not the most popular of head coaches, though his heart-on-the-sleeve personality certainly makes for some box office moments, as he looks to steer Charlton up the second tier table this season.

‘Second ball’ may be a Sunday league mantra but Yates showed its top-flight relevance

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Midfielder’s old-school attributes came to the fore during Nottingham Forest’s win against Manchester City

Modern football is more systematised and data-driven than ever but some things never change. Winning the first ball is important but the second ball – the holy grail for Sunday league footballers – is often more so.

Nottingham Forest’s 3-1 win against Manchester City last weekend featured a classic example. Forest led 2-1 inside the last 20 minutes but City, lifted by Rayan Cherki’s sweet half-volley, were pushing for an equaliser.

Another City attack comes down their right: after Jair Cunha’s clearing header from a deep Matheus Nunes cross, the ball loops up in the air before descending inside the home penalty area.

Ryan Yates, Forest’s old-fashioned midfield general, is being pressed from behind by the Argentina international Enzo Fernández as the ball drops. Yates stands his ground, using his strength to hold off his opponent’s muscular challenge. Fernández tries to impose himself, too, but ends up on the turf.

Not only does Yates win that crucial one-on-one, by cleverly improvising an overhead kick to shift the ball away from danger he inadvertently begins a counterattack that arguably changes the course of Forest’s season.

Liam Delap shepherds the ball to Morgan Gibbs-White. Enzo Maresca’s team are hopelessly exposed and Gibbs-White devours the space in front of him with a driving run.

He lays a pass off to Delap, who takes one touch before swerving a sensational shot into the far corner for 3-1. It is a memorable move that will surely compete for goal of the month. But none of it would have been possible without the dirty work of Yates: a show of strength and desire from the captain at the other end of the pitch.

Oliver Glasner understands the importance of the moment and sprints to celebrate with his players. After a barren scoring run Delap suddenly has two goals in one afternoon. The home fans are delirious. Twelve seconds after Yates won the duel, Forest have a two-goal cushion rather than a desperate fight to maintain their lead.

The second ball is a pleasingly old-school term but remains of paramount importance. Visit your local amateur club and you are likely to hear it being shouted as a motivational mantra.

At grassroots level it’s a reminder to teammates that finesse and creativity are all very well, but it’s hard work, desire and a refusal to let your opponent get the better of you that win matches.

And it remains part of the elite game’s lexicon. Premier League managers sometimes point to upcoming opponents’ second-ball prowess – a back-handed compliment if ever there was one. The hidden meaning: “They feed off scraps at set pieces. They don’t create from open play.”

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

After seeing Southampton Under-21s lose to Wealdstone in the National League Cup a couple of years ago, I mentioned to the then Saints manager, Simon Rusk, that his goalkeeper had been shouting “second ball” throughout.

“To be honest,” Rusk replied with amusing frankness, “I’m not sure some of them even know what second ball means.”

Managers like relying on data. They like predictable outcomes. They prefer to remove risk, reduce chaos and exert control over as much of the game as possible. But while the outcome of a one-on-one duel can never be certain, the consequences can often be far-reaching. That’s why second balls matter.

Against City Yates was named in Forest’s starting lineup, and given the captain’s armband, for the first time this season. Glasner, asked how he had motivated his players, revealed: “Ryan Yates spoke. I played more than 500 games as a player, I’ve managed, I don’t know, 500 games … and I’ve never heard such an inspiring speech.”

Glasner’s captain backed up stirring words with action. If there was a sense Yates’s star was fading – that his attributes of graft, tenacity and box-to-box drive were losing currency – then it was emphatically extinguished at the City Ground.

Win the first one if you can. But it’s the second ball that often makes the difference.

built99 positions india’s contextual intelligence on the global stage

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

built99 introduces an annual selection of 99 nominated studios. backed by designboom and archdaily, the initiative champions low-impact construction, regional craft, and deep contextual intelligence.

mariana rodrigues November 12, 2026 3 min

Built99 arrives to bridge Indian architecture and design with the global creative sphere, spotlighting practices defined by low-impact construction, native materials, and cultural responsiveness. Backed by designboom and ArchDaily, the initiative introduces an annual list celebrating 99 nominated studios whose work actively challenges conventional building practices. The goal is clear: ensure this regional contextual intelligence takes its rightful place in shaping the future of contemporary architecture and design.

Steering clear of pay-to-play rankings, Built99 acts as an independent cultural institution dedicated to deep architectural substance. Focusing on responsible construction and lasting cultural value, the platform keeps standards rigorous through a strictly nomination-based model, open only to studios with at least a decade of proven built work across India. Out of roughly 250 reviewed candidates, the final 99 practices earned their place through the real-world impact of their built spaces and critical thinking.

Curated by editors and design voices across DAAily platforms, designboom, and ArchDaily, including Martin Zelger, Timo Behrend, Iñaki Laguardia, and Susanna Moreira, the selection relied on five core pillars. The team examined spatial clarity, contextual intelligence, and the ethics of making, with a clear focus on local labor, native materials, and regional craft. Civic footprint and lasting relevance carried equal weight, spotlighting studios that enrich their communities with architecture built to outlast fleeting fads.

The resulting index mirrors the regional variety of Indian architecture, refusing to limit itself to primary metropolises or singular aesthetics. From dense urban hubs like Mumbai, Delhi, and Bengaluru to practices embedded across Kerala, Punjab, Tamil Nadu, Raipur, and Udaipur, the 99 studios chart a truly decentralized landscape. Here, established figures share the stage with hands-on ateliers and collaborative workshops, united by clear spatial thinking, local roots, and genuine civic commitment.

Securing physical permanence in print, the Built99 hardcover book launches this November to document the projects, research, and material methods of all 99 studios. The release pairs global coverage across designboom and ArchDaily with features across leading Indian titles like Forbes India, Fortune India, Robb Report, and GoodHomes. With the full index now live since November 10, leading up to an invitation-only gathering and forum on November 14 at New Delhi’s ITC Maurya, held alongside event partners Amici, Marmo Solutions, and Within.

Viant’s Vanderhook Brothers Want To Make Your Next CTV Ad

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Viant would like a crack at your video creative, please.

Earlier this week, Viant announced its $20 million acquisition of Waymark, a generative AI video ad platform that media companies, including Comcast, Fox, NBCU, Spectrum Reach and Gray Television, have used to generate more than a million ads over the past year for local advertisers.

“Ten years ago, no one would even let you touch the creative,” Viant CEO Tim Vanderhook told AdExchanger. “Our bet is that’s changing.”

Waymark is the latest in Viant’s string of acquisitions over the past two years, including Iris.TV, which supplies content signals, and TVision, which tracks attention.

Viant’s pitch is that it can now offer advertisers a tidy loop. Iris matches ads to the content, TVision shows whether anyone is paying attention and Waymark can crank out fresh versions when the old ones wear out.

If that sounds familiar, it should. Viant is borrowing right from Meta’s playbook.

“Meta takes over all the media optimization and audience targeting, and then they just tell brands to produce as much creative as they possibly can,” said Viant COO Chris Vanderhook. “That’s a big part of their success, and it’s something we’re looking to replicate.”

The Brothers Vanderhook spoke with AdExchanger about the cost of creative fatigue, understanding frequency and who’s really to blame for the open web’s troubles.

TIM VANDERHOOK: We were missing a piece. The mid-market and DTC brands we want are spending tens of millions a month on Meta, and Meta has the field to itself because you need a self-service model to do that. You can’t staff salespeople and account managers for those customers. To do the same for TV, you’ve got to generate the spot for them. We couldn’t do that before Waymark.

CHRIS VANDERHOOK: And creative wears out. When we did diligence on TVision, we’d ask what marketers do when that happens, and the answer was basically, “Well, we already spent that money and we don’t have it in the budget to make new creative.”

Big brands may not be first movers on generative creative, but they’re testing it now, and many of the brands we work with already use AI for editing. We think that’ll only accelerate.

What keeps CTV from just filling up with AI slop, though?

TV: We’re moving past that time when every hand had six fingers.

I think the sensitivity comes from two places. One is the lack of disclosure that something is AI, and the other is putting AI-generated humans in ads. If it’s just the product with no people in it, there’s more leeway and less backlash.

It’s not perfect today. You can still tell an AI voice, for example. But because these tools are improving at a hockey-stick curve, soon it’s going to be hard to discern whether something is AI-generated – which is why proper disclosure will be so important.

It’s great that Waymark can churn out ads at scale, but how many versions of an ad does anyone really need?

TV: It’s different for every brand. Look at Morgan & Morgan, the biggest personal injury ad spender in the country. One of its ads has run for five years and gets 21% human attention. Its newest ad is at 86%.

Once people recognize an ad, their brain almost subconsciously tunes out. To hold their attention, you have to keep coming with another message, another use case, a different angle, a funny one. It’s entertainment value, to a degree.

CV: There’s no such thing as one frequency standard. In TVision’s data, it’s all over the place. Healthcare ads need a frequency of 50 over time and they keep driving engagement, while a Super Bowl ad falls off a cliff after one view. And when CTV serves the same ad five times in a row, the publisher doesn’t get blamed; the advertiser does. So you need a lot of creative.

I remember when you bought lockr last year, some investors were confused because they didn’t know what it was and didn’t seem to get the vision. Do they get it now?

CV: I hope so. I think it was Mark Benioff who said people have to hear something seven times before it sinks in.

Sounds like an argument in favor of overfrequency!

CV: Well, we try to be succinct, but we go through our strategy step by step to make sure people get it, and I think our core investor definitely does. We’re not building products just to get an advertiser’s money. We want advertising that works.

Viant owns the buying platform, identity, content data, measurement and now creative. Do you get questions from investors and marketers about grading your own homework?

TV: Everything we do is auditable. Nothing the walled gardens do is. And I don’t own content, so I have no dog in the fight over whether you give money to Disney or CNN or even YouTube.

Google, Amazon and Meta have reporting to maximize their share of wallet. We’re trying to show who’s actually driving sales.

During your Q3 earnings call this week, you said that the open web is just 10% or less of Viant’s business these days and that it’s not a growth driver. Be honest, you can tell me, do you guys still care about the open web?

TV: Display still serves a purpose, just a much smaller one. A banner gets about 2% ad recall, a TV ad gets 32% and a TV followed by a display gets 38%.

Web traffic is shrinking because Google controls it. If they decide they’re not going to send you web traffic anymore, then you don’t exist. It’s a monopoly eating all the traffic, because they have to compete with ChatGPT or their stock goes down.

That’s why I’ve said Facebook didn’t kill Myspace. Google did.

Over the summer, you came out and said you want to relaunch Myspace, which you’ve owned since 2011. When will Myspace ride again?

TV: Long live Myspace! We love Myspace, which is why we never sold it, although I get at least three to five offers a day from people who want to buy it. There’s a thirst for nonalgorithmically controlled social media.

But a product needs repeat usage above 30% for it to work. When we first relaunched Myspace, the repeat usage rate was 13%, and we knew it was in trouble. It was very expensive.

The good news is that the cost profile to deliver a product has drastically plummeted since we ran Myspace the first time after we bought it. And now that the cost barrier has come down, we’ll see. Never say never.

Are you still in touch with “Myspace Tom”?

TV: Tom’s living a great life between Southern California and Hawaii. Of all the founders, he’s actually the least interested in a comeback.

What’s funny is that so many people think I’m Myspace Tom, because my name is close. They get confused and call me “MySpace Tim,” and I have to be, like, well, no … not exactly!

This interview has been lightly edited and condensed.

For more articles featuring Tim Vanderhook, click here.

For the first time, a new feature in Google Ads breaks down the dollar amount a campaign saved from IVT blocking and exactly how much Google is crediting the campaign for bots caught post-billing.

Schibsted is working with Nodals AI, a company that creates custom ad optimization algorithms for publishers, to court brands who’ve been wooed by Meta’s own optimization tools.

AI is taking over the media and tech industries in the name of simplification, and marketers are pining for provable campaign outcomes, including from TV. These shifts are also causing significant change in Agency Holdco Land, according to Angela Steele, the first chief client officer for WPP Media US.

No major retail media player has taken as circumspect an approach to ad revenue as Shopify. But the company has recently taken its next important step with a product called Campaign Autopilot.

OpenAI’s head of monetization, Benji Shomair makes the case for ads as a way to bring ChatGPT to more people, with user trust taking priority over advertiser demands.

In Q3, The Trade Desk beat the admittedly pessimistic revenue forecast it set for itself earlier this year. But growth has stalled.

Tommy Lee Jones Couldn’t Care Less About His Legacy

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The screen legend has more interesting things going on—including a stellar turn on The Lowdown season 2, as an estranged, crusty old father.

Tommy Lee Jones, 80, an Oscar winner and a star of the second season of The Lowdown, the noirish Tulsa crime story undercut with terrific dime-store waggery, sits flat-footed in the green room at Good Morning America, in downtown Manhattan. He has just done an on-camera interview with Ethan Hawke, who stars as the show’s protagonist. In the next dressing room are many adorable, pawing puppies being primped for a segment on National Adopt-a-Dog Month. Jones is adorable too, in his avuncular way, his face an old-dog scowl that softens when you ask the right question, as if you’d scratched him in the right spot.

On The Lowdown, he plays Hawke’s estranged father, a crusty old bastard who holds secrets. There’s a bad history between them, and Hawke’s character, an impulsive, truth-seeking investigative journalist, at first wants nothing to do with his dad, but that situation evolves. They’re both named Lee Raybon.

Jones wears a glen-plaid sport coat, a blue dress shirt, and comfortable sneakers. When the interview begins, he looks at you, ready but giving nothing until he’s asked to.

ESQUIRE: I just watched the first six episodes of this season, and man, I wasn’t ready for [REDACTED].

TOMMY LEE JONES: Oh, you watched all those episodes? The [REDACTED] scene was—I hope that was kind of shocking.

We didn’t see it coming, which I guess is a testament to you all.

That’s the whole point. You didn’t see it coming. That’s the reason we did it.

The way you developed your character’s way of speaking, the way he trails off here and there, tells us that this guy knows some stuff he’s not giving away. He’s shifty. It’s just brilliant. I’m just trying to give a compliment, not really asking a question.

You said to Charlie Rose in an interview a long time ago that there’s no through line that runs through the characters you play. At the same time, you don’t take just any role that comes along. Do you ever think about legacy? How are they going to remember Tommy Lee Jones? Or is it really just “This role would be a challenge to play, so I’m going to take it?”

I don’t have long-term thinking, and I don’t even think in a term like legacy. I think more like a dog. I’m absorbed with what’s in front of me. I might run off and chase a squirrel.

I believe you. It’s just that I’ve started thinking recently, What am I writing about? Is there a body of work here? I hadn’t thought that way before. With each story it was just This is a cool story. I’ll do this story.

That’s what I thought. I thought this show was really cool. The people that were working on it were cool, and Tulsa is an extremely cool place. Ethan made a good point on TV just now when he said good noir stories always have a sense of place, so the location was very important. It’s true about film noir, and this show really qualifies as part of that genre. A keen sense of place, leading to inarguable originality.

How far are Tulsa and eastern Oklahoma from where you live in Texas?

Oh, I don’t know. It’s northeast of Dallas. So I think the drive up—

[He turns to his wife of of 25 years, Dawn, who sits in the corner looking at her phone.]

How long would it take to drive to Tulsa from San Antonio?

When I was in prep school, we went up to Oklahoma to play a school in football, and they always cheated. Also when I was in high school, they would sell 3.2 beer to minors, and it was fun, a big adventure every once in a while, to pile into a car and drive to Oklahoma to buy a beer.

Ethan’s hilarious on The Lowdown, but you’re also very funny! Always have been. The warden in Natural Born Killers, Dwight McClusky, is your funniest role, I feel. Samuel Gerard in The Fugitive. And then I’m watching this show and was reminded how funny you can be. How would you describe your sense of humor? What’s funny to you?

People. People are hilarious. The best humor is found in human behavior. Any student of human behavior is going to be highly amused, when they’re not horrified.

Did you watch stuff as a kid that made you laugh?

The Three Stooges. Slapstick. Warner Brothers cartoons. That’s fundamental humor. I mean, if I see a fat lady slip on a banana peel, I swear I’m going to laugh.

You share some beautiful scenes with the young actress who plays your granddaughter, Ryan Kiera Armstrong. I couldn’t think of many films in which we see you spend a lot of time with children.

There was a movie called River Rats, and I was on a boat for a good part of this movie with two children, one of whom was Martha Plimpton, as a little slip of a girl, playing my daughter. We became good friends on that, and I was happy to see her on The Lowdown.

You and Ethan, and Martha now too, are seasoned veterans. Is the approach different when you’re working with young Ryan, who doesn’t have 50 films under her belt? Is there a greater need with a child actor to get to know each other before filming?

Oh, actors are actors, so it’s not difficult for me to relate to an actor regardless of their age. And she’s a pro. Actors, they have a way of getting to know one another very quickly.

Yeah, she is. And she’s a fearless and admirable young lady.

[Shit.] Not from you. I don’t mean in your scenes. I mean her other scenes.

Getting back to the idea of a through line: If I may point out one commonality across your performances, it’s that your characters have wisdom. You yourself are obviously well-read. You went to Harvard, and, for example, you’ve compared the satire of Natural Born Killers to Molière. Is reading still a big part of your life?

I’ve been making a study of Texas history, which begins with—well, there is no beginning. It’s always been there. But its experience as an American territory, for me, begins with James K. Polk. I read a political biography of Polk. That’s a good place to start a study of modern Texas history.

Yeah, well, he was Andrew Jackson’s bitch.

Getting back to Lee Raybon Sr., whose nickname is Frog, and his son. Fathers and sons are always rich territory.

Ethan’s character changes his attitude toward you. It gets better. Did you see that as Frog’s project, what this character is after—his son’s love?

[Mildly annoyed.] Well, I compared myself to a dog earlier because I really deal with what’s in front of me.

And all the qualities that you see in that character are ones that Sterling Harjo, the show’s creator, wrote. We learn that this character is dying. That slowly begins to dawn on us, and we see that he’s trying to somehow reconcile life with his son and get to know his granddaughter. But that quest in this character is not advertised. It’s discovered as we watch things unfold in an often amusing way, and always original. That was appealing. I mean, I think that’s the way it works.

Can I, at the risk of pissing you off: I know you say that you’re like a dog. Are you telling me that—

I don’t want to belabor that too much. I’m a little bit like a dog. [Laughs, kind of.]

But this is what I’m getting at. I’ve seen every film you’ve ever made, and you don’t give a lot of interviews, but in the ones I’ve seen over the years you’ve made it clear that each character is its own separate project. No connections. But at the same time, I know how smart you are, and so part of me thinks, Come on. Of course you must see some continuum along the characters you play. We see similarities among characters in literature, even across different authors in different centuries. The through line of all your parts is that you chose them. So pushing back a tiny bit, I’m suggesting that your performance as Lee Raybon has inherited something from these previous men that you’ve inhabited in the last 50 years. Is that fair at all?

[Breathing a sigh of relief, and perhaps satisfaction.] Okay.

[Pauses, hoping this line of questioning was over.] Oh. Well, I don’t know. I’m not aware of any continuum. [Any sense of Esquire’s relief or satisfaction disappears.] I don’t think it’s in my best interest, and I’m not inclined to think about what all the characters I play have in common. The only thing they have in common is that I’m playing them. And they’re all built out of experience. Let me refer you to an essay by T.S. Eliot called “Tradition and the Individual Talent.” It’s an indication of what happens to actors, really. Their performances are made out of the shards and the pieces and the bits of your experience of life and your thinking about it. One of the reasons you read that essay is so you have a better understanding of The Waste Land or even James Joyce. Modern art is comprised of the shards of experience, reassembled in a way that has a dramatic form, with a beginning, a middle, an end, and a purpose.

Do you see a performance such as this one as a piece of art?

No. Not really. I don’t know what it—you know, this is the thing I never give any thought to: What is art?

Compare your view of God when you were six or seven years old to now.

[Coming to life a bit.] Oh! Well? I never imagined a man in a white robe with a long white beard sitting on a throne mounted on a cloud with some lightning bolts in his hand. That’s one image. It’s probably almost as good as any other, but the question is, you either believe in such a thing as God or you don’t. I do.

Has your view or understanding changed over your life?

Not really. My grandmother and my aunts and my mother always took me to a Southern Baptist church. I was baptized in the Southern Baptist church. And, like all organized religions, there’s a good deal of nonsense in it. But it also leads to an awareness of the deity, which by definition exceeds our capability to define it.

[A long pause as Jones stares at the floor, then looks up, eyebrows raised and smiling.]

That was pretty good. I’m gonna write that down.

If you had to cook a meal for dinner, what’s the best thing you could come up with?

[For the first time during the interview, Jones’s body relaxes—arm over the back of the chair.]

I’d probably buy some pork ribs, an entire side of a little young pig, and slowly barbecue that over a mesquite fire and serve it with a mixture of sautéed yellow squash and onions and probably a sweet potato. You can scratch them with a fork and rub them down with butter, wrap them up in tinfoil and cook them alongside the pig right over the fire. You can feed a lot of people that way, and it tastes good. And it’s fun to eat.

There’s a product you can buy in San Saba, Texas, called Bill’s Season All. Bill’s Season All is something you sprinkle on pork ribs and it makes them taste real good. I don’t know what all’s in it, but it’s delectable.

Texas chili: Do you have strong opinions?

[Jones snaps to even more, speaking a little louder and with some of the gusto and urgency of U.S. Marshal Samuel Gerard saying, “Listen up, ladies and gentlemen. Our fugitive has been on the run for 90 minutes. Average foot speed over uneven ground barring injuries is four miles an hour, and that gives us a radius of six miles. What I want out of each and every one of you is a hard-target search of every gas station, residence, warehouse, farmhouse, henhouse, outhouse, and doghouse in that area. Checkpoints go up at 15 miles. Your fugitive’s name is Dr. Richard Kimble. Go get him.”]

Don’t use ground beef. You need to cube it. Cube that beef into pieces about the size of the dice that you play Monopoly with. And after that, your own creativity comes in with the spices. But it’s important to avoid ground beef. And you don’t put beans in chili. That’s on the side. That’s at the discretion of the diner. Some people just like chili, but they just don’t necessarily want to see a bunch of beans floating in it. You’ll find one recipe in the Hill Country of Texas and another one in the hills above Santa Fe. Quite different.

Last night, I was watching either the fifth or sixth episode of The Lowdown, and Martha Plimpton’s character talks about putting a couple spoonfuls of Tang in chili.

I can’t imagine putting Tang in chili. But chili is like gumbo. Or Kentucky burgoo. Or Irish stew. It’s a food that’s in and of the people, so the way of cooking it and the ingredients vary from place to place and from circumstance to circumstance. Real gumbo has always got—well, it can have anything in it. Duck … goose … whatever crawled across the porch last night. Chili’s the same kind of thing. It’s the food of the people, and as such, the main ingredients are whatever you’ve got.

I don’t think so. I’m closer to profligate than frugal. We spend a lot of money on horses. My wife is one of the better female polo players in the United States. I played polo for 40-some-odd years. I’m a bit too old for it now. “Old and worn-out” is what they would call it.

Where did you live when you lived here in New York?

Yeah, it was. When I first came here in 1969, the streets were covered in dog shit. And it really got to be bad. Land mines. They passed some ordinances in the city that people had to clean up after their dogs. You’d have to be very careful walking down the street. Nothing more embarrassing than going into a meeting with dog shit on your shoes.

So now that you’ve turned in this astonishingly beautiful performance as Lee Raybon—

—and are there hills left to conquer as an actor? Are there types of roles you have yet to play?

I don’t know. There’s a schedule for the day. And I looked at it, but I don’t really—I’d have to ask her. That’s the reason I don’t have to know. I have her.

Okay, well, I appreciate this. I hope it wasn’t too painful and I didn’t—

I know talking about acting is not the most interesting thing.

Well, it’s breakfast time. When it’s first thing in the morning at home, if your cook’s not there and you’re hungry, what could you whip up?

I have a French press for coffee, a toaster for a bagel. And we’ve always got cream cheese and smoked salmon. Bagel, lox, cream cheese, coffee, and a grapefruit. That’s what I ordinarily eat for breakfast.

You’re right, this is more interesting than talking about acting.

Oh, breakfast. Breakfast is more interesting than anything.

What makes this little wearable worth a look? For starters, it tracks sleep, activity, heart rate, and menstrual cycle data, then delivers insights through the Oura app. You can check your sleep score, explore trends in stress and recovery, and track how they change over time, all without wearing a screen on your wrist.

Exclusive: Details on the Oura Ring 5 Black Friday Sale

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

THE OURA RING has been my go-to daily fitness tracker for almost a decade, and for the first time ever, Oura is offering a discount on its latest model. Men’s Health can confirm this sale exclusively, and you won’t want to miss it. We’re sharing it early because we recommend you snag an Oura Ring 5 sizing kit for the best fit.

The sizing kit cost applies to the ring at checkout, so it’s technically free.

If you’ve been interested in getting the Oura Ring 5, now’s your time. We’re talking a serious deal. The Oura Ring 5 was just released this summer, which is why we haven’t seen any offers like this yet. And after this event ends, it’s unlikely it will go on sale again soon. So if you are looking for its cutting-edge smart ring tech at a discount, this is your best opportunity.

Also, heads up: if you have an FSA or HSA plan, Oura Ring 5 is covered.

Here is everything you need to know about the Oura Ring Black Friday and Cyber Monday sale. I’ll update this story as soon as the sale goes live, so stay tuned here.

The Oura Ring 5 is Oura's latest offering, and a few upgrades earned it a spot on our Best Smart Rings list. This version has no noticeable sensors, while previous models had three interior nodes that could be a bit uncomfortable. It still collects health data seamlessly.

The Oura Ring 5 is lighter and slimmer than any previous models. The ring provides real-time stress tracking, continuous heart-rate monitoring, and more precise temperature sensing. All of this is designed to give you more precise, helpful feedback throughout the day. The ring’s interior has also changed from an epoxy to all-over titanium for a lighter feel.

The charging case is another great upgrade available to Oura Ring 5 users. This case lets you store a charge so you can power up your ring without being limited by nearby outlets. This means you can keep an extra charge for your ring while you're traveling or on the move so you don't miss out on any insights. The new charging dock is also improved and can charge your ring in about an hour.

Oura recommends wearing the ring on your forefinger, since that's where your pulse is strongest, which can mean slightly better data, but any finger works. I know a few people who use their Oura Ring as their wedding band.

If you're unsure about your size, it could be a good idea to order one of Oura's sizing kits. It’s now $10 on Amazon, and that gets applied to your future ring purchase, so technically it's "free." And it allows you to get an early jump on the sale.

When you purchase on ouraring.com the process of getting a sizing kit is a little more involved. You order the ring first, and then they send you a sizing kit to use to get your correct size in the system. Depending on which route you choose for the sizing kit, you'll use that same route to purchase your ring when you decide to buy.

You might not be looking for a super-slim smart ring. If you’re used to wearing rings with some weight, or you just like something with a little more heft, the Oura Ring 4 is the first version I think is worth keeping, even if you’re buying a new Oura Ring 5.

Smart, powerful, thoughtful women are at the center of today’s issue. It’s a rare treat to peek into the machinations of two great minds — not least to see them deepen each other’s thinking, as happens here. “I definitely tend to think about beauty as a kind of covering up, you know, like hiding blemishes or imperfections,” Chang says at one point. “But I like this idea about the relationship, the closeness between beauty and violence. I’d like to think about it more …” Read, please. It’s like watching two blades hone each other to an even finer edge.

When I first saw Patty Chang’s short film “Melons (At a Loss)” (1988), I was stunned by its cheeky and chilling meditation on the female Asian-American body and how it carries the weight of historic inheritance, both within the family and the nation. The stationary camera shows Chang herself, waist up, dressed in nothing but a 1940s-ish cone-shaped “bullet bra.” (Already, femininity flirts with aggression.) Chang places on the top of her head a ceramic plate that she tells us she inherited from a dead aunt. As she balances the plate, she verbally narrates the history behind this commemorative (that is, unusable) porcelain plate, with its poisonous dye. With this object, Chang references not only a familial and feminized legacy, but also a much larger and longer heritage of porcelain as a historically transglobal commodity of Euro-American desire, and how that material desire has shaped Western conceptualization of Asiatic persons and things from the 18th century to this day. | Anne Cheng Read More

Andy Baraghani's Ditto Cafe Is His Most Personal Project Yet

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Andy Baraghani is more afraid of becoming stagnant than he is of dying. Stagnancy, he’s quick to clarify, is different from stillness. Stagnancy is like a fetid pond; stillness is meditative. Baraghani seems to be at risk of neither.

It is late morning on the first actually chilly day of autumn and we are staring one another down across a stainless-steel prep table, which has been dragooned into use as an island in his outrageously good-looking kitchen. He is about to make me scones—salty-sweet ones with dates and lemon zest, an offering under development for his forthcoming café Ditto. But first, we’ll need to pop out for cream.

Quite literally in motion since I arrived, Baraghani finishes his point on stagnancy: He fears a life in which he is not learning, moving, or evolving “as a writer, a cook, and a good gay,” he says.

He lives just a few blocks from the Union Square Greenmarket, where niche pepper varieties are currently splayed in colorful fractals across folding tables. We have just completed a ground floor tour of the apartment that the chef–cum writer–cum chef shares with his husband Keith Pollock. Now, he moves across the kitchen and pauses at the Crittall-style window. It gives me 30 seconds to take inventory of his countertop alliums.

These details feel important. Baraghani has become a sort of standard-bearer for exquisite seasonal produce, and the exquisite life one can lead preparing such stuff. I clock a couple of shallots, which sit in a Swid Powell bowl beside five heirloom tomatoes. It occurs to me that even onions are more beautiful in Baraghani’s universe.

“Oh,” he says casually, following my gaze. “Torpedo shallots.” As if all of our countertops look like this.

In the decade or so since he became a household name for a certain set of a certain era (food-obsessed aesthetes who love an acidic drizzle and a hard, salty cheese), as a video star for a certain magazine—okay, this one—his fandom has only continued to explode.

His debut cookbook, The Cook You Want to Be, was a New York Times bestseller and the recipient of a James Beard Award. He has more than 370,000 followers across Instagram and TikTok. He’s been called “the Internet boyfriend of our dreams.” Vogue covered his wedding. The New York Times published his ideal Thanksgiving menu. In the last 24 hours alone, Baraghani has been busy beating out creative director Terrence O’Connor for a Best Body prize at the podcast How Long Gone’s inaugural “Goneies” Award Show, (Baraghani was asked to remove his blazer to reveal a form-fitting Dario Vitale-era Versace pink tank top) before running into David Tanis while shopping for groceries.

When Baraghani announced he would be opening a restaurant this fall near Union Square with restaurateur Michael Silber, publications began tripping over themselves to cover the news. Still, he says, few have pushed him on the true meaning of the name Ditto. Perhaps they were too busy trying to keep up.

Baraghani takes a seat on one of the iron stools beside the prep table and there is a rare, brief silence, which is broken almost immediately by the tinny opening notes of a techno song emanating from the crew performing a renovation on the lower floor. I inquire about his cortisol levels as he juggles opening Ditto a few blocks away, the second cookbook he has just shot in this apartment, and now the remodel below us.

Baraghani grins. “I feel calm,” he insists. “If something goes wrong, I can fix it, because I feel pretty clear on what I want to achieve.”

He has spent the last 10 years peppering his recipes with caveats. “I know that my food may be a touch too lemony for some,” he writes in The Cook You Want To Be. “And that I use what has been called a ridiculous amount of herbs.”

His background too. Years ago, he penned an essay for this magazine about growing up Iranian in a post-9/11 America; he started to go by Andy instead of Andisheh and bought an “ITALIAN STALLION” tee shirt. He wrote about learning to accept his sexuality in restaurant kitchens, where he rarely encountered other gay men, and in his family life. He grew up in the Bay Area, the son of parents who had emigrated from Iran to Berkeley in 1977. The piece ended with an admission: “So as nice as it would be for this story to end with that Iran feature or getting my dream job at Bon Appétit, the truth is that I’m still trying to figure things out. It’s not always steady progress.”

Since I had a baby six weeks ago, I have been walking around floored that the adults in my life could have ever been children, but with Andy, I don’t have to work too hard to picture it. He has a particularly cheerful sense of wonder. When I ask about it, he says it comes from his upbringing. His parents were political activists. Andy’s mother worked as an aesthetician (yes, he established his famous skincare routine early). He grew up surrounded by a diverse community of immigrants who would invite his family to their celebrations and into their homes, where a young Baraghani got to taste dishes from far-flung places. After he landed in New York, he would take respite in stints abroad, traveling from hostel to hostel so he could eat his way across Vietnam, Lebanon, and Turkey.

Now, though, Baraghani says he is done footnoting his vision. In the intervening years since that essay, Baraghani has written and cooked and tasted at warp speed. He was bound to self-actualize in the same timeframe it took the rest of us to figure out how to style barrel-legged pants. Or perhaps it’s the reality of having to reckon with what he can’t change: In 2024, after a battle with small-cell lung cancer, his beloved father passed away. Baraghani tells me that before it was stagnancy, his greatest fear was losing a parent. He has since learned that loss isn’t something to solve, but a tremendous void one must learn to live with.

“There’s an explicit confidence you’ll see [at Ditto,]” he says. The dishes will weave together all of the threads of Baraghani-ism: seasonal vegetables prepared deftly with Persian flourishes, blending tart and sour and aromatic with a firm point-of-view. If the specifics seem hard to picture, I think that might be the point. Baraghani has thrived historically by taking a straightforward-seeming dish and twisting it just off-kilter, so it is a few degrees punchier and much more beautiful than you were expecting. The result is food that is as much about the people and places evoked by the flavors as it is about aesthetic provocation. (That Italian Stallion shirt, he tells me, is long gone.)

Literally speaking, Ditto will be a revival of the sort of counter-service restaurant Baraghani has long adored, from Gjusta in Los Angeles to City Bakery in New York. He and Silber sprang for a shiny rotisserie oven, for what they’re calling a “beyond chicken program,” with plans for spit-roasted porchetta, romanesco, and cabbage.

The goal is dishes that are palpably human, in a space Baraghani hopes will seem accessible to everyone. It was important for both owners to find a space in a hub neighborhood. With the storefront they chose, a former Agnès B. on 16th Street, Greenmarket shoppers can stop by for a selection of California-ish sides and salads displayed Ottolenghi-style, counter pastries, or just to peruse marketplace provisions: tahini, harissa, dates (and for now,no tinned fish). Maybe, one day, they’ll stay open for dinner service, but for now the plan is breakfast and lunch.

Suddenly Baraghani is moving again, reaching for his phone to show me a photo of a dish he would have loved to serve, had Con Ed turned on the gas in time for a September opening: a mound of spicy tuna salad piled perkily in a blanched tomato, with a turret of scallion greens.

The sight makes me hungry and I realize we still haven’t gone out for that cream. Ninety minutes have passed and I have yet to ask any of the questions I’d prepared for Baraghani, who keeps surprising me with disclosures. (Such as: he did not speak until he was three years old.)

Suddenly, it is time to head to Ditto for a tour, cream and scones be damned.

As we walk over, I ask about the restaurant’s name. He asks if I’ve seen the movie Ghost. I have, but not for a while. “It’s another way to say ‘I love you,’” he says simply, before disappearing to join a meeting.

Later, I dig up that scene Baraghani mentioned and press play. In it, Whoopi Goldberg tries to convince a frustrated Demi Moore that her recently deceased boyfriend has returned as a ghost. Moore jumps up in disbelief to flee the restaurant as Patrick Swayze, the boyfriend, scrambles to find a way to prove to her that he’s right there. “Tell her ‘ditto,’” he urges Goldberg, who screams “ditto” after Moore as she bolts. “Ditto!” It’s their code word for, I love you too.

Moore stops in her tracks and turns, her gaze connecting with a man she misses, who she isn’t totally sure is there.

And for the first time in a while, she is no longer in motion. She is still.

RooMoo transforms abandoned mountain cabins into hotel in China

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Concrete drill cores, discarded bricks and timber salvaged from abandoned mountain cabins have been repurposed for a 24-room retreat by Chinese studio RooMoo on Jinfo Mountain in Chongqing, China.

RooMoo transformed a five-level former sales office into the Cloudhaus Hotel, retaining the building’s original structure while incorporating materials collected from the surrounding landscape and nearby villages.

Located at an altitude of 1,200 metres on the mountain’s northern slope, the building has a white-rendered facade punctuated by eight projecting white frames that extend vertically across its elevations.

Bamboo panels and stone slabs fill sections between the frames, while timber detailing and sloping tiled roofs reference traditional buildings in the region.

Rather than using new materials, RooMoo visited local abandoned wooden cabins and furniture workshops to identify resources that could be reused. The studio ended up using timber from the cabins for the project.

“Instead of relying solely on research, we planned most of our conceptual design time to explore the mountains for locally available materials and resources to respect the local environment,” the studio said.

Among the most unusual interventions are walls made from concrete cores extracted during structural renovations, which have been stacked vertically and separated by dark metal supports.

Demolished red bricks were reused in the restaurant, while discarded bamboo scaffolding treads were cut into strips and transformed into ceiling panels and outdoor paving.

The restaurant occupies an expanded podium at the building’s lower level, with indoor dining areas leading towards outdoor terraces.

Its interiors combine stone walls, timber furniture and woven seating, while reclaimed bricks and bamboo offcuts feature in the finishes.

Visitors enter the hotel via the reception on the building’s uppermost floor, which is accessed from an entrance courtyard and connecting bridge.

A timber reception desk sits in front of a wall covered in bamboo panels, woven surfaces and discarded bamboo-shoot shells, which had been harvested locally in the mountains.

Above the lobby, an exposed pitched roof was lined with bamboo and supported by substantial timber beams.

A large woven bamboo installation hangs over the lounge, where black sofas and tan leather chairs were arranged beside full-height glazing overlooking the forested mountains.

Guestrooms were arranged in a fan-shaped layout facing the mountain landscape.

The hotel offers several room types, including courtyard rooms, suites and standard king rooms, with interiors featuring earthy colours, salvaged timber and locally made furniture.

Founded in Shanghai in 2017 by Tao Zhang, Ray Zhang and Marine Bois, RooMoo works across hospitality, restaurant, commercial, office and residential projects.

Other recent interior projects in China include a Linehouse-designed hotel with an industrial feel and a Shanghai tea shop that draws on Rockbund’s historic buildings.

Israel’s Arrest of Journalist Abed Abu Shehadeh Raises Concerns

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Abed Abu Shehadeh, a Palestinian citizen of Israel, was detained without being charged with a crime, raising concerns that the domestic security service is overstepping its authority.

Abed Abu Shehadeh, a 38-year-old journalist and a Palestinian citizen of Israel, said he was putting on his pajamas on the evening of Nov. 3 when police officers and intelligence agents burst into his family’s third-floor apartment in Jaffa.

They strip-searched him, confiscated his phone, allowed him to dress and then whisked him away, he said in an interview at a restaurant in the city.

Five days later, after interrogations by Israel’s domestic security agency, the Shin Bet, Mr. Abu Shehadeh, who lives with his wife and two young children, was let go.

A former elected member of the Tel Aviv-Jaffa City Council, Mr. Abu Shehadeh has not been charged with a crime. The Israeli authorities have not publicly offered a reason for his arrest, though court records reviewed by The New York Times show that he was accused of being in contact with an unnamed “foreign agent.”

His highly publicized arrest — a week after Israel’s national elections — came as Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his allies have claimed that foreign actors illegally funneled large amounts of money to bribe Arab citizens to vote for Arab political parties.

Critics say that Mr. Netanyahu and his allies are seeking to undermine the legitimacy of the record gains by Arab parties in the Parliament, or the Knesset, by contending without basis that Arab voters stole the election.
