Jamie Carragher & Gary Neville discuss one thing they 'don't like' in the EFL

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

EFL supporters will tend to disagree on most topics, but amid the incessant discourse and fierce daily debates, there is one thing that the majority of fans sing from the same hymn sheet with.

Club and player performance, divisional standards, club size, fanbase volume and stature and hit-or-miss signings are all subjects which invariably promise to polarise and split opinion, yet that's not the case for every single EFL-related matter.

As such, one notable topic may often spark discussions among supporters of League One and League Two clubs but they rarely disagree on it, and their school of thought also has backing from two of the nation's most prominent pundits in Jamie Carragher and Gary Neville.

That subject is, of course, the EFL trophy, which currently goes under the name of the 'Jersey Mike's Trophy' and is contested by clubs in League One and League Two alongside 16 Category One Premier League under-21 sides.

The formatting of the competition includes one academy team for each group, of which there are a total of 16 on a regionalised basis with eight northern and eight southern, and the top two progress to the knockout stages. All fixtures are regionalised until the semi-final stages, with the final two sides ultimately battling it out for silverware at Wembley.

For a number of reasons, however, the EFL trophy and the requirement of each EFL club outside the Championship to compete in the tournament has long represented a point of often-frustrated discussion, and exactly how Carragher and Neville feel about it has become clearer.

Speaking on the latest episode of The Overlap's Stick to Football podcast, the high-profile punditry pair began the show by discussing the participation of Carragher's son James in the competition for Wigan Athletic's 2-1 Group B victory at Crewe Alexandra on Tuesday evening.

Carragher revealed that he switched the match off at half-time, before Neville disclosed that he doesn't like the competition through his boardroom role with League Two leaders Salford City - who lifted the trophy over Portsmouth behind-closed-doors at Wembley back in 2021.

The ex-Liverpool defender explained: "He (James) played last night. It was on at 7 o'clock, you can get it on Sky and he told me he was playing 45 minutes.

"I just switched it off at half time, so I missed the second half. They won 2-1, I think they had already qualified anyway.

Neville then added: "I don't like that trophy. It's a weird one."

Carragher responded that "it's probably a bit like the Carabao Cup for Premier League teams, but when you get to see something at Wembley," to which Neville recounted: "We (Salford) won it in COVID in League Two, we beat Portsmouth at Wembley.

"We were playing in the final against Portsmouth, it was going to be in March and, when lockdown came in February, they'd sold 32,000 tickets for the final and we were going to make quite a lot of money out of it because we'd sold five or six (thousand).

"It was looking at about 40,000, which is a decent crowd at Wembley and the money splits halfway. We ended up playing it the year after infront of no people, the directors and owners were in there but there was literally nobody in the ground and we had to sit two seats apart from each other.

Football League World assesses and ranks the FIFTEEN best away fanbases in the EFL right now

"We absolutely battered them, it was mad."

The EFL Trophy presents a variety of drawbacks for contestants as EFL outfits face much more jeopardy and risk than the competing under-21 sides, which is a key reason as to why many have slammed the tournament over the years.

Understandably, it is widely felt that the now-11-year-old decision to include Category One academy contingents undermines and devalues the competition's integrity and has created a tournament which is essentially designed to accommodate Premier League clubs, despite the fact that no under-21 side has ever lifted the trophy at Wembley.

Indeed, many age-group contestants will actually take prestigious youth tournaments such as the UEFA Youth League and Premier League International Cup more seriously, which only reduces the competitiveness of the EFL Trophy.

Therefore, the matchday experience for EFL Trophy encounters pales in comparison to any league fixture, as matches are often marked by extremely low attendances and non-existent atmospheres in near-empty stadiums in an accurate display of precisely how most EFL clubs and fanbases alike value the competition.

Another prominent issue is how it provides additional fixture congestion in already-hectic schedules, which proves particularly challenging for sides with weaker or lighter squads.

Naturally, it's frequently to the frustration of promotion-chasing or relegation-battling League One and League Two sides when they have to hop out of league action to compete for the trophy when much greater outcomes and jeopardy are on the line.

Be honest, how do you feel about the EFL Trophy? Do you think that all League One and League Two clubs should have to compete? What would you change about it? Let us know your thoughts in the comments below!

EFL rules mandate the fielding of a minimum number of experienced players, and some clubs can feel the benefits of promoting young talent into senior football through the competition, but the injury risks and fixture congestion often outweigh the advantages and you would be hard-pressed to find too many third and fourth-tier outfits who genuinely value their own participation.

The disadvantages of the EFL Trophy certainly outstrip the benefits for the overwhelming majority of EFL participants, though it's not all universally negative.

Quite obviously, the major upside is the prospect of lifting a trophy at Wembley. We're hardly talking about prestigious silverware here, of course, but either way, that opportunity is one that is nigh-on impossible to experience in any other fashion for almost any non-Championship EFL side unless they win the play-offs, though that's not a classic-style tournament.

Just how much of a benefit that really is remains open to debate, as with only one team capable of winning it per season by definition, everybody else misses out and fails to obtain such an exclusive advantage.

Few would say that trying to get to Wembley in a second-rate competition is of superior importance than trying to battle for promotion or against relegation, too, yet it's a rare upside that can provide supporters with something of a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity.

As aforementioned, meanwhile, the ability to integrate young academy-honed prospects who may not yet be reared and readied for consistent league action into a senior footballing competition, albeit one with debatable pedigree and competitiveness, is still not to be sniffed at.

It can have positive long-term effects, even if the short-term risks mean most clubs would perhaps wish to simply not participate in the first place.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Renovation delays pose "Notre Dame-like" fire risk for Houses of Parliament

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Image by Pandora Pictures courtesy of Shutterstock

A government report has found that risks of a major fire at the Palace of Westminster are rising as health and safety incidents increase at the UK parliament building in London, amid continuing delays to its renovation.

Released last week by the Public Accounts Committee (PAC), the report said that maintenance costs for the UNESCO-listed structure are expected to rise from £1.5 million to £2 million a week if no action is taken. According to the latest report on the restoration, the overall cost of the renovation will rise by £420 million for each year the project is delayed.

Parliament voted in 2018 to vacate the Palace for restoration, however this has been delayed by ongoing disagreements about how fully the House of Commons and House of Lords should leave during the anticipated decades-long renovation.

“Political allergy” to updating the building

According to Public Accounts Committee chair Geoffrey Clifton-Brown, current fire risks could result in “Notre Dame-like flames” at the parliament building, which in 2023 was reported by the committee to be at “constant risk of fire”.

“There has long been a political allergy to gripping the nettle that is R&R [repair and renovation],” Clifton-Brown said.

“Consider the impact of global rolling news footage of Notre Dame-like flames licking out of the building’s roof, resulting in the destruction of our heritage, or worse, injuries or loss of life,” he continued.

“These are not notional possibilities, but real and rising risks which our committee identified three years ago.”

Renovation strategy to be decided by 2030

The report outlines four strategies for the renovation of the building laid out by the R&R Client Board, with one needing to be selected by parliament by 2030 for the programme to progress.

Two of these strategies have been recommended by the board in a bid to speed up the decision-making process. These are fully leaving the building, which is estimated to cost between £11.1 billion to £15.6 billion and take 19 to 24 years, and the partially vacating the building, which is estimated at £19.5 billion to £39.2 billion and could take up to 61 years.

Approving either of these recommendations would require parliamentary approval.

“All options ahead involve extremely high figures with very big ranges,” Clifton-Brown said.

“At a time of national fiscal constraint, we would expect the parliamentary authorities to examine these figures extremely carefully to see how they can be reduced before the vote.”

The PAC’s report also found that there are areas that still need costing, including aspects like decommissioning, relocation of the building’s services, and the restoration of the medieval cloister court.

The cross-party committee has urged the clerks of both House of Commons and House of Lords to set out remaining cost estimates and provide a detailed schedule for the works.

“It is crucial that we preserve the palace as a symbol of our democracy,” the report said. “A failure to act would have serious consequences for taxpayers and future generations.”

The renovation of the adjacent Elizabeth Tower was recently completed by architecture studio Purcell, and last year was awarded a RIBA London award.

Image by Pandora Pictures courtesy of Shutterstock

Libre de tout contrat depuis son départ de Manchester City en mai 2026, Pep Guardiola pourrait rapidement retrouver un banc prestigieux. La Fédération américaine de football (U.S. Soccer) a placé le technicien catalan en priorité absolue pour prendre les rênes de la sélection nationale, alors que Mauricio Pochettino se rapproche de Manchester United.

Un changement majeur se prépare outre-Atlantique. Le poste de sélectionneur des États-Unis va probablement se libérer en raison du départ imminent de Mauricio Pochettino vers Manchester United pour remplacer Michael Carrick. Selon les révélations de The Athletic, un accord verbal a été conclu ce mercredi 11 novembre 2026 entre le club mancunien et le technicien argentin, pendant que les dirigeants Omar Berrada et Jason Wilcox validaient à Monaco avec Sir Jim Ratcliffe le limogeage de Carrick.U.S. Soccer négocie désormais le départ de Pochettino avec Manchester United contre une indemnité de résiliation estimée à 10 millions de dollars, soit environ 7 millions de livres sterling.

Pour combler ce vide, les dirigeants américains ont de grandes ambitions. D'après The Athletic, la fédération américaine fait de Pep Guardiola sa priorité absolue pour succéder à Mauricio Pochettino. Le nom du technicien catalan se trouve déjà au centre de discussions en interne afin de déterminer la méthode pour le convaincre. Selon le journaliste Tom Bogert, l'instance prévoit d'initier la démarche par un appel direct au coach espagnol. Sans club depuis son départ de Manchester City en mai 2026, après dix années marquées par six titres de Premier League et une Ligue des champions, Guardiola avait déjà été approché par U.S. Soccer en 2024 puis avant la Coupe du monde 2022.

U.S. Soccer est prête à répondre aux exigences salariales très élevées du technicien catalan pour finaliser sa venue. Les décideurs américains savent également que Pep Guardiola possède une attache particulière avec les États-Unis, pour y avoir habité à New York pendant son année sabbatique en 2012 après son passage au FC Barcelone. Toutefois, convaincre l'ancien entraîneur des Cityzens reste un défi complexe. En juillet 2026, Guardiola avait ainsi décliné une offre de la fédération italienne après une rencontre à Barcelone avec Paolo Maldini et Leonardo, car il souhaitait impérativement respecter son année sabbatique à l'écart des terrains.

L'argument décisif de la sélection américaine pourrait ainsi venir d'un calendrier taillé sur mesure. En effet, ce projet permettrait à Pep Guardiola de prolonger son repos, le match amical de décembre contre la Jamaïque étant réservé aux joueurs locaux, ce qui repousserait ses débuts effectifs à mars 2027. En attendant l'issue de ce feuilleton, Mauricio Pochettino dirige actuellement l'équipe américaine en Géorgie. Les États-Unis disputent leur quart de finale de Ligue des nations de la Concacaf contre Haïti, le 14 novembre à Miami pour le match aller, puis le 17 novembre 2026 à Cincinnati pour le retour, avant le ralliement attendu de l'Argentin vers Manchester.

Bitcoin faces a $25B Treasury test after demand for US debt weakens

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The thirty-year sale follows weaker ten-year participation, with Bitcoin’s risk tied to yields and financing conditions.

Treasury's Nov. 10 sale of $42 billion in 10-year notes drew weaker bid coverage and indirect participation than October's auction, despite clearing at a lower yield. That leaves Bitcoin investors watching whether today's long-bond sale makes financing more expensive.

Treasury has scheduled a $25 billion sale of 30-year bonds for Nov. 12 at 1 p.m. Eastern. Its result will offer another reading on demand for long-term government debt.

The November 10-year auction cleared at a high yield of 5.02%, down from 5.3% at October's reopening. But its bid-to-cover ratio fell to 2.46 from 2.77, indicating less bidding relative to the amount awarded. The yield measures the return accepted by buyers; coverage describes the volume of bidding.

Indirect bidders received about 61.2% of November's competitive awards, compared with 80.3% in October. These shares exclude noncompetitive allocations and Federal Reserve add-ons.

The size comparison matters. October's $39 billion sale reopened the August note, while November offered a new security. August provides a same-size comparison: its $42 billion new issue drew 2.53 bid coverage and approximately 76.7% indirect participation. It cleared at 4.68%, below November's yield.

November drew weaker participation on both measures against each comparator. August matches its offering size and new-issue status, although the securities and market conditions differ.

November's ten-year sale drew lower bid coverage and indirect participation than August and October, even though the yield comparison changes.

October: $39bn reopening, 5.300%. November: $42bn new issue, 5.020%. November's yield was lower, alongside weaker participation.

August and November: $42bn new issues. November's yield was higher than August's 4.683%, while participation was weaker.

The participation signal is consistent across both comparisons; the yield signal depends on the benchmark.

Different securities and market conditions limit comparison. October was a smaller reopening; August matches November's offering size and new-issue status. Indirect bidders include domestic investors. No thirty-year outcome or Bitcoin price response is shown.

Separate linear bar scales: bid coverage 0-3 and indirect competitive-award share 0-100%. Shares equal indirect competitive awards divided by total competitive awards, rounded to one decimal. Yield dots use a 4.0%-5.5% axis. All observations are 2026 auction results.

If weaker bond demand translates into higher long-term yields and tighter financing, investors could have less room in their risk budgets for crypto exposure. Firmer demand and stable financing would weaken that channel of concern.

The authors of a 2023 IMF working paper found that U.S. monetary tightening reduced a broad crypto price factor through investor risk-taking. The study examines monetary tightening, rather than the effect of a single Treasury auction.

The financing timetable also limits the cash interpretation. The 30-year auction settles Nov. 16, alongside the other November refunding securities. Treasury's $125 billion refunding replaces approximately $97 billion of privately held maturities and raises about $28 billion in new private cash. The bond sale's $25 billion headline amount is gross issuance, rather than wholly new borrowing.

Today's test is whether buyers return for the 30-year sale, and whether yields rise if demand disappoints.

BREAKING: Michael Carrick sacked as Manchester United prepare for Mauricio Pochettino

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

Manchester United have sacked Michael Carrick as head coach following a disastrous start to the 2026/27 campaign that has left the club in the Premier League relegation zone.

The former Manchester United midfielder was informed of the decision today after travelling to the Carrington after meeting chief executive Omar Berrada, director of football Jason Wilcox and co-owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe.

Michael Carrick initially took charge on an interim basis after Ruben Amorim was sacked last season. He had a great start in the interim role, guiding Manchester United to third place and securing a return to the Champions League after missing the last two seasons.

Michael Carrick sacked by MUFC. Top lad, got the team in the Champions League against expectations. Gave it his all, night and day. Protected others, never threw anyone under a bus. Worked in difficult circumstances. Leaves with head held high. I wish him and his staff the very…

His first full season at Old Trafford has been a completely different story.

Manchester United have managed just one Premier League victory from their opening 10 matches, collecting just eight points from five draws and four defeats. Their only win came against Ipswich Town in August, while a disastrous run of results has pushed the club down to 18th in the table.

Sunday’s chaotic late 4-4 draw against Aston Villa was another setback, with United failing to climb out of the relegation zone before the international break.

Manchester United have already identified Mauricio Pochettino as their preferred replacement, with reports indicating that an agreement in principle has been reached with the former Tottenham and Chelsea manager.

The Argentine is currently in charge of the United States national team but is understood to be interested in taking the Old Trafford job.

Pochettino’s contract reportedly includes a release clause worth around £7-7.5 million, which United would need to activate. Talks over personal terms are also understood to be advanced with Pochettino’s camp.

United are now preparing for another change in the dugout, with Pochettino expected to be handed the responsibility of rescuing their season.

Steven Holl designs The Sculpture Watch as a tribute to Álvaro Siza

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Photography by Nuno Sousa Dias unless stated

American architect Steven Holl has designed a colourful watch for Swiss watch brand Cauny that was informed by conversations with Portuguese architect Álvaro Siza.

Named The Sculpture Watch, the timepiece has a dial that includes four intersecting blocks of colour within a circular case that resembles a watch designed by Siza for Cauny.

According to Holl, his watch was designed to be a dialogue between the two architects and was directly informed by a compliment the Portuguese architect once gave him.

“On July 25, 2018, I gave a summer lecture in at the Porto Academy,” Holl told Dezeen. “Siza came to my talk and came up to me after and said ‘Steven, you use colour like Matisse, I must try in my next project’.”

“Siza is the greatest living architect,” he continued. “I will never forget that afternoon. This watch is an homage to Alvaro Siza,”

The Sculpture Watch has a three-dimensional dial with the red, green, yellow and blue colour blocks raised on top of each other.

It was intended as a direct translation of Holl’s watercolour paintings that were complimented by Siza. Holl is well known for his painting and begins the majority of his architecture projects with a small watercolour sketch.

“Watercolor painting, integral to my design process for architecture, inspires this timepiece,” said Holl.

“The colour volumes on the dial are made of brass; well-tested in watchmaking and best to allow Cauny to reproduce the watercolours.”

“My approach to colour is conceptual,” he continued.

“I try to engage colour in the idea driving the design, like the ‘projected colour’ from unseen surfaces in the DE Shaw trading floor, or the complimentary colours in the ‘seven bottles of light’ in our Chapel of St. Ignatius, which is analogous to the Jesuit spiritual exercises.”

Holl also reflected on how colour can reflect the passing of time.

“Time is marked in a personal way, with each moment its own polychrome experience,” he said.

“Colour turns time’s passage into both a visual and temporal experience. The fragility and variation of colour remind us that time is a human experience of duration.”

The Sculpture Watch is the fifth piece created for Cauny in its The Architects of Time Series. Following Siza, who designed the first timepiece in the collection, architects Rafael Moneo, Eduardo Souto Moura and Tadao Ando have designed watches for the brand.

Numerous high-profile architects have turned their hands to watch design and we recently rounded up six watches designed by Pritzker Architecture Prize-winning architects.

Photography by Nuno Sousa Dias unless stated

The Best College Ski Towns in the U.S.

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Big Sky and Lone Peak (Photo: Aerial_Views / Getty)

In today’s fast-paced, instant-gratification-obsessed world, it’s harder than ever for colleges to grab a young person’s attention. Don’t get us wrong, they’re trying, turning to drastic measures like on-campus waterparks, steakhouses, and even valet parking to attract a rising crop of future graduates.

But what if colleges had a taller, more natural card to play? For some mountain lovers, the idea of a college experience that combines curriculum with outdoor adventure is a prime way to push the needle. The prospect has made university towns like Burlington, Vermont, and Salt Lake City, Utah, into winter sports juggernauts, but it has also brought smaller towns into the discussion, putting them on the map and putting their universities on the “must apply” list.

So, what’s the best U.S. college town for skiing and snowboarding? We considered a handful of factors (proximity is just the start, after all) to find that answer, region by region. That means more than just the skiing itself, but also the culture, cuisine, and community that come along with it, making these mountain paradises appeal to college students and graduates alike. When it comes to studying hard and shredding harder, here are the best places to earn those turns…err, degrees.

The sheer amount of ski areas and college towns in the Northeast is enough to overwhelm, but let’s keep it simple: there’s only one Burlington, Vermont. Perched on the edge of Lake Champlain, this four-season wonder is also home base for the University of Vermont, St. Michael’s College, and Champlain College—and a whole lot of skiers and boarders between the three. In addition to national championship-winning alpine and Nordic ski programs, UVM has also grown one of the strongest ski and snowboard clubs in the country. Mountains around Burlington are all about showing their student population love, serving up heavily discounted college season passes at resorts like Jay Peak, Sugarbush, Mad River Glen (skiers only), and Smugglers’ Notch. The days of the legendary Triple Major may be over (if you know, you know), but there are still plenty of cheap turns for the higher education crowd.

After a long day on the slopes, take a walk down Church Street for the ultimate cultural contrast. This pedestrian-only brick walkway echoes Europe with a uniquely New England appeal. Stop at the Church Street Tavern for a local brew and then trek to Bove’s for its famous Italian cuisine. Burlington is a music hotbed, so don’t forget to pay your respects at Higher Ground on your way out of town.

Lake Champlain—need we say more? This large body of freshwater is Burlington’s lifeblood, providing public beaches, public sailing centers, unmatched paddling access, and even some fun urban cliff jumping (we suggest Red Rocks for an evening plunge). The Green Mountains earn their name here in the summer, crisscrossed with hiking and biking trails that rival most any in the country. Camel’s Hump might be the crown jewel, but there are plenty of day hikes within a short drive of town.

You might not think of the Southeast as ski country, but don’t tell Boone, North Carolina. This college town is home to Appalachian State University and set at the foot of the Blue Ridge Mountains—one of the premier snow-sliding regions in the South. Boone is an easy drive from three formidable Southeast ski resorts, and Appalachian Ski Mountain is less than 15 minutes from campus. In addition to having one of the best ski schools on the East Coast, the local area has night skiing and four terrain parks. On select nights throughout the season, students can score $16 night skiing tickets as well. For the more advanced, Beech Mountain is just 45 minutes away, opening up a realm of on-hill possibilities.

If you’ve made it out to Beech Mountain, don’t sleep on the 5506’ Skybar—the highest elevation bar on the Eastern Seaboard. For those looking for a lower elevation reward, the town of Boone is known for its home-crafted beer scene and beautiful downtown. Grab a pint at Appalachian Mountain Brewing or size up a mocktail at Fizzed before checking out Blowing Rock Village south of Boone’s downtown.

Boone was a summer escape long before it became a winter destination, once considered the finest vacation retreat between Newport, Rhode Island, and Palm Beach, Florida. Nowadays residents live the best of both worlds, scoring winter turns before hitting the summertime bike trails at Rocky Knob Mountain Bike Park. Fly-fishing aficionados have their pick of honey holes along the Blue Ridge Parkway and local guides are friendly enough to share the goods, or at least some of them.

Set smack dab in the middle of one of the country’s most overlooked snowbelts, Marquette, Michigan, has the distinct pleasure of being both off the tourist map and in the crosshairs of winter’s biggest storms. The largest city on Michigan’s Upper Peninsula, Marquette is also a university town, welcoming students to Northern Michigan University every fall. But when snow starts to fall, this student population converts to bona fide powder hounds. Marquette Mountain sits within city borders and receives over 210 inches a year—making it one of the snowiest places east of the Mississippi. NMU students get a whole season of it for just $361.

If just enjoying the slopes isn’t enough, undergrads can also pursue an associate degree in ski area management. This unique program runs in conjunction with Gogebic Community College, a school that places students into five-month internships at actual ski resorts and has its own ski hill, Mount Zion, right on campus.

Marquette is where college bar and ski bar collide into one heck of an après evening. Grab a $2 mystery beer at the Wooden Nickel Bar or dive into a face-sized cocktail while taking over the jukebox at Remie’s. The Midwest does après different—and we’re all for it.

Set on the shores of Lake Superior, Marquette turns students into long-term residents. Presque Isle Park and the Black Rocks offer up stunning, cliffside trails and wild forest, while biking in the NTN trail systems features a little something for every ability level. Cutting right through town, the Iron Ore Heritage Trail stretches 47 miles across the region, connecting caves, ruins, and historical stops related to the area’s mining roots.

This pick’s a little spicy, but hear us out. Boulder and Crested Butte have the skiing and mountain biking thing on lock. Salt Lake City has mountain proximity in spades. But Durango? This college town has a bit of everything—minus the people and mountain-bound bustle. Home to Fort Lewis College, Durango sits in the cradle of Colorado’s San Juan Mountains, an overlooked corner of the state with some serious vertical relief. Students attending Fort Lewis have their pick of Purgatory, Wolf Creek, and Hesperus within an hour or so of campus, but for those seeking bucket-list turns, Silverton and Telluride are a respectable day trip. If class is letting out late, there’s even Chapman Hill, a small (but steep) community ski area at the edge of campus, which offers an entire season of skiing and riding for $130.

Durango is a locals’ town right down to its libations. While several breweries have popped up over the years (Animas, Steamworks, and Ska, to name a few), it’s hard to pass up a post-mountain beer at Anarchy Brewing Co. Aside from some seriously strong IPAs (we liked the Sex Panther), this spot is four-legged friendly and has good food served on its outdoor patio.

If you just can’t wait to wet your whistle, the town’s biggest cheat code is the Durango Hot Springs Resort and Spa. It may sound bougie, but this series of naturally heated pools is a hangout that will soothe the soul without breaking the bank. The facility offers beer and wine for purchase and is only 20 minutes from Purgatory Ski Resort.

It’s deals and access like this that keep Durangatangs in town after they graduate, but while winter is a highlight, this locale is all about summer recreation. The Animas River runs right through the heart of Durango, often dotted with rafters and flotillas of varying sizes. Further downstream, the town has its very own whitewater park, and Durango Whitewater is a direct feeder program to the U.S. Olympic whitewater teams.

Beyond the river, Durango features hundreds of miles of mountain biking trails ranging from flowy beginner to some of the gnarliest technical terrain in the West. In fact, in 2030 the town is slated to host the UCI Mountain Bike World Championships.

Set between Montana’s two biggest mountain resorts and home to a robust network of in-town Nordic skiing, Bozeman has quietly been cultivating its ski community for decades. Students at Montana State University can take a free shuttle bus to nearby Bridger Bowl or unlock a discounted season pass at Big Sky (though it won’t include Saturdays or tram access). But Montana State skiers and snowboarders are not just on-piste enthusiasts. For the backcountry curious, the school has a strong backcountry scene including a dedicated Backcountry Club for avalanche education, gear help, group trips, and networking with professionals in the nearby Custer Gallatin National Forest and beyond.

It just so happens that some of Montana’s most epic skiing is located above some of Montana’s most epic après bars, so lucky you. Grab a pint at Scissor Bill’s in Big Sky or sip a Cold Smoke Scotch Ale at the Grizzly Bar off the Bridger Bowl parking lot—either way, you can’t lose.

The key to a good night is making it back to Bozeman, though, where Main Street offers up one of the more fun Eagles Club experiences around, complete with cheap drinks and rowdy live music. The Zebra is another community staple, where college students, ranchers, and old-timers can mingle over good local tunes.

Bozeman is an outdoors town with a frontier soul. The changing demographics lean toward outdoor recreation, but there’s an underlying and common current dedicated to protecting and enjoying the wild places in its periphery, and it shows. Hyalite Canyon is home to some of the best ice climbing in the world, and hosts a slew of climbers, runners, Nordic skiers, and hikers year-round. The Gallatin and Madison Rivers and Bozeman Creek all pass through or near town as well, giving anglers and kayakers primo access right out the front door, and plenty of options for anyone looking to play a little midweek hooky.

This Northwest representative was a close call with Bellingham and its coastal charm, but there’s something about the high desert that just doesn’t quit. Bend didn’t start out as a college town, but with the arrival of Oregon State University’s Cascade Campus (and the continued local support for Central Oregon Community College), this outdoors hotspot has become one heck of a diploma destination. Just 30 minutes from the 360-degree volcano skiing and riding of Mount Bachelor, Bend is a college town wrapped in mountain pedigree. We’d be silly not to mention the extensive backcountry access in Deschutes National Forest, and the Olympians that have come out of Mount Bachelor’s powerhouse Nordic skiing program. All of that and 300-plus sunny days a year? Where do we enroll?

Ski bar legend has it that before après was a French word, it was a guiding light in Central Oregon. Kidding aside, few ski towns have as many post-ski food and beverage options as the mighty Bend. While the boom has curbed in recent years, there are still over 30 breweries and taprooms within Bend city limits. Crux Fermentation Project and Monkless Belgian Ales rest on the banks of the picturesque Deschutes River, but the cozy climes and kickass beer at Terranaut Beer won Oregon’s Small Brewery of the Year in 2025.

For folks who turn college degrees into outdoor family roots, there are few spots quite like Bend. With year-round river recreation (including a retractable surf wave co-designed with surfer Gerry Lopez) and multiple trail running and mountain biking trail systems around town, Bend residents don’t know how or care to sit still. Beyond city limits, Central Oregon is an absolute natural playground, whether it be rock climbing and hiking around Smith Rock State Park, wandering the woods around Sisters, or getting out on the water in Prineville. If your friend says they’ve gotten bored of Bend, it’s time to find a new friend.

Bitcoin Price Slides Toward $73K as Nvidia Plunges 9% and MSCI Drops Saylor’s Strategy

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

BTC slips as Nvidia sinks 9% after hours and MSCI drops Strategy. ETFs lost $1.88B in November, 94% of it from IBIT.

In Bitcoin news, the coin fell from $74,811 to $73,480 within two hours of the U.S. close. Bitcoin slipped overnight after two shocks hit U.S. markets after Wednesday’s close. Nvidia shares fell about 9% in after-hours trading on a new report about CEO Jensen Huang. Index provider MSCI also confirmed it will remove Strategy, the largest corporate Bitcoin holder, from its global indexes.

It trades at $73,588 on Binance on Thursday morning, down 0.4% in 24 hours and 5.8% in a week.

The funds are not helping. U.S. spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs) lost $299.8 million on Wednesday, their fifth straight day of outflows. Nasdaq futures are down 0.9%.

Bitcoin ETFs Shed $300 Million, Ether Funds Lose $145 Million

According to SoSoValue, U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $300 million in net outflows on November 11, while Morgan Stanley's MSBT led individual fund inflows with $8.96 million. Spot Ethereum ETFs saw $145 million in… pic.twitter.com/5sn88B2JQX

— Wu Blockchain (@WuBlockchain) November 12, 2026

So what happened, and how much of the selling comes from the ETFs?

The first hit came from Nvidia, the world’s most valuable chipmaker. The New York Times reported on Wednesday evening that Huang had a second relationship with a senior employee who reports to him. The Wall Street Journal reported that Nvidia’s board is preparing to remove him as CEO.

Nvidia had closed the day up 2.9% at $173.73. Within two hours, it traded at $157.99, down 9.1%. It is still down 8.6% before Thursday’s open. Nvidia has lost about 15% since last Friday. It first fell on Monday, after its board opened a separate probe into reports that Huang was the target of a foreign intelligence effort.

Bitcoin has traded closely with tech stocks this year, and it fell with them. Nasdaq 100 futures dropped 1.1% in the same two hours.

The second hit is closer to crypto. MSCI, whose indexes guide trillions of dollars in index funds, will delete Strategy from its global indexes after the market close on November 30. It ruled that Strategy looks more like an investment fund holding Bitcoin than an operating business. Japan’s Metaplanet and Upexi are also being removed.

Funds that track those indexes will have to sell their Strategy shares. JPMorgan earlier estimated that selling at about $2.8 billion. Strategy says the funds hold about 13 million shares, 3.1% of its stock. Its shares are down 1.8% before the open.

For Bitcoin news, that matters beyond one stock. Strategy is the steadiest corporate buyer of Bitcoin. Fewer index buyers for its shares could make it harder and more costly to raise money for new purchases.

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Predicts Best Low-Cap Crypto

The ETF selling started before either shock. To see how big the ETF side of this Bitcoin news is, we added up the daily net flows of the U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs for every trading day in November.

The streak is not a record yet. The longest run of outflows this year lasted 13 trading days in May and June, just before Bitcoin fell below $60,000.

The Fed adds to the pressure. October’s hot inflation report on Tuesday pushed the odds of a December rate hike on Polymarket to about 97%. Higher rates make cash and bonds pay more, which tends to pull money out of funds like these.

EXPLORE: Why Is Crypto Crashing Today? Bitcoin Falls Below $73K as Zcash (ZEC) Leads Crypto Losses

BTC trades at $73,588, holding above Tuesday’s low of $72,727, its lowest price since August 20. On the daily chart, where each candle covers one day, it sits below both of its exponential moving averages (EMAs), averages that give recent prices more weight. The 50-day EMA stands at $78,696 and the 200-day EMA at $76,422.

The next Bitcoin news to watch is Thursday’s ETF flow data and how Nvidia trades at the open. A sixth day of outflows and a weak tech session would put $72,727 to the test.

Dogecoin (DOGE) Wallet Pushes Quick Fix for Bitcoin (BTC) Transactions Ahead of Major Release

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Dogecoin wallet 'MyDoge' has rolled out a quick update to address Bitcoin swap and send issues affecting users of its Classic Wallet, ahead of the planned release of version 3.1.2 this weekend.

Alex, CTO of MyDoge Wallet, announced the update, explaining that the team had pushed an interim fix to resolve the reported BTC transaction problems before the broader software release.

Pushed an update to quickly address BTC swap/send issues while using "Classic Wallet" before we release the full v3.1.2 this weekend Simply close / reopen the app and you should see: 3.1.1-652.1 or 3.1.1-652.2 in the "About MyDoge" settings

According to the announcement, users do not need to manually download a separate update. Instead, they can simply close and reopen the MyDoge app to receive the latest version.

Users can verify whether the update has been applied by navigating to the "About MyDoge" section in the app settings. The expected version numbers are 3.1.1-652.1 or 3.1.1-652.2.

In Dogecoin ecosystem news, DogeOS is nearing mainnet launch, bringing an EVM-compatible app layer to DOGE that would let developers build DeFi, games and other apps on Dogecoin for the first time.

In October, DogeOS opened a public test network for Ethereum-style trading, lending and other applications using DOGE, seeking to give Dogecoin holders more ways to use their coins beyond payments and price speculation.

In a recent post on X, Dogecoin Foundation director of product Timothy Stebbing shared a Dogecoin ecosystem 2026 update.

Stebbing predicts that the next three to four years for Dogecoin are likely to be shaped more by the development and adoption of smart-contract application layers than by large, pre-emptive changes to Dogecoin’s base protocol. He cited DogeOS as an early and visible example of that direction. If it and similar efforts succeed in attracting sustained developer activity and real usage while continuing to treat Dogecoin as the settlement and value-transfer foundation, Stebbing sees the ecosystem gaining additional utility without compromising the simplicity and reliability of the Layer 1.

However, the proposed mechanism for native proof verification on Dogecoin still requires community discussion and, ultimately, activation. Thus, adoption will depend on whether the resulting applications are useful enough that people choose to use them. None of these uncertainties change the underlying architectural advantage: the Layer 1 can remain a focused means of exchange while the surrounding ecosystem gains the capacity for richer functionality.

Ranking every Man Utd manager since 1992 from worst to best: Carrick 6th…

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

After 26 years of the same man in the dugout, Manchester United have become just like everyone else with quick turnarounds for their managers.

The very high bar set by Sir Alex Ferguson has never been reached, or really come close to, but of the eight men to have managed United in the Premier League era, there are some obvious highs and some even more obvious lows.

Here’s our ranking of the eight permanent Manchester United managers since the Premier League began.

It’s an obvious answer for the worst manager.

Amorim just never really seemed to get it. He was wedded to a 3-4-3 that he simply did not have the players for.

The Portuguese man is not alone in taking the blame. The INEOS hivemind knew what Amorim was about and also insisted he joined midseason rather than give him a full summer, but Amorim should have been more flexible.

He used up all of his goodwill with a 15th-place finish in his first season, United’s lowest in 51 years, and did not help himself by telling the world media thoughts he should perhaps have kept for his personal diary. Then there was Grimsby.

Summer spending of £200million did see an improvement the following year and he even changed to four at the back in December, but he signed his own death warrant when he criticised the club’s hierarchy.

He left Old Trafford with the worst win percentage of a permanent manager since Frank O’Farrell in 1972.

Moyes’ win ratio was not even that bad, but the job seemed to overwhelm him.

He reportedly told Rio Ferdinand to play like Phil Jagielka and his desire to sign two of his former players suggested he never really stepped up from being Everton manager.

The squad Ferguson left him was not exactly built for the future and he had a hapless chief executive in Ed Woodward trying to sign every player on the planet but early losses to Chelsea, Manchester City and West Brom corroded fans’ confidence.

United and their fans were likely always expecting a drop after Ferguson left but a loss to Spurs on New Year’s Day had the club in seventh and out of all European spots.

As for Europe, United were embarrassed away at Olympiacos and even if they recovered in the second leg, it simply delayed their execution as Bayern swept them aside 4-2 over the two legs.

The United board, to their credit, gave Moyes time but his tenure came to an end when qualification for the Champions League was mathematically impossible.

Perhaps most damning of all, Moyes initiated a collective lowering of expectations which United have never recovered from.

Carrick undeniably did well in his return to the Old Trafford dugout, but it was a bit like playing FIFA on easy mode.

Amorim’s failure gave Carrick the gift of a clear calendar and putting square pegs in square holes was an easy early win for the former Middlesbrough boss.

But his first full season in charge saw United marooned in the relegation zone after 10 matches. A sole win against promoted Ipswich has the club in 12th even after £150m spent in the summer.

Ultimately, Carrick did little to prove he was at the required level. But that’s the fault of the people who picked him.

On paper, a 55% win rate and two trophies suggest Ten Hag was a success at United, but there were serious flaws behind the numbers.

His signings were, by and large, terrible. £86million for Antony is the tip of an ugly iceberg that also includes expensive purchases of Rasmus Hojlund, Casemiro, Mason Mount and Andre Onana.

The Dutchman was also a bad man-manager and communicator, summed up by Fred’s recent comments which described him as “a piece of crap” when it came to speaking to players.

But tactically he could be very good, with his best win coming against a superior Manchester City side in the FA Cup final. However, for every good win there is a 7-0 to Liverpool and 6-3 at the Etihad defeat to counter it.

After Mourinho had released a mushroom cloud of toxicity, United needed their belly rubbed and a pat on the head. Enter Ole.

The biggest achievement of Solskjaer’s career was instilling a positive attitude, something that contributed to 91 wins out of 168 matches.

He got the club to third and second in his two full seasons and often got his team to turn up in big occasions, including a notable win over PSG.

But Solskjaer was always the bridesmaid. United reached four semi-finals under the Norwegian and lost them all. When they finally did get past the hurdle in the 2021 Europa League final, they lost to Villarreal.

His tenure was also massively upended by the arrival of Cristiano Ronaldo. While a great PR win,  accommodating Ronaldo comes at a cost to the rest of the team and a 5-0 loss to Liverpool highlighted the disparity between Solskjaer and the likes of Jurgen Klopp.

After the Moyes experiment failed, United opted for experience in Van Gaal.

This was a man who had managed Ajax, Barcelona, Bayern Munich and the Netherlands so was no stranger to the pressure that comes with top jobs.

But he was in his 60s by the time he arrived at Old Trafford and already looked like a manager whose best years were behind him.

A 2-1 defeat to Swansea in his first game was an early warning sign and Van Gaal spent far too long suggesting his team was not adjusted to his methods yet.

To make matters worse, the football was ponderous. United fans chanted for more attacks but Van Gaal is as stubborn as they come and insisted on sticking to his slow build-up.

He did though push United back towards the top of the table and, crucially, Champions League qualification.

Van Gaal also won the first silverware of the post-Ferguson era before being shuffled off for Mourinho.

There’s a price you pay when you hire Mourinho.

The positives of his reign are a second-place finish behind Pep Guardiola’s Manchester City, which has since been labelled as illegal. Even before the verdict, Mourinho described that season as one of his greatest accomplishments as a manager.

He also brought silverware back to the club with a League Cup win, Europa League and the Community Shield, something he referred to as a treble.

On a player front, he oversaw the departure of an ageing Wayne Rooney, which was always going to be a tricky bridge to cross, and generally made the team better than the sum of its parts.

Yet the cost of Mourinho is the position he left the club in.

By the end of his 31-month tenure, the dressing room was toxic. He had fallen out with the board, fallen out with Paul Pogba and took to publicly calling out the likes of Luke Shaw and Anthony Martial.

The result was a sigh of relief when Mourinho left and United needed a morale booster in the form of Solskjaer to shake the Jose cloud.

"One of those ways where you can use AI ethically": CDPR have modified Geralt's Polish voice in The Witcher 3: Songs of the Past with genAI, due to actor's stroke

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Jacek Rozenek reportedly gave his blessing

CD Projekt Red have reportedly enlisted Ukrainian AI firm Respeecher to modify voice lines recorded for the Polish version of Geralt of Rivia in The Witcher 3's upcoming Songs of the Past expansion. The move's been made with the permission of Geralt's Polish voice actor, Jacek Rozenek, and is rooted in the studio wanting his new lines to sound similar to the ones he recorded for the original game prior to having a stroke which has altered how he sounds.

Game File report that after suffering a stroke in 2019 and having to rehabilitate his speech, Rozenek was able to record some lines as Geralt for a quest in The Witcher 3's 2022 next-gen update, but that doing so left the actor "so exhausted". With next year's Songs of the Past expansion, CD Projekt Red were seemingly more concerned about making sure lines recorded by post-stroke Rozenek don't sound markedly different to those he recorded a decade ago for the base game and its existing expansions.

After a meeting between the two sides confirmed that Rozenek's voice had grown lower and gruffer due to some combination of the stroke and him simply getting older, the studio made the decision not to recast the actor, but instead let modify Respeecher modify his lines to make them sound closer to those original recordings. Rozenek reportedly gave his blessing for this to go ahead. "The point is that this voice should be as close as possible to my voice from 12 years ago," he told Game File. "I have great trust...for the creators of this game."

This isn't the first time CD Projekt Red have enlisted Respeecher's services. Back in 2023, it was revealed that the studio had used speech synthesis software to make ripperdoc Viktor Vektor's lines in the Polish version of Cyberpunk 2077's Phantom Liberty expansion sound like actor Miłogost Reczek. Reczek died in 2021 before he could reprise his role as Vektor for the DLC, so rather than recasting the character across the whole game to avoid noticeable differences in voice, CD Projekt got another actor to voice Vektor's new lines for Phantom Liberty, then used the genAI tech to make those lines sound like Reczek was delivering them. CD Projekt Red said at the time that they'd gotten permission from Reczek's family before going ahead with their plan.

Speaking to Game File about this latest use of genAI on Rozenek's Songs of the Past lines, CD Projekt localisation producer Mikołaj Szwed noted that "as a person working with voice actors over all the regions in the world, this is a big concern". "I do think this is one of those ways where you can use AI ethically, with consent and also to do something good," he added. The argument the studio are making at current is that this was "a special case" due to Rozenek's specific situation with Songs of the Past.

Geralt's English voice actor Doug Cockle reportedly won't have his lines for the new expansion get the same genAI treatment, nor will any lines Rozenek records for future Witcher games, with the studio seemingly happy to lean on the passage of in-game time as an explanation for Geralt sounding different in the latter case.

As ever, there's a debate to be had as to whether CD Projekt Red should have simply recast Rozenek's role in Songs of the Past if the difference bothered them so, possibly while bringing the actor on to help tutor whomever his human replacement for Songs of the Past would be in nailing the exact gravelly tone needed for Geralt. That way, you'd have two actors being paid for work rather than forking out for genAI instead of one of them, though it's possibly an idea that'd only work well in theory. If I were Rozenek I'm not sure I'd be totally happy aiding someone who could potentially be tapped to replace me going forwards, though it does very much seem like the lesser of two evils when compared to the potential of voice actors being replaced by genAI line readings.

As far as the game alone is concerned, I'm also not sure Geralt's voice being different in the new expansion is something that'd bother me too much when playing. One would assume he'd not suddenly be sounding like a member of Alvin and the Chipmunks, and there are plenty of storyline justifications you could concoct or lean on for him sounding that bit gruffer at times.

Max Lowe breaks silence as Sheffield Wednesday situation changes

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Dave Challinor has taken over from Henrik Pedersen as the manager of Sheffield Wednesday, and he may find it a bigger challenge than he first thought.

The Owls were defeated by Oldham Athletic of League Two in Round One of the FA Cup last weekend despite lining up with a strong team, before a more youthful side were hammered 4-0 by Accrington Stanley in the EFL Trophy.

Challinor is still yet to take in a league game, which comes this weekend against Plymouth Argyle on home soil, and he'll soon welcome back his former Stockport County favourite Louie Barry into his plans, having been sidelined since early September with an ankle injury.

But with Barry heading back to fitness, Challinor is now having to deal with one more injury to a key player at the opposite end of the pitch.

One of Sheffield Wednesday's key players for a number of years now has been Max Lowe - the left-sided defender has been a top performer and has stuck with the club through their tough period when he could've easily left for another side.

EFL expert Don Goodman has revealed the three clubs across all three tiers to have surprised him the most so far in 2026/27 to Football League World

If there's one drawback to Lowe though, it's his injury record. The defender missed the final eight games of his debut 2024-25 season with a knee injury, and in 2025-26 he'd miss a full three months with a calf problem.

More setbacks followed in late August this year when Lowe suffered with a hamstring injury, and after returning to action a month ago, it's now been revealed that the 29-year-old has yet again suffered a reoccurrence of the problem he picked up two-and-a-half months ago.

Lowe entered the Oldham defeat from the bench after 71 minutes at the weekend, and whilst finishing the entirety of extra time at Boundary Park, the after-effects of his injury means that he's going to be missing for at least four weeks, and potentially up to six.

The Owls and Challinor himself could do with Lowe back for the busy Christmas period, where they'll play three times between Boxing Day and New Year's Day, but given it's his second spell on the sidelines already this season, it's likely that no risks will be taken with the ex-Sheffield United left-back.

And Lowe himself has taken to his Instagram account to send a message to Owls fans regarding his new injury, and it's one that's come from the heart.

Some Sheffield Wednesday fans have been left frustrated at Lowe's newest injury though, with one claiming that he should've been replaced in the summer knowing what his fitness record was like.

And another Owls supporter believes that the medical department itself may need a complete overhaul, given the amount of niggling injuries that keep flaring up across the squad.

If Wednesday were playing in a back four under Challinor, then Reece James would be the obvious replacement like-for-like with Lowe, and that would be the case too in what looks to be the manager's preferred left of a back three.

However, James is also on the sidelines right now, having suffered a groin injury, and he's also expected back around the same time as Lowe.

It'll now likely be Sil Swinkels who comes in for Lowe on the left-hand side of a back three, having served his one-match suspension for a red card against Blackpool, although he too has already had a period on the treatment table.

Challinor may of course look to play a back four in some matches, given his last few months at Stockport saw him pivot to more of a 4-3-3 and sometimes a 4-4-2 given the attacking options he had to choose from, but for now, it may make more sense to utilise the 3-4-2-1, with Jordi Liongola and Yan Valery operating as wing-backs.

Manchester United must end a vicious cycle after Michael Carrick’s exit

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Michael Carrick’s Manchester United sacking has to act as a circuit-breaker

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The more things change at Manchester United, the more they seem to stay the same.

In the 13 years since Sir Alex Ferguson finally hung up his hairdryer, Old Trafford has burned through serial winners, eminent coaches of varying ages and those steeped in club DNA, with Michael Carrick joining the latter category in the wake of his sacking.

The former midfielder becomes United’s third mid-season change in as many years, joining Ruben Amorim and Erik ten Hag in their increasing hall of managerial infamy.

He was the latest convenient scapegoat for the failings of those above him, who are yet to face repercussions no matter how many managerial reigns continue to be tarnished by them. The unstinting backing he received from the United faithful, even as results continued to tumble, bought director of football Jason Wilcox and chief executive officer Omar Berrada more time to conjure up something resembling a coherent succession plan.

The top brass, including absentee co-owners Sir Jim Ratcliffe and the Glazers, were put on notice as early as a late-October humbling by Bournemouth. The green and gold movement which dominated early protests against the American overlords is back in vogue, as are industrial-language chants demanding they sell up and vacate at speed. Players, too, are no longer being afforded a free pass after throwing yet another manager under the bus. A steadily-formed queue of dissidents in the wake of a Champions League humbling by Como, headed up by captain Bruno Fernandes, lifted the veil of immunity which previously hung over the dressing room.

If not for a sprinkling of hollow platitudes, the confirmatory statement which sealed Carrick's fate could have easily been copied and pasted from the one which rendered Amorim as persona non grata in January. There will be no shortage of material for Amazon's upcoming behind-the-scenes documentary on the club, yet the modus operandi remains painfully familiar. The Red Devils have cast aside another Champions League-winning fan favourite who answered the call in their latest hour of need. But while Ole Gunnar Solskjaer spent three years 'at the wheel', Carrick was barely allowed to ride pillion in his 10 months.

Both remained publicly in thrall to Ferguson's heyday despite the fact their former mentor indirectly set the wheels in motion for their respective dugout departures. For all his reverence, the Scot's previous autonomy paved the route for the imbued chaos that is the modern-day United. He was an analog man who resisted moving into football’s new digital age at every turn. A necessary recalibrating of the power dynamic for those that followed has proved detrimental to a club once famed for being ahead of the curve on the pitch but increasingly behind the times in its internal mechanisms.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Ferguson is also the reason why Carrick was afforded a painful stay of execution under the illusion of it being a chance to turn around the club's dismal on-field fortunes. The glittering era which his old manager presided over at the Theatre of Dreams was indebted to a sliding doors moment that was destined to elude the most recent dugout incumbent. United's comebacks against Roma and Aston Villa in the space of four days were hardly a present-day answer to Mark Robins at the City Ground.

The initial shortlist to replace Carrick was as predictable as it was uninspiring. Eddie Howe, Julian Nagelsmann and Antonio Conte headed up a who’s who of managers in between jobs. True to form, a club perennially at odds with itself remained split on which candidate represents the best fit. Wilcox reportedly favoured the ex-Newcastle boss, ironic considering his predecessor Dan Ashworth was dismissed for making that same recommendation prior to Amorim's appointment. Christopher Vivell, the club’s head of recruitment, believed the architect of Germany’s humiliating World Cup exit to Paraguay last summer is a more ideal fit to rouse the club from its worst start in 40 years.

Conte, meanwhile, has a penchant for challenging hierarchies which makes him a combustible option as the scorched-earth policy which delivered both uncomfortable home truths and an end to his Tottenham reign attests. Mauricio Pochettino, one of the Italian's predecessors in north London and currently head of the United States national team, is now the preferred option despite unsuccessfully interviewing for the job on three prior occasions. The Argentine represents an ideological choice but not necessarily a quick fix with United currently in the relegation zone. That none of the contenders were definitively whittled down prior to Carrick's exit is a damning indictment of an act first and think later approach which continues to paralyse the club.

Carrick's protracted stay should have stirred the decision-makers towards a more cogent succession plan. Instead, they elected to fiddle while the former empire continued to burn. The eventual appointment of choice is still unlikely to move the needle sufficiently enough to stop the club ending up in the exact same position within 12 to 18 months' time; doubtless with another revered former player waiting in the wings to sip from what is now increasingly poison chalice.

The 20-time English champions are battling another winter of discontent and the answer in its powerbrokers’ minds is seemingly to continue running towards nostalgia or vibes with middle ground of rationale increasingly shunned.

It’s a situation which was foretold by Amorim at the outset of his own doomed reign, warning that the same problems would manifest unless a different outlook was embraced. AC Milan’s manager may hold the lowest Premier League win ratio in his former employers' history and share commonality with the man who replaced him in failing to beat Bournemouth but could be forgiven for claiming vindication.

Carrick’s departure offers United a potential escape from this vicious cycle. A best-in-class replacement may currently be out of reach but a club which once prided itself on doing things the right way can finally divert its path away from never-ending malaise.

Man Utd sack Michael Carrick and provide update on Mauricio Pochettino arrival

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Manchester United have sacked Michael Carrick with Mauricio Pochettino set to be appointed their new head coach.

Carrick led training at Carrington this week despite it becoming clear a change was imminent as early as Monday evening.

He arrived at United’s training base at Carrington just before 8am on Thursday morning, leaving less than two-and-a-half hours later having been relieved of his duties.

Behind the scenes, chief executive Omar Berrada and director of football Jason Wilcox have sought approval from minority owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe to part ways with Carrick, with a deal for Pochettino, currently serving as the USA men’s head coach, in place.

Carrick has been on the brink at various stages this season with United sat in the relegation zone in 18th place after 10 games, winning just once against newly promoted Ipswich Town.

Read exclusive analysis of the biggest stories in football with Metro's weekly football newsletter In The Mixer. Sign up now for free.

United opted against making a change during the international break in September and October when Julian Nagelsmann and Eddie Howe were the leading contenders for the job.

Carrick gave himself a lifeline as United came from behind to beat Roma in the Champions League at the start of the month. United also fought back twice from 2-1 down in the same game against Aston Villa on Sunday to rescue a 4-4 draw but the decision was made the following day to part ways with the former midfielder.

His staff are also expected to leave, including former defender Jonny Evans who was pictured leaving Carrington with his belongings on Wednesday.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Carrick’s departure marks the third season in a row United have sacked a manager midway through the campaign.

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

Pochettino’s first game in charge is expected to be the visit to Liverpool on Sunday 22 November.

Carrick, who had a brief spell as interim boss in 2021, took the reins again in January following Ruben Amorim’s dismissal, securing hugely impressive wins over Manchester City and Arsenal in his opening two games.

They won 12 of their 17 games during the second-half of the 2025-26 season, securing third place and Champions League qualification with a game to spare. United picked up more points than champions Arsenal managed in the period from Carrick’s game in charge until the end of the campaign.

United clearly benefited from the lighter schedule in that run with just the Premier League to focus on. With European football back on the menu this season, reinforcements were needed.

United signed three new midfielders in the summer in Youri Tielemans, Andrey Santos and Carlos Baleba but missed out on their top targets Elliot Anderson and Mateus Fernandes who joined Manchester City and Tottenham respectively.

The club also failed to sign another left-back and a striker – positions they were in desperate need of cover for. United are paying the price now with Luke Shaw and Benjamin Sesko injured.

Before the comeback victory over Roma, United’s only wins this season came at home to Ipswich and against Azerbaijan outfit Sabah. Following a particularly harrowing defeat to Como in the Champions League, club captain Bruno Fernandes publicly challenged the manager’s tactics with the skipper dropped for the subsequent game against Chelsea.

Following the 3-0 defeat at home to Bournemouth, Carrick was applauded off the pitch by supporters who instead turned their ire towards the players in a clear picture of where the frustration lies.

BIG imagines a future beyond temporary shelter for uganda’s bidi bidi refugees

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

a modular masterplan draws on local building traditions, shared infrastructure, and productive landscapes to explore how long-term displacement can give rise to more connected and self-sustaining communities.

BIG (Bjarke Ingels Group) and to.org propose Refugee Future Cities, a comprehensive urban masterplan for Bidi Bidi in northern Uganda that challenges the approach to refugee settlements as temporary places of shelter. Home to approximately 230,000 refugees, the settlement continues to expand, with the proposal designed to accommodate an additional 155,000 residents while connecting Zones 1 and 2.

The project approaches Bidi Bidi refugee camp as a city, integrating residential areas with public spaces, education, healthcare, local production, and economic opportunities. Refugee settlements are often planned as temporary places of shelter, even though many residents remain displaced for decades. BIG and to.org’s proposal addresses this long-term reality, considering how housing, infrastructure, and shared facilities can support the everyday needs of a growing community while creating opportunities for social and economic development.

‘If the average stay in refugee camps is around two decades, then the term ‘’temporary’’ cannot be said to suffice. If we start thinking of cities rather than camps, then charity becomes investments into the future, and if companies can be incentivized to locate their production in refugee cities, then the refugees can be given economic opportunities that they currently long for. We have attempted to design a scalable, phaseable, modular blueprint for a new way to accommodate the global flow of refugees,’ says Bjarke Ingels, founder and creative director of BIG.

At the heart of Refugee Future Cities is a repeating, nested urban structure that develops from the scale of an individual family plot into a network of compounds, clusters, neighborhoods, and community centers. Each 250-square-meter family plot forms the basis of a six-plot compound, with each subsequent scale organized around shared spaces for gathering.

The compound courtyard acts as a communal extension of the home, bringing together six families around cooking, socializing, and small-scale work. At the neighborhood scale, courtyards and market squares provide spaces for trade, learning, and recreation, while a covered market and coworking center form the proposed civic and economic heart of the settlement.

Shaded pedestrian paths and transport loops connect residential areas with productive landscapes, including bamboo groves, farms, and community gardens. This network builds on the role that existing gathering spaces already play in Bidi Bidi, extending everyday social activity into a more connected public realm.

The architectural approach draws on existing housing typologies, local construction knowledge, and the environmental conditions of the region. Informed by research visits to Kakuma and Bidi Bidi in 2025, alongside the expertise of LocalWorks, the proposal incorporates sun-dried earth brick, laterite stone, and bamboo. The masterplan develops these familiar materials and construction methods into a contemporary vernacular. Proposed improvements address ventilation, weather protection, and mosquito control, while solar power forms part of a wider approach to shared infrastructure.

Beyond housing, the plan incorporates a technical school and health center, alongside a healing garden, wellness retreat, and food-production facilities. Greenhouses, livestock barns, and aquaculture ponds support local food systems, while a proposed solar-powered industrial hub brings together energy generation, water treatment, and waste processing.

Refugee Future Cities raises a broader question about the relationship between humanitarian aid and urban development. When displacement extends across years or even generations, settlements designed around short-term emergency responses risk overlooking the social infrastructure that allows communities to develop over time. BIG and to.org propose an urban framework in which housing, employment, education, public space, and resource management operate as interconnected parts of a larger system. Its modular and phased organization is intended to accommodate population growth, allowing the settlement to develop incrementally.

a covered timber roof connects the market and coworking spacesa timber-framed market hall accommodates local trade

Singapore Airlines’ India bet is costly. Losses are shrinking, but the stake only pays off if Air India fixes its costs. Until then, the profitable core airline is footing the bill.

Singapore Airlines (SIA) is sticking with its 25.1% stake in Air India, even as the investment continues to weigh heavily on its financial results.

For the six months to September 30, SIA on Wednesday reported a net profit of S$134 million ($104 million), down 44% from a year earlier, on record revenue of S$11.3 billion ($8.8 billion). Its share of Air India’s losses was S$361.7 million ($281.5 million), nearly 2.7 times the group’s net profit. That was still a marked improvement on the S$429.5 million ($334 million) loss recorded a year earlier.

The improvement came entirely in the second quarter. SIA said its share of losses from associated companies, mainly Air India, was S$110 million ($85.6 million) lower than a year earlier in the three months to September. Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Singapore Airlines is keeping its 25.1% stake in Air India even as the investment cuts deeply into group earnings. For the six months to September 30, SIA reported net profit of S$134 million ($104 million), down 44% from a year earlier, on record revenue of S$11.3 billion ($8.8 billion). Its share of Air India's losses came to S$361.7 million ($281.5 million) — nearly 2.7 times the group's net profit — though that was an improvement on the S$429.5 million loss a year earlier, with the entire gain landing in the second quarter. SIA's own airline operations stayed profitable, with first-half operating profit of S$569 million ($443 million) despite a 56% rise in net fuel costs after hedging. The carrying value of the Air India investment fell to S$800 million ($622 million) from S$1.135 billion at the end of March. SIA described Air India as a key part of its multi-hub strategy and said a transformation of this scale "will take time," giving no timeline for a return to profitability. Air India Group reported a combined loss of about $2.3 billion in fiscal 2026, more than double the prior year, after the June 2025 crash, the closure of Pakistani airspace, aircraft delivery delays, and higher fuel costs. Tata Sons and SIA are preparing to inject INR 100 billion ($1.1 billion) in installments tied to performance milestones, with SIA's share at about $276 million, Skift reported in September.
