Michael Carrick is SACKED by Man United: Manager leaves training ground just 2½ hours after arriving - and now Mauricio Pochettino is ready to take over

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Published: 05:22 EST, 12 November 2026 | Updated: 07:34 EST, 12 November 2026

Michael Carrick has been sacked by Manchester United, with former Chelsea and Tottenham Hotspur boss Mauricio Pochettino waiting in the wings as his successor.

Just 173 days on from Carrick being given a two-year contract, having guided United to a third-placed finish and back into the Champions League as an interim, the decision to fire him was ratified at a crucial board meeting in Monaco on Wednesday.

Carrick arrived at United's training ground at 7.50am this morning and was dismissed. He left at 10.18am, less than two and a half hours later. He is the first Premier League manager to be sacked this season, followed hours later by bottom-of-the-table Crystal Palace boss Pierre Sage.

In a statement released at 10.37am, Carrick's sacking was confirmed. United's statement read: 'After a disappointing start to the season, the club has come to the extremely difficult conclusion that a change is necessary in order to achieve our ambitions.

'Michael is, and always will be, a Manchester United legend. Just as he did as a player, he has conducted himself with the highest standards of dedication and professionalism throughout his time back with the club. His leadership and achievements last season will always be fondly remembered.'

Sources close to United say the club's leadership believe their squad of players is far better than the performances and results under Carrick have indicated. They gave the under-fire manager a chance to turn things around against Chelsea, Roma and Aston Villa but ultimately felt the team were not on the right path.

Michael Carrick - pictured arriving at Carrington on Thursday morning - has been sacked after Manchester United's disastrous start to the season

Carrick was seen leaving the training ground less than two and a half hours later

USMNT boss Mauricio Pochettino is ready to take over as United's new manager

Minority owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe, along with the Glazer family, who dialled in virtually from the United States, chief executive Omar Berrada and director of football Jason Wilcox reached a verdict in the south of France yesterday, all while Carrick remained in limbo back in Manchester.

Carrick took charge of training on Tuesday morning after it had emerged that Berrada and Wilcox were heading to Monaco to recommend his sacking and did so again on Wednesday when it transpired United were closing in on an agreement to bring in Pochettino as his successor.

The current United States national team manager reached a verbal agreement with Man United to take over on Wednesday. United then made a formal approach to US Soccer shortly after, with the club having to trigger the 54-year-old Argentine's £7million release clause.

The final nail in Carrick’s coffin came in Sunday's dramatic 4-4 draw against Aston Villa, which saw United stuck in the relegation zone in 18th position.

The handling of Carrick’s departure has been particularly damning on Ineos and the shot-callers at United, not least given he was given no show of public support when he continued in post following an emergency board meeting after the 3-0 defeat to Bournemouth.

A defeat at Chelsea, in which Lisandro Martinez badly let his manager down by getting sent off, and a win over Roma to go with the draw against Villa, was not enough of a sign that it will turn around for the board to keep faith.

With Carrick's departure, it pushes the compensation paid out to coaching staff and executives over the last three years under minority owner Ratcliffe and Ineos close to £30million. Buying Pochettino out of his contract with the US national team will cost another £7m. Adding his large coaching staff will take millions more.

Matchgoing supporters will be sad to see Carrick go and made those feelings clear in recent games when he was loudly cheered at the end of matches, with the ire and frustration levelled against players and executives.

Optimism heading into the campaign was sky high given that no team in the Premier League performed better than Carrick's side between January and May.

But that level of success and dominance has long since evaporated after a shambolic start to the current campaign - and it has cost the 45-year-old his job just five months after the ink dried on a new deal.

It has been a season in which United won just three matches, one of which came against Azerbaijani minnows FK Sabah, and are yet to keep a clean sheet in the league all season.

A season in which club captain Bruno Fernandes publicly questioned his manager's tactics and Martinez eviscerated the hierarchy for hanging Carrick and the players out to dry. Fernandes was even dropped for the first time in five years for the Bournemouth humiliation.

Questions will continue to be asked of those upstairs, with Berrada and Wilcox now on to their third permanent manager this year alone and their fourth in their tenure having burned through Erik ten Hag, Ruben Amorim and Carrick since taking control of football operations in February 2024.

It means Omar Berrada and Jason Wilcox (pictured on Thursday morning) need a third manager this year as INEOS' running of the football club comes under scrutiny once more

Sunday's draw with Aston Villa proved to be the final nail in the coffin for the United boss

Gary Neville believes Michael Carrick will be 'disgusted' by his Manchester United players

Amorim lasted 14 months in the role, even in spite of embarrassing defeats to the likes of League Two Grimsby Town, but for Carrick it was a dream job that would run until early November and end in him being unceremoniously dumped at the training ground by Wilcox.

Carrick vowed after the Villa draw that he wouldn’t walk away and took on a spiky side when quizzed on his future by reporters. After a week of shame against the board, he has finally been put out of his misery.

Carrick leaves with a record of 14 wins - 12 of which came last season, prior to being made permanent manager - six draws and eight defeats, including exiting this season's Carabao Cup at the first hurdle against Brighton at Old Trafford.

Now it looks set to be Pochettino’s challenge to turn this massively underperforming squad around, something former United star Gary Neville believes is a mightily difficult task given the 'unprofessional' squad the new man will inherit.

'They've teased him, haven't they? They've conned [Carrick] for a few months. They have, they've conned him,' Neville said on his Sky Sports podcast recently.

'Honestly, he would never say this publicly because he's got class. I can say it for him though.

'He'll look at those players with disgust. Some of them, he'll be disgusted with some of those players.

'A few of them aren't that professional. I live in Manchester. Some of them aren't that professional. So I've got no time for them.

'There's a few of them that don't go to bed at the right time, don't eat the right things, don't think about Manchester United every single minute of every day, and aren't living and breathing this football club as they should be - and as professionals should.

'Why? Because it's gone to their heads. They are nothing, some of those players.

'He's not been able to get a tune out of them and they've turned on him. I know that because if Bruno has turned on him publicly, with that little statement, imagine what they're like behind the scenes.

'If that's your captain saying that, imagine what they're saying in the dressing room behind closed doors when no one's listening. That's horrific.'

Minnesota’s Voyageurs National Park Named 2026 Dark Sky Place of the Year

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Voyageurs National Park Dark Sky. (Photo: Daniel Dendler)

For people living beneath the artificial glow of city lights, a sky filled with thousands of stars can seem like something out of science fiction. According to a recent National Park Service report, 80 percent of Americans cannot see the Milky Way from their homes.

But at Minnesota’s Voyageurs National Park, a roughly five-hour drive from Minneapolis, some of the darkest skies in the country reveal the heavens in dazzling detail. And thanks to the park’s growing lineup of in-person and virtual programs, you don’t have to travel to the North Woods to experience the magic.

This week, Voyageurs received one of the highest honors in conservation: DarkSky International’s 2026 Dark Sky Place of the Year award. The annual award honors individuals and organizations dedicated to protecting nighttime environments.

“What makes this award so meaningful is that it isn’t about Voyageurs being a beautiful place to see the stars. We were certified as an International Dark Sky Park in 2020 for that,” Christina Hausman Rhode, executive director of Voyageurs Conservancy, its nonprofit partner of the park, told Outside.

Since earning its International Dark Sky Park certification in 2020, Voyageurs has introduced some 8,300 students to the wonders overhead through its Virtual Dark Sky Classroom, welcomed nearly 6,000 people to Park After Dark and other nighttime programs, and drawn more than 1,300 visitors to six annual Star Parties. Add to that roughly 40,000 students nationwide who participate in Boreal Stargazing Week each year, and it’s clear the park’s reach extends well beyond its 218,000-acre boundary.

This honor recognizes what has happened since, Rhode added: “A shared commitment by the National Park Service, Voyageurs Conservancy, and our gateway communities to protect these skies and share them with as many people as possible.“

Voyageurs also stands out because it’s about 40 percent water—covered by interconnected glacial lakes—a fact that adds to its stargazing prowess. Sweeping views are unobstructed by foothills, and its 30 calm lake surfaces act as mirrors reflecting the constellations and making the dark-sky experience all the more impressive. This is no Great Smoky Mountains National Park with its 12 million annual visitors either. With roughly 200,000 visitors each year, Voyageurs offers plenty of wide open space to take in the starscape.

“Voyageurs doesn’t just protect some of the darkest skies remaining in North America,” Ruskin Hartley, CEO and executive director of DarkSky International, told Outside via email. “It’s the people behind that protection.”

Hartley said that from the land managers and rangers to community partners, there’s a shared commitment at the park to preserve the night through responsible lighting, as well as to share the park’s wonders with the public.

The recognition comes at the end of a particularly challenging year for wilderness conservation in northern Minnesota. In April, Congress passed and President Donald Trump signed a resolution that reversed a 20-year mining ban covering roughly 350 square miles of Superior National Forest near the neighboring Boundary Waters Canoe Area Wilderness. The move reopened the door to proposed copper and nickel mining operations.

The threat to Voyageurs? Well, what happens in the Boundary Waters doesn’t necessarily stay in the Boundary Waters. The two protected landscapes are linked by the Rainy River watershed, a sprawling network of lakes, rivers, and wetlands. (Incidentally, the Boundary Waters and Voyageurs, along with Ontario’s Quetico Provincial Park, form one of the world’s largest dark-sky regions.)

“Copper mining from a proposed Twin Metals mine would absolutely impact Voyageurs,” says Libby London, communications director for the nonprofit Save the Boundary Waters.

Once pollution reaches the water, it can spread quickly for several hundred miles, explains Pete Marshall, communications director for Friends of the Boundary Waters Wilderness, another nonprofit organization that’s been advocating for the Boundary Waters area for 50 years. Governor Tim Walz signed an executive order in August to block the state from permitting copper mines in the watershed, but that order expires three months after Walz leaves office.

In July, lightning-sparked wildfires fueled by hot and windy conditions forced the rare closure of Boundary’s entire million-acre wilderness, upending summer travel plans and prompting evacuations. Out of an abundance of caution, Voyageurs officials banned campfires and charcoal grills for six weeks.

Against that backdrop, Voyageurs’ Dark Sky Place of the Year award is a welcome conservation victory.

Protecting the night sky at Voyageurs has required more than simply keeping the lights off, of course. The park has had to rethink how it uses light in the first place. Under a new lighting management plan, outdoor fixtures were replaced with dark-sky-friendly alternatives that direct light downward rather than into the sky. National Park Service staff also began regularly visiting five locations throughout the park to measure the darkness using a sky quality meter, a device that measures the brightness of the night sky. On a scale where 22.0 represents the darkest natural night sky, Voyageurs averages an impressive 21.45—meaning that when the sun goes down, the stars really do come out.

And while the award puts Voyageurs in the spotlight, the park’s greatest achievement may be what happens when the lights stay out. For Rhode and the Voyageurs Conservancy, the hope is that the recognition inspires others to look up—and take action.

New Research Finds That Slower Running May Be Good for Your Brain. A Neuroscientist Weighs In.

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

It’s easy, as a runner, to slip into an all-or-nothing mindset. There may have been times when you’ve caught yourself thinking, “What’s the point of going on a run if I can’t commit to at least 3 miles?” or “If I’m going to run this slow, I might as well walk—or skip it altogether.”

The reality is that something is almost always better than nothing, and running, even very slowly for a short time, can boost your brain and mood.

In Aug. 2026, researchers in Japan set out to discover if very light intensity running would affect the brain, especially the parts that are involved with executive functioning. They weren’t curious about the effect of very light intensity running over time; rather, they wanted to know if a single bout of light intensity running—just one workout—could yield noteworthy brain benefits.

For their study, researchers recruited 24 young and healthy adults, including 13 males and 11 females, with the average ages of 23.38 and 20.55, respectively.

The main experiment included a control (CON) day and a very slow running (SRUN) day, with every participant engaging in both days. For the CON session, participants sat on a chair on an unmoving treadmill for ten minutes.

Before the SRUN session, maximal oxygen uptake (VO2peak) was measured for each participant to determine a very slow run speed that would elicit 35 percent VO2peak. Then, participants ran on the treadmill for ten minutes at their predetermined very slow speeds.

Males averaged 3.39 mph, and females averaged 2.40 mph. While these are easily walking speeds, the researchers observed that participants were able to maintain running form.

Before and immediately after each session, participants completed the following: The Color-Word Stroop task (CWST) to measure inhibitory control and the Two-Dimensional Mood Scale (TDMS) to measure pleasurable moods.

For the CWST task, participants were shown two rows of letters on a monitor and had to determine (by pressing “yes” or “no”) if the color of the letters in the top row matched the color name written in the bottom row.

There were three conditions: Neutral, Congruent, or Incongruent. For the neutral condition, Xs were written on the top row in a particular color, and the name of that color was written in black on the bottom row.

For the congruent condition, the word “red” was written in red in the top row and, in the bottom row, the word red was written in black. The incongruent condition was included to introduce cognitive interference. In that condition, the word in the top row did not match the colors of the letters, but the bottom row still had the name of the upper row letters’ colors written in black.

For each trial, in which either the neutral, congruent, or incongruent condition was displayed, participants would, as quickly as possible, press either “yes” or “no” to report whether or not the color of the letters in the top row matched the color row in the bottom row.

Participants were given three practice sessions to make sure they understood how to perform the task.

So, what does CWST measure? It directly measures something called Stroop interference, which is the difference in reaction time between the incongruent and neutral conditions.

Essentially, Stroop interference is a way to assess executive function, with lower values indicating higher executive functioning. In this study, Stroop interference was lower after participants ran versus the seated control setting.

The TDMS assessed participants’ arousal and pleasure states. Participants were asked to rate the following eight descriptors on a scale from 0 (“I’m not experiencing this at all”) to 5 (“I’m currently feeling this way quite extremely”): energetic, lively, lethargic, listless, relaxed, calm, irritated, and nervous.

The results indicated that immediately following the short and very slow run, participants reported feeling more energized and content than after the control setting.

But what effect does ten minutes of slow running have on brain activity? To examine this, researchers used a non-invasive brain imaging technique called functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS). fNIRS measures changes in hemoglobin throughout the brain’s cortex, which corresponds with cortical activity.

For this study, participants wore an fNIRS cap with electrodes while they performed the CWST task, five minutes after running. As hypothesized, very slow running can generate increased activity in multiple subregions of the prefrontal cortex—namely, the left dorsolateral prefrontal cortex, the left ventrolateral prefrontal cortex, and the right and left frontopolar areas.

Activation of these brain regions keeps us sharp and functioning well day to day; the prefrontal cortex is involved with executive functions such as managing competing goals, decision-making, reasoning, and self-control.

There was one more variable that the researchers were curious about: head acceleration. Even among smooth runners that glide across the ground, the head moves vertically with each step.

A previous study suggested that this mechanical force may underlie some of the psychological benefits of running by impacting neurotransmitter activity. So, the researchers of the current study sought to explore that more. They found that even the small head acceleration that occurs during very slow running was positively correlated with the amount of post-run pleasure the participants reported.

As is the case with every study, there were a few limitations. First, electrodes (such as on the fNIRS device) can pick up signals other than neural activation, such as skin blood flow. The researchers minimized this potential by carefully designing their protocol and checking if the data made sense. Second, head acceleration is obviously different between seated controls and running conditions, so a third, walking group would enhance future experiments.

Here’s the main takeaway: a single, ten-minute run at a very slow, almost walking pace may be enough to boost short-term cognitive performance and elevate your mood.

Consider what this means if you’re returning to running after an injury or illness. Or, perhaps you’re in a new season of life, and you can’t, realistically, carve out more than a few minutes to move your body. This study encourages all of us to keep running, no matter how fast or far we go.

A.I. Weapons Need Data for Their Training. Ukraine Has a Lot to Offer.

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The Ukrainian government has opened up a trove of five million battlefield videos to defense companies at home and abroad.

The forest, seen from a drone, appeared as a vast, monotonous landscape of black tree trunks, shadows and patches of snow.

To the human eye, it appeared deserted. But to an artificial intelligence system looking for people, it did not. The system put a red box around a tiny human form, probably a soldier lurking in the trees.

Behind this feat lay vast volumes of data. Just as ChatGPT and other A.I. chatbots must be exposed to how humans write, military A.I. systems must be exposed to how humans fight. In Ukraine, this happens through a huge trove of battlefield videos, audio and text data collected over nearly five years.

The war database is being used by about 30 Ukrainian defense companies to develop A.I. weapons. Ukraine is now opening this repository, which it calls Avenger Labs, to firms in allied countries, hoping to deepen security partnerships.

Ukraine is providing the data at no charge, but companies must give the Ukrainian military licenses to any software they develop. Under the first agreement to be reached, 12 British defense companies will gain access. Ukraine is in talks with other countries.

While there is no shortage of Western companies developing autonomous weapons, there is a shortage of the data needed to train them. Ukraine can offer more of it than any other potential partner.

The Generational Housing Divide: Millennials Had It Better Than Baby Boomers–Until the Pandemic Changed the Math

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

If millennials hadn’t spent so much money on avocado toast, they’d all own their dream home by now–right? Wrong. Just over half of millennials own their home. For the rest, housing costs have soared so much in recent years that skipping the avocado toast in their 20s wouldn’t have made a dent.

The story isn’t about constant struggle, though–it’s about timing. Elder millennials who bought a home in their early 30s had it better than baby boomers did at the same age: Historically low mortgage rates and higher household incomes meant those millennials spent about 27% of their income on housing at age 30, compared with 36% for boomers. Then the pandemic changed the math. Mortgage rates jumped from around 3% to nearly 7%, and home prices surged. Since rates rose above 6% in 2022, elder millennials have spent up to 35% of their income on monthly mortgage payments, compared with roughly 25% for boomers at the same age–even though millennials earn more, adjusted for inflation.

Boomers might point out that mortgage rates were much higher in their day. They’d be right: Rates peaked at over 16% in 1981 and stayed in double digits for much of the decade. But prices were lower. In the 1980s, when many boomers were in their 30s, the typical U.S. home sold for about $235,000 to $265,000 in today’s dollars. When elder millennials reached the same age–prime homebuying age–in the 2010s and 2020s, typical prices ranged from about $275,000 to $455,000.

Key takeaway: Buying at the wrong time was painful for both generations, but boomers had a better chance to recover. Falling rates throughout the late 1980s, 1990s and early 2000s gave them many opportunities to refinance into lower payments. Millennials who bought before 2022 secured historically low mortgages; those who bought later—or never managed to buy—face a much less forgiving market. Of course, it’s possible the market could become more forgiving for millennials over time; rates may eventually fall significantly enough that many millennials have the opportunity to refinance into lower payments.

This is based on an analysis of Redfin and U.S. Census data. A few notes to help contextualize the numbers: All dollar figures are adjusted for inflation. Much of the data illustrates each generation’s typical experience at a given age, combining everyone in that generation who has reached that age, whenever that happened for them. Because millennials are still aging into their 40s, for example, our 40-to-44 figures only capture the oldest millennials, who hit those ages between 2021 and 2025. All boomers have been through that age range, so their figures include the whole generation. Please see the end of this report for a detailed methodology.

In their early 30s, elder millennials who bought a home spent a smaller share of their income on housing than boomers did at the same age, despite much higher home prices. Imagine a median-earning millennial born in 1984 who bought a home at age 30. They would have spent around 27% of their income to purchase the typical home, within the “don’t spend more than 30% of your income on housing costs” rule of thumb.

Two factors helped: cheap borrowing and higher household incomes. Millennials benefited from roughly a decade of historically low mortgage rates; they fell below 3% in 2021. Millennials also tend to have higher household incomes than boomers did at the same age. At age 37, the typical elder millennial household earned an average of $100,000, compared with $88,000 when boomers were that age. The discrepancy is partly because two-income households are more common today. About 78% of women participate in the U.S. workforce, up from roughly 60% in 1980. It’s also worth noting that millennials typically work similar hours for more money than baby boomers at the same life stage.

The millennial advantage didn’t last. Mortgage rates jumped from around 3% in late 2021 to nearly 7% in late 2022, after home prices had surged about 40% nationwide during the pandemic homebuying frenzy. Rising rates often cool prices, but this time, prices didn’t fall. That mix of high prices and mortgage rates that had doubled from their pandemic-era low meant Americans had to spend a much higher share of their income to buy a home. Imagine that same millennial, born in 1984: If they bought a home at age 40 instead of 30, their housing costs shot up to about 35% of their income.

Rising prices and rates also priced many Americans completely out of the housing market, which is why elder millennials have a lower homeownership rate than baby boomers at the same age.

“For millennials who haven’t yet bought a home, affordability is a challenge. But it’s important to remember that you’re not trying to climb Mount Everest, you’re just trying to get back to 2018 or 2019, which was the last time the average homebuyer was spending just about 30% of their income on housing,” said Chen Zhao, Redfin’s head of economic research. “Before the pandemic, younger generations actually had it easier than the ones before them. But then home prices surged during the pandemic homebuying frenzy and mortgage rates shot up, while incomes kept growing at a normal pace–and the advantage flipped. If you bought in 2020 or 2021, you’re coming out ahead, but those years were the exception, not the rule. One piece of hope for younger Americans who haven’t yet bought a home: Home prices are already falling in parts of the U.S. and they’re softening in others, which means we could be headed back to 2018 or 2019 levels sooner rather than later.”

Millennials are also contending with other major expenses; childcare costs are squeezing them particularly hard. A recent Redfin analysis found that housing and childcare consume more than half of the typical American’s household income. Student loans are another big expense; millennials have significantly more student loan debt than boomers did at the same age.

Adult Gen Zers, who are in their twenties, are tracking behind millennials at the same stage of life. Gen Zers have spent anywhere from 40% of 60% of their income to buy the typical home since they’ve reached homebuying age. That’s partly because they’re still young enough that they haven’t yet hit their earnings peak, so their current income is lower than older generations, and partly because housing costs are higher than in the past. But as Gen Zers move through their careers, their earnings are likely to grow substantially, as they have for every generation before them. Combined with a gradual normalization in housing costs, homeownership should become increasingly attainable over time.

Baby boomers faced their own affordability crisis when they were young, in the 1980s. In their early 20s, the typical boomer household who bought a home would have spent more than 60% of its income on housing costs, reflecting the era’s runaway inflation and double-digit mortgage rates.

But their housing burden fell quickly as they aged, dropping to around 40% of income by their late 20s and 26% by their late 30s. Baby boomers’ homeownership rate steadily rose from about half in 1985 to over 70% by the late 1990s, and it kept climbing after that. By their 40s and 50s, the share of homebuyers’ income spent on housing had settled into the low-to-mid 20% range, lower than millennials at the same point in their life.

Much of that relief came from the same force that had hurt them initially—mortgage rates—as rates gradually declined through the 1980s and 1990s, letting many boomers refinance into far more manageable payments even as home prices kept climbing.

Millennials haven’t had the same opportunity. Those who bought before 2022 locked in historically low rates, while those who bought later faced a costly combination of high prices and climbing rates. Would-be buyers who couldn’t afford that combination were left out altogether.

“The housing market has historically been better for baby boomers than younger generations because lower home prices and higher rates are preferable to higher home prices and lower rates,” Zhao said. “You can refinance your rate into a lower housing payment, and that’s exactly what boomers did: They refinanced for up to 40 years as rates kept falling. Then home prices soared, which is why a lot of boomers are rich in real estate.”

That dynamic shows up clearly when you ask people directly. In a recent Redfin survey, more than three-quarters (76%) of baby boomers homeowners said they can easily afford their payment, while the rest struggle. Roughly half (53%) of millennial homeowners can easily afford their payment, and the other half struggle.

The same generational squeeze shows up in the reasons people are staying put in their current home. Among people unlikely to move soon, 43% of millennials cited high home prices as a reason, compared with 31% of boomers. Three in 10 (30%) millennials pointed to high mortgage rates, versus just 18% of boomers, likely because many baby boomers have paid off their mortgage and built up substantial equity. This is from a Redfin survey conducted by Ipsos in May 2026, fielded to 4,000 U.S. residents.

Consider two hypothetical couples. Alex and Jordan are millennials born in 1984 and live on two incomes, a common setup for millennial households. Pat and Chris are baby boomers born in 1956 and got by on one, more typical for their generation. The numbers below show what it would have cost each couple to buy a home to buy a home in their early 30s and their late 30s.

This analysis uses income data from 1976 to 2025 from the U.S. Census Bureau’s Current Population Survey Annual Social and Economic Supplement (CPS ASEC), accessed through IPUMS. The sample includes household heads aged 19 and older, and results are weighted using CPS household weights.

Each household is assigned to a generation based on the age of the household head in the survey year.

Home prices. We start with the 2025 median sale price of U.S. single-family homes from Redfin data ($456,409). Prices for earlier years are estimated by scaling that 2025 price back using the S&P Cotality Case-Shiller U.S. National Home Price Index from 1987 onward and the FHFA All-Transactions House Price Index for years before 1987. Both indexes are averaged by year.

Monthly housing payment. For each year, we calculate the monthly payment to buy a median-priced home. It covers principal, interest, property taxes and homeowners insurance. We assume:

Income and inflation. Household income is total household income as reported in the CPS ASEC. All income and payment figures are converted to 2025 dollars using the Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U).

Share of income spent on housing. For each generation and age, we calculate the median monthly housing payment and the median household income, and divide the payment by one-twelfth of the income.

How to read the results by age.  Much of the data in this report shows each generation’s typical experience at a given age. Because a generation spans many birth years, people born at the start of that generation turn, say, 40 in a different calendar year than people born at the end of it. For this analysis, each age point combines averages for everyone from that generation who has reached that age, in whatever year that actually was for them. For instance, millennials (born 1981 to 1996) have only had the oldest members of their generation—those born in the early-to-mid 1980s—reach their 40s so far. So when we show what the typical 40-to-44-year-old millennial paid for housing, we’re only capturing people who hit that age between 2021 and 2025. Younger millennials haven’t gotten there yet. Baby boomers (born 1946 to 1964) are fully through that age range, their 40-to-44 data point include averages from all boomers.

As a data journalist at Redfin, Dana Anderson writes about the numbers behind real estate trends. Redfin is a full-service real estate brokerage that uses modern technology to make clients smarter and faster. For more information about working with a Redfin real estate agent to buy or sell a home, visit our Why Redfin page.

Yingqi is a Senior Economist at Redfin. He studies housing market trends and explains what’s driving them for a broad audience. He previously led research on responsible AI at Freddie Mac, and also worked at Meta and the JPMorgan Chase Institute. Yingqi holds a Ph.D. in economics from Georgetown University, and his research is published in the American Economic Journal: Microeconomics and the Journal of Real Estate Finance and Economics.

Szu Yu Chen is a senior data visualization designer and developer who creates interactive graphics and data-driven experiences. Previously, she worked as a graphics reporter at The Washington Post and the Los Angeles Times.

How to Use Ornamental Edibles for Eye-Catching Containers

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

If you’re looking past vegetables, fruits, and herbs because you think they only belong in the kitchen garden, you’re missing a whole range of interesting, versatile possibilities for creating exciting ornamental containers. Edible plants can bring striking foliage, flowers, fruit, and structure to a container, even when producing a harvest isn’t the primary goal.

As a designer, I create vignettes in my gardens that engage the senses. It’s no different when composing a container or a grouping of containers. For now, set aside flavor and look at their other sensory qualities: visual appeal, fragrance, texture, and even the sounds of foliage and seed heads moving in the wind.

Read More: Best Sensory Garden Plants for Sound, Touch, Taste & Smell

Successful container design balances aesthetics with the growing needs of individual plants. Before exploring specific edimentals—plants valued for both their edible and ornamental qualities—consider a few practical guidelines.

Growing vegetables in containers demands certain conditions if you want a good harvest. But when appearance takes priority over production, you can make different choices. For instance, allowing lettuce and other leafy greens to bolt can introduce interesting flower stalks and seed heads, even though the leaves may become bitter and less desirable for eating.

Containers give you greater control over growing conditions, but plants still need the right light, moisture, and soil. Choose edible varieties that tend to be compact, dwarf, bush, or well-behaved vines or climbers.

It’s helpful to choose the largest container your budget and space will allow. Larger pots generally provide more room for roots and retain moisture longer, while smaller containers dry out more quickly and may require more frequent watering and feeding. Mixing container sizes also adds visual interest to a display. Whatever the size, make sure the container has adequate drainage.

One advantage of container gardening is the ability to tailor the soil to your plants’ needs. Start with a quality potting mix and choose plants with similar preferences for drainage, moisture, and soil acidity. This flexibility also lets you grow plants that might struggle in your native garden soil.

Because container plants depend on a limited volume of soil, they may need supplemental fertilizer as nutrients become depleted. Think about what you’re trying to encourage: Excess nitrogen, for example, can promote leafy growth at the expense of flowers or fruit. Choose a fertilizer appropriate for your plants, follow label directions, and consult your local nursery or Extension service for specific recommendations.

Mulch helps regulate soil temperature and retain moisture, even in containers. For open compositions where the soil surface is visible, use decorative materials such as small pine cones, shells, buckwheat hulls, or gravel. Leaf mold and finely shredded bark work well, too. Apply a shallow layer that allows water to pass through, and keep mulch away from plant crowns and stems.

Use what you have and think about growing your own. Many vegetables are quite easy to start from seed. Growing your own is both economical and a way to reduce the need for purchased transplants, especially when you use peat-free soil mixes. Choosing peat-free mixes can also help reduce demand for peat extracted from carbon-storing wetlands. Collect rainwater where permitted for an additional irrigation source. Avoid using untreated household gray water on edible crops. Incorporating perennials also extends the life of your displays. They can be combined with seasonal plants or relocated elsewhere in the garden when a container needs refreshing.

The ever-popular Thriller-Filler-Spiller container method holds its ground because of its snappy moniker and reliable formula. In this method, the thriller is the dramatic focal point; the filler is a bushy, mid-level plant or plants; and the spiller—wait for it—spills over the edge of the container. It’s an easy enough recipe that most people can create something attractive, but truthfully, many fail to think much about what they are combining, which results in the big-box special: a nondescript spiky grass towering over a showy fuchsia, with invasive creeping Jenny (Lysimachia nummularia, Zones 3–9) trailing out.

We can do better. While perhaps less catchy, think about composing your container much like you might a landscape. Consider structure (the framework); form and habit (the shape); spatial orientation (how it reads visually—does the plant have a horizontal, vertical, or some other orientation?); visual texture (is the foliage fine, coarse, or medium?); and color (is it intense, receding, or something else?). Go beyond appearance and consider the full range of sensory experience: how plants move in the wind and what that sounds like, what they smell like, and how they feel when brushed up against.

Not every plant or combination will hit all the notes, so don’t stress. Focus on a few complementary qualities. Combine the plants in your container to create contrasts or enhance similarities. Look at leaf, bloom, and seed head form. The idea is to create layers—low, mid, and upper—and aim for three to five plants that play off each other’s traits within the container.

Are you trying to create something formal, informal, transitional, organic, or eclectic? Knowing how you want your container to read to the viewer can help you make choices about which edible plants may be a better addition to the mix.

When composing your containers, don’t forget to look beyond edimentals. Often the best containers mix edible plants with purely ornamental plants, such as leafy or flowering perennials and small shrubs. The juxtaposition of disparate plants usually determines a container’s success—different textures, colors, heights, and forms.

While incorporating disparate elements creates visual interest, it’s also important to give the eye somewhere to rest. A collection of pots filled with different plant combinations can feel jarring without repetition or quieter areas to balance the display.

A repeat may be exactly what it sounds like: using the same plant or using the same color, form, or texture across pots. Areas to pause are pots that may contain one uniform element—such as like plants, colors, or containers.

Another approach is to view the containers as individual components. Rather than composing containers with several different kinds of plants (including vegetables, of course), try grouping containers that each have one type of plant. This can be a powerful display akin to naturalistic block-style planting design.

Sometimes a container needs more. Perhaps it needs some staking for a vine, or simply a bit of additional texture. Incorporate natural materials such as pruned branches (dry or living), fallen branches covered in lichen, wild berry clusters, pine cones, grape or hop vines, dried elements such as grasses, or even small stumps.

And don’t neglect the value of seed heads—just because that kale bloom has now passed doesn’t necessarily mean you want to remove the plant; wait to see those prolific little yellow pods. And a radish gone to seed showcases its most redeeming feature: crunchy seed clusters that rattle in the wind!

By definition, a container is simply a receptacle for holding goods or objects, so feel free to think beyond the pots you find at the nursery. Salvaged and vintage items work well combined with more traditional terra-cotta pots. Take a high-low approach and use found items from your own home along with a special planter from a boutique garden center.

Don’t forget these irreverent options can also be repurposed in unexpected applications—that antique copper colander can go on to lead a new life as a hanging basket. Just make sure repurposed containers provide adequate drainage and are suitable for growing food if you plan to harvest from them.

One of the biggest benefits of working with containers is the opportunity to modify them by adding or removing plants to capture the best seasonal foliage. While many edibles are annuals that end their season late in the year, others produce seed heads or even a second flush after cutback.

Some common edible members of the carrot family (Apiaceae), including carrots (Daucus carota, biennial); parsnips (Pastinaca sativa, biennial); parsley (Petroselinum crispum, biennial); and celery and celeriac (Apium graveolens, biennial), produce flowers in their second growing season. Their delicate foliage and umbrella-shaped flower clusters add texture and attract pollinators. Deep-rooted crops such as carrots and parsnips need sufficiently deep containers, particularly if you plan to leave them in place for a second season.

Osage orange (Maclura pomifera, Zones 4–9) isn’t a citrus fruit at all. This North American native tree produces large, greenish yellow, orange-like fruits with a distinctive, bumpy surface. Although the fruits aren’t considered edible, their unusual appearance and citrus-like fragrance make them wonderful decorative accessories for autumn container displays.

Another lesser-known edible is a well-known perennial: ostrich fern (Matteuccia struthiopteris, Zones 2–8). Its tightly coiled young fronds, known as fiddleheads, are harvested as a spring delicacy before unfurling into the familiar feathery foliage.

Lady fern (Athyrium filix-femina, Zones 3–8) offers similarly beautiful emerging fronds for ornamental containers, but it should not be treated as an interchangeable edible. Many ferns contain potentially harmful compounds, so accurate identification is essential. Only properly identified ostrich fern fiddleheads should be eaten, and they must be thoroughly cooked. For more information, see the University of Maine Cooperative Extension’s guide to fiddlehead safety. Of course, you can simply enjoy their fascinating emerging foliage up close in a container.

Vegetables that you may not have thought about embracing in your ornamental containers include okra (Abelmoschus esculentus, annual), with its large pods and hibiscus-like flowers; artichokes, such as ‘Purple Italian Globe’ (Cynara cardunculus var. scolymus ‘Purple Italian Globe’, Zones 7–10), with dramatic silvery foliage and purple flower heads (use your largest container); cucamelon (Melothria scabra, annual), also called mouse melon, which resembles a tiny watermelon on a prolific climbing vine; and leafy greens of all sorts. Look out for the striking edible red-veined dock (Rumex sanguineus, Zones 5–8), with the unpalatable common name, bloody dock—it’s a stunner. Its green leaves are marked with vivid red veins, and the young foliage is edible in moderation, although it contains oxalic acid.

More common edibles with uncommon traits include delicate currant tomatoes such as ‘Sweet Pea’ (Solanum pimpinellifolium ‘Sweet Pea’, annual) or compact tomatoes such as ‘Tiny Tim’ (Solanum lycopersicum ‘Tiny Tim’, annual).

Alpine strawberries (Fragaria vesca, Zones 4–9) come in a range of colors from white and pale yellow to pink and red, depending on the selection. These diminutive berries add character and spill over the edge of containers.

Peppers such as the teardrop ‘Biquinho Red’ (Capsicum chinense ‘Biquinho Red’, annual) are eye-catching and tolerant of many conditions. Compact blueberry (Vaccinium cvs., Zones 3–8) foliage glows red in the autumn, acting as a foil for its partners.

And there are so. Many. More. Whatever you’re interested in, you can find an edimental that will fill that niche.

Edible plants offer a broader palette that many gardeners and designers overlook—choosing them for containers broadens your range of plant materials and, most importantly, creates opportunities for delight and intrigue when used in unexpected ways.

They also make it possible to garden in small spaces or places where in-ground planting isn’t practical. Containers can be moved to improve growing conditions, rearranged to create a new focal point, or grouped to screen an unwanted view. They also let you accommodate plants that might struggle in your native soil or spread too aggressively in garden beds, such as mint (Mentha cvs., Zones 3–9) or sunchokes (Helianthus tuberosus, Zones 3–9). Just be sure to give vigorous plants enough room to grow.

In the eastern United States, ornamental kales and cabbages, paired with mums, are a common sight this time of year. While there is nothing inherently wrong with them, now you know you have more edimental options, so why not experiment with a wider range of edible plants? From colorful leafy greens to fruiting vines and impressive seed heads, there’s no shortage of possibilities for creating containers that look as interesting as the plants themselves.

Heather Tuckman is a garden designer and owner of Resilient Ground Design in southeastern Pennsylvania. She combines her background in horticulture, garden design, and psychology to create beautiful, ecologically thoughtful landscapes.

West Ham Target Giant Rangers Striker As Koppen Looks To Scotland

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

West Ham United have been linked with a January move for Rangers striker Ryan Naderi, although the Scottish club are reportedly reluctant to sell their 6ft 4in centre-forward. According to TEAMtalk journalist Fraser Fletcher, the Hammers and Wolves are among several clubs monitoring the 23-year-old.

Naderi has registered 14 goal contributions in 17 appearances this season, attracting interest from England and Germany. However, Rangers are understood to be wary of entertaining a January sale as they chase the Scottish Premiership title.

The link is particularly interesting given West Ham’s search for a replacement for Taty Castellanos. As Claret & Hugh exclusively revealed yesterday, Nils Koppen has already approached a France based striker ahead of the January transfer window, while the club would consider offers for the Argentine.

To see West Ham linked with a striker from Rangers should really come as no surprise.

After all, the Hammers have recruited several senior executives with connections to Ibrox, including Koppen and new chief financial officer James Taylor, who previously worked together at Rangers alongside acting chief executive Karim Virani.

Given those relationships, it stands to reason that Koppen would have a particularly close eye on developments at his former club.

In fact, it’s not just the blue half of Glasgow where Koppen appears to have his finger on the pulse. West Ham broke the Championship transfer record during the summer by bringing Arne Engels to east London from Celtic.

Koppen worked at Rangers as Director of Football

At 6ft 4in, Naderi certainly offers the physical presence of a traditional centre-forward. The German-born Czech international joined Rangers from Hansa Rostock earlier this year and has made an impressive start to life in Scottish football.

Whether he is the right striker for Scott Parker remains to be seen, but the reported interest makes sense given West Ham’s need to strengthen their attacking options.

I very much doubt this is about replacing Joël Piroe, who remains part of Parker’s plans. The more pressing issue is Castellanos, with West Ham already exploring the possibility of moving on the £27 million striker.

Given Koppen’s connections in Glasgow and West Ham’s search for another centre-forward, this is one transfer link that doesn’t require too much imagination to seem believable.

With over a decade of professional sports media experience, he is a frequent West Ham United analyst and contributor for Sky Sports News, BBC Football Focus, and BBC Radio 5 Live. He previously served as an official video and content creator for West Ham United FC during the club's tenure at Upton Park and featured in the official documentary commemorating the historic final game at the Boleyn Ground.

A lifelong Hammer since the legendary 10-0 victory over Bury, when he isn't providing daily match analysis on the Hammers Chat YouTube Channel or broadcasting on X (@GONZObignose), he can be found walking his dog and restoring classic pinball machines.

Justin Bieber's Instagram Photo Has Us Wanting Owls in Our Gardens – Here's How to Attract Them

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

5 Easy Fall Wins to Help Your Backyard Birds Survive Winter – How to Beat the Big Chill and Ensure More Visits

Want To Befriend a Crow? Here's How To Attract These Clever Birds To Your Yard and Earn Their Trust

The 2-Minute Lard Trick That Helps Smaller Songbirds Get Their Fair Share of Food in Fall and Winter

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

What a hoot! If Bieber's garden visitor has left you wishing for an owl of your own, here's how to make your yard more inviting.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Baby, baby, baby, oh! Justin Bieber might not be a name we'd usually associate with gardening, but the singer's latest Instagram photo dump has certainly given us food for thought.

Among the snapshots was a glimpse of what looks like a rather dreamy outdoor living space, complete with cozy terrace sofas, an outdoor fireplace and even an outdoor TV screen. But it was another, slightly blurrier photograph that caught this writer's attention: an owl, seemingly a great horned owl (aka the birth month owl for January babies everywhere!), paying a visit to his garden.

A post shared by Justin Bieber (@lilbieber)

Naturally, this isn't Bieber's first foray into the world of owl appreciation. The singer has had an owl tattoo on his forearm since 2012, with the bird representing wisdom – so it's easy to assume that this latest garden visitor was a particularly welcome sight for him.

Now, Justin Bieber certainly isn't alone in his fascination with these magnificent birds of prey. Whether it's their haunting calls, striking eyes or wonderfully mysterious reputation, there's something rather special about spotting an owl close to home.

So, if Bieber's feathered visitor has left you wondering how to welcome one of these beautiful birds into your own backyard, the good news is that there are a few things you can do to make your yard more owl-friendly.

One of the best ways to encourage owls into your yard à la Justin Bieber is to give them somewhere safe to nest. Depending on the species in your area, then, installing an owl box could provide just the inviting home they're looking for.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

The trick is to figure out which owls are visiting before you buy (or DIY) your nesting box; eastern screech owls, for example, are cavity nesters and will readily use suitably sized nesting boxes. Great horned owls, on the flipside, typically use open nests rather than enclosed boxes.

Once you've researched the requirements of your local owl species, all that's left to do is position your new nesting box high up in a mature tree, somewhere quiet and away from too much human activity.

A perfectly manicured lawn might look lovely, but a slightly wilder yard? Why, that could be much more appealing to visiting owls, as tese birds of prey need somewhere to hunt. (A healthy population of small mammals, such as mice and voles, can provide an important food source).

Try leaving a patch of grass a little longer, planting native grasses and wildflowers, and preserving mature trees wherever possible. Trees offer useful perching and roosting spots (something like the ‘Temple of Bloom’ Seven-Son from Fast Growing Treescould prove just as appealing to nesting hummingbirds as it does owls), while natural vegetation can help support the small mammals that owls feed on.

Don't worry; you really don't need to let the whole yard run wild (as Justin Bieber's near-immaculate garden proves) – even a small corner can help support local wildlife.

Owls are particularly active around dusk and (duh!) during the night, so creating a peaceful and naturally dark environment can help make your yard more welcoming. Consider switching off unnecessary outdoor lights, using motion-sensor lighting where needed, and avoiding bright spotlights that illuminate trees and potential nesting areas throughout the night.

Basically, invest in the kind of garden lighting that reduces light pollution as a rule, and keep it off at night if you want to do your bit. And try, too, keeping noise and disturbance to a minimum around trees and nesting sites.

If you want an owl to visit your garden just as it did Justin Bieber's yard, then it's also important to think carefully about how you eliminate rats.

Rodenticides (rat and mouse poisons) can be dangerous to owls, which may become poisoned after eating contaminated critters. If you want to encourage these beautiful birds into your yard, avoid using rodent poisons, then, and instead opt for safer, more owl-friendly approaches to pest management instead.

If you, like Justin Bieber, are lucky enough to spot an owl in your garden, take care to admire it from a distance rather than approaching or shining a light towards it (and consider investing in a Solar Trail Camera like this one to get your photos). After all, the idea isn't to persuade an owl to become a regular garden guest at any cost, but to create an outdoor space where local wildlife can thrive.

And while there's no guarantee that making these changes will result in an owl visit, you might just find that your garden becomes a little more appealing to some of nature's most fascinating nighttime visitors.

Kayleigh is an enthusiastic (sometimes too enthusiastic!) gardener and has worked in media for over a decade. She previously served as digital editor at Stylist magazine, and has written extensively for Ideal Home, Woman & Home, Homes & Gardens, and a handful of other titles. Kayleigh is passionate about wildlife-friendly gardening, and recently cancelled her weekend plans to build a mini pond when her toddler found a frog living in their water barrel. As such, her garden – designed around the stunning magnolia tree at its centre – is filled to the brim with pollinator-friendly blooms, homemade bird feeders, and old logs for insects to nest in.

'Slave payments' and 'expensive and exclusive shoe' discovered on 17th-century Danish ship that sank after deadly mutiny

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Archaeologists have discovered the shoe of the first governor of the Danish West Indies, recovered from a 17th-century ship that was wrecked after one of the most violent mutinies in Denmark’s history.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Maritime archaeologists investigating a 17th-century shipwreck off the coast of Sweden have discovered a fancy shoe and two metal rings used as currency in the transatlantic slave trade. The items were lost during a mutiny on a slave ship in 1683, during which the governor of the Danish West Indies was thrown overboard.

The Danish frigate Havmanden (which translates to "Merman") was en route to St. Thomas, a Caribbean island that is now part of the U.S. Virgin Islands, with convicts and indentured servants aboard destined for work on the island with the Danish West India Company. The plan for the Havmanden, after dropping off the new colonists, was to sail to West Africa to buy enslaved Africans. But due to a shortage of food and water, several crewmembers and convicts aboard seized control of the ship near the Azores in the North Atlantic and began sailing it back to Copenhagen.

The mutineers shot the ship's captain and threw other officers ‪—‬ including Jørgen Iversen Dyppel, the first governor of the Danish West Indies ‪—‬ overboard in the English Channel. After the ship ran aground, the mutineers were tried for their crimes in Copenhagen and sentenced to death.

The story of the Havmanden is one of the most violent mutinies in Denmark's history, according to a Nov. 11 translated statement from the National Museum of Denmark. But now, underwater archaeologists are learning more about the fateful voyage from the wreck, which lies on the seabed just east of Gothenburg, Sweden.

Archaeological divers discovered the rear half of a man's "unusually fine" shoe in the wreckage, according to the statement.

"I see a great many shoes from shipwrecks, but I have never before seen a shoe of such high quality," Vivi Lena Andersen, director of the Rudersdal Museums in Denmark, said in the statement.

The sole has a high level of craftsmanship, Andersen said, which can be seen in the minuscule holes perforating the edge of the shoe. The regularity of the holes would have required tools and expertise that only the most skilled shoemakers had at the time. It was "undoubtedly an expensive and exclusive shoe that only the elite could afford to buy," Andersen said.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

Because the shoe was likely owned by an upper-class individual, experts think it could have belonged to Iversen, the Danish West Indies governor who was thrown overboard.

"The shoe testifies not only to Jørgen Iversen Dyppel's life and dramatic death, but also provides a personal point of entry into the larger story of the establishment of the Danish colonies in the West Indies and the widespread slave trade Denmark was a part of," Andreas Kallmeyer Bloch, a maritime archaeologist at the National Museum of Denmark's Center for Maritime and Underwater Cultural Heritage, said in the statement.

Additional clues to the Danish slave trade on board the Havmanden included two manillas, ring-shaped pieces of metal that were used as currency in West Africa. European merchants cast manillas from copper, brass or iron and traded them for enslaved people in West Africa, particularly along the coast of modern-day Nigeria. But manillas are rarely found in Scandinavia, according to the statement.

"The manillas add another layer to Havmanden's story and underscore the connection to the slave trade, which was a central part of Denmark's colonial activities in both the West Indies and on the west coast of Africa," Kallmeyer Bloch said.

Denmark continued to engage in the transatlantic slave trade for more than a century after the Havmanden sank. Although King Christian VII of Denmark-Norway banned the trade in 1792, slavery continued in the Danish West Indies until 1848, when hundreds of enslaved people revolted and were finally granted their freedom.

Kristina Killgrove is a staff writer at Live Science with a focus on archaeology and paleoanthropology news. Her articles have also appeared in venues such as Forbes, Smithsonian, and Mental Floss. Kristina holds a Ph.D. in biological anthropology and an M.A. in classical archaeology from the University of North Carolina, as well as a B.A. in Latin from the University of Virginia, and she was formerly a university professor and researcher. She has received awards from the Society for American Archaeology and the American Anthropological Association for her science writing.

Some Democrats doubt Trump is serious about cutting deals in new Congress

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump’s postelection statements about working with Democrats have presented the incoming majority with a kaleidoscope to decipher, but some Democratic lawmakers have their doubts.

On the one hand, the Republican president has contended that he can work with House and Senate Democrats after the new Congress is seated in January, even floating a bipartisan immigration bill. But on the other, he promoted his hard-line aide Stephen Miller to White House chief of staff, raising doubts about any cooling of the political environment.

At every stop along his October campaign rally blitz before Republicans suffered massive losses on Nov. 3, Trump railed against “Dumbocrats” and their “bad” policies. He mocked their candidates as too extreme, too corrupt and too beholden to the most progressive wing of their party. He even warned that, if voters handed them control of the House and Senate, it would set off “a 1929-style economic depression.”

Voters ignored him. They handed House Democrats over 250 seats at least come January, and the party will have at least 54 seats in the Senate.

Trump was asked during a “60 Minutes” interview taped Friday afternoon at the White House if he could resist insulting Democratic lawmakers for six months if that’s how long it took to reach a deal on major legislation. “Very easily,” he responded.

But several Democrats are so sure about getting to a “Kumbaya” moment with Trump during his final two years.

Virginia Sen. Tim Kaine said it was hard to take the president at his word because he is “so unpredictable.”

“But I’m certainly telling my colleagues we should try to put as many bills as we can on his desk that do good things for the American public, and then see how he behaves,” he said Tuesday.

“We’re gonna put bills in front of the president on affordability starting Day One,” California Rep. Robert Garcia, the likely incoming House Oversight and Government Reform chair, said Tuesday. “We’ll have to see if he’s serious, but I don’t trust Donald Trump for a second.”

The White House on Monday sent Garcia a letter, obtained by CQ Roll Call, warning House Democrats that their anticipated spate of probes “may cross the line into blatant criminality.”

Rep. Jim McGovern, a Massachusetts Democrat who is in line to chair the Rules Committee in the new Congress, said on the floor Tuesday that he does not see a GOP that is ready to heed the election’s lessons and come to the bargaining table.

“Instead of taking the hint or listening to the overwhelming majority of Americans who told them to change course, Republicans are doubling down on the same tactics and messaging they’ve been jamming down our throats these last two years, and it’s a perfect example of why they lost in the first place,” he said. “They do not listen.

“Then to double down even further, the failed lame-duck president appointed Stephen Miller, the architect of all the cruel policies that most people hate, as his new White House chief of staff,” McGovern added, referring to the Trump administration’s immigration crackdown. “What planet are these people living on?”

Kaine said congressional Democrats might not notice that much of a change from the White House with Miller moving into the chief’s suite near the Oval Office.

“Stephen Miller was calling a lot of the shots, even if he wasn’t chief of staff,” the Virginia Democrat said. “So I view that as a kind of a change of the face, but not of the character of the administration.”

House Republicans’ Tuesday vote to make hard-line conservative Jim Jordan of Ohio their next leader is another red flag for Democrats.

“White House Chief of staff Stephen Miller and House Minority Leader Jim Jordan,” Brendan Duke, a former senior Democratic Senate aide now with the Center on Budget and Policy Priorities, wrote on X. “Where is the pivot to the center after a historic election loss?”

Getting to the center is what retiring Nebraska Rep. Don Bacon says his fellow Republicans and Trump should be doing after the election massacre.

“I do wish we could get to the point where we could solve big problems, instead of just beating the other side,” said Bacon, who has floated a 2028 presidential bid. “We can’t do this Republican-only or Democrat-only. And I would say this to the president, too: We’ve got to start working across the aisle to solve these problems.”

Despite his postelection talk of working with Democrats, Trump has sought to blame them for an up-and-down U.S. stock market.

“Right after the election it went down $3 trillion,” he said Monday during an unplanned White House press briefing. “That was because of the result of the election, because the stock market does not want to see Democrats. And your 401(k)s have gone way down because of the ridiculous result.”

He’s also returned this week to questioning the midterm results: “It’s not even a result. It’s almost hard to believe. Now, I wasn’t on the ballot.”

Members of House Republicans’ new leadership team on Tuesday sounded dug in and ready for a partisan brawl over Democrats’ promised investigations into the president, many of his top aides and Trump family members. Jordan’s election as GOP leader shows that “we are ready to dig in for the fight,” outgoing Speaker Mike Johnson told reporters Tuesday.

Michigan Rep. Lisa McClain, who was reelected House GOP conference chair, pointed to Garcia’s two-word statement on Election Day: “Lawyer up.”

“Tell me how ‘lawyering up’ helps anybody with affordability?” she said. “They’re going to be focused on going after President Trump. We are going to be focused on improving the quality of life for every American.”

To that end, retiring Maryland Rep. Steny H. Hoyer, a former No. 2 House Democrat, said Republicans should heed one big takeaway from the election: “Trump lost.”

NYC’s First Pied-à-Terre Tax Bills Are Coming—and Buyers Are Recalculating

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

New York City's new tax on high-value second homes was designed to raise revenue. But changes to the tax code can also influence how people buy and sell property.

"There's always the risk of avoidance," explains Jake Krimmel, senior economist at Realtor.com®. "Tax bases are famously mobile—especially across municipal boundaries—and that's doubly true for wealthier individuals."

With the first surcharge bills expected to be mailed in the coming days, Realtor.com analyzed the nearly 1 million property and residential-unit records in the city's supplemental tax roll, identifying 24,218 potentially exposed homes that met the relevant valuation thresholds.

Of those, 2,536 (10.5%) sat within just 5% above the minimum valuation required for taxation, while 560 (2.3%) fell within 5% on either side of a higher tax-rate threshold.

Which side of that divide a home falls on can come with huge financial stakes for owners—and agents say buyers are already taking notice.

"The biggest change since July is that buying a second home in New York has become a much more calculated decision," says Donald Brennan, a real estate broker with Engel & Völkers.

In at least one building, two apartments with government-assigned market values just $6,009 apart could face a $37,813 difference in annual surcharges simply because they fall on opposite sides of a tax-rate threshold—assuming neither qualifies for an exemption.

New York City has long attracted wealthy buyers looking for a second residence, whether for work, leisure, or the opportunity to spend part of the year in the city.

"I might be biased, but it's a fantastic place to live, especially if you can afford it," says Krimmel.

His caveat is telling: if you can afford it. With an estimated 52% of New York City renters already burdened by housing costs, wealthy second-home owners present a tempting target for Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s tax-the-rich agenda.

But the new tax will add considerable expense even for those who can afford multiple homes.

For condos and co-ops, the surcharge starts at 4% of their Department of Finance market value at $1 million. One- to three-family homes face an initial rate of 0.8% beginning at $5 million.

The city values houses using comparable sales but condos and co-ops based on their potential rental income, so their tax thresholds aren't directly comparable. At either entry point, however, the annual surcharge starts at approximately $40,000.

Brooklyn accounts for 560 of the 713 Class 1 properties just above the minimum threshold, while Manhattan accounts for 1,700 of the 1,823 condo and co-op units in the equivalent position.

But local brokers point out that many of these properties may never face the surcharge at all.

"In Brooklyn, I think this creates a slightly different conversation because so many of our luxury buyers are purchasing homes they actually intend to live in," says Jessica Peters, a Brooklyn-based broker with Douglas Elliman.

Already, thousands of owners have secured exemptions from the new tax. Of approximately 17,000 notices mailed to owners, close to 11,000 exemption applications had been submitted and more than 8,800 approved as of Oct. 29, Gothamist reports.

That leaves a great deal of uncertainty around how much revenue the city will actually be able to bring in.

While the city budgeted $500 million in annual revenue from the new tax, an April report from City Comptroller Mark Levine warned that exemptions and other behavioral changes could reduce collections to between $340 million and $380 million.

But exemptions aren't the only factor affecting what owners could owe. There's also the matter of the threshold values.

Condo and co-op rates rise to 5.25% above $3 million and 6.5% above $5 million. Rates for one- to three-family homes, meanwhile, increase to 1.05% above $15 million and 1.3% above $25 million.

And because the higher rate applies to the property's entire value, even a small difference in valuation can trigger a monumental increase. The owner of a $3 million condo or co-op subject to the 5.25% rate, for example, would owe just over $157,000.

These higher-rate thresholds have a much narrower geographic footprint, too. Of the 270 records sitting within 5% above a higher-rate threshold, 264 are in Manhattan, including 33 in ZIP code 10022 and 29 in 10021.

Krimmel says owners could respond by reconsidering where they buy or how they use their properties.

"The question is whether there are other places which act as a close substitute for the newly taxed properties," he says.

Brennan sees that calculation emerging among buyers who may spend only a few months a year in New York.

"When you're looking at a multimillion-dollar apartment that you might only use a few months out of the year, that additional tax becomes part of the conversation very quickly," he says.

Some may decide to rent rather than purchase, he adds.

Those decisions could ultimately help free up housing for other buyers or tenants, as Krimmel notes. At the same time, those sales could also reduce the number of properties generating surcharge revenue.

Owners are also challenging the tax in court. Three lawsuits have been filed, two of which remain pending.

A Nov. 4 appeals court ruling dismissed a lawsuit challenging the city's rollout on standing grounds, while the remaining cases challenge its constitutionality.

But homeowners don't need to overturn the law to challenge what they might owe. They can also dispute the city's valuation of their properties—and for those sitting just above a tax threshold, even a small adjustment could eliminate the surcharge entirely.

Narrowing the analysis to properties within just 1% of a tax threshold identified 602 records, including 496 just above the minimum valuation required for taxation.

An additional 106 records fell within 1% of a higher-rate cutoff, including 61 just below and 45 above. All were in Manhattan, and of those, 66 were condos or co-ops near the $3 million threshold, where the annual surcharge increases from 4% to 5.25%.

From a bird's-eye view of the city, those differences might seem negligible. But when looking at a specific building, they are more dramatic.

At 111 West 57th St., a luxury condominium tower near Central Park, unit 57 has a DOF market value of $2,999,794—just $206 below the higher threshold. Unit 38, meanwhile, is valued at $3,005,803.

That $6,009 difference in valuation translates to a $37,813 difference in potential annual surcharges, with the higher-valued apartment facing a charge of approximately $157,805.

A similar disparity appears at 768 Fifth Ave., where two units separated by $19,337 in DOF market value face a $38,317 difference in potential annual charges.

That’s no small sum, even for a wealthy homeowner.

"If they're already paying substantial carrying costs, they're going to look for ways to offset that expense, whether through the purchase price or other concessions," says Ben Jacobs, a Douglas Elliman broker specializing in Manhattan and Brooklyn.

A lower asking price wouldn't automatically reduce the government's valuation or the tax owed, but it could help buyers offset the cost of carrying the property.

Krimmel sees another potential consequence: The surcharge could change what economists call a property's option value—the value of being able to use it differently in the future. Condominiums, for instance, typically offer more flexibility to rent out than co-ops, making that potential rental income part of what a buyer is purchasing.

The same principle could apply to homes that buyers might eventually want to use as second residences.

Even if a property is exempt today, the possibility of a future surcharge could become another consideration in negotiations. Jacobs adds that uncertainty can create its own complications.

"Buyers want to know exactly what their exposure will be before they commit," he says. "When those answers aren't immediately clear, it can slow down a transaction."

To his point, there are early signs of a sharper slowdown in New York City's luxury condo and co-op market since the city began notifying potentially affected property owners in July.

New condo and co-op listings initially priced between $1 million and $3 million fell 11% year over year from July through Nov. 7, compared with a 1% increase between April and June, according to an analysis of Realtor.com listing data.

The decline was even steeper among properties priced between $3 million and $5 million, where new listings fell 16% year over year after July, following a 5% decline in the preceding three months.

By comparison, new listings priced below $1 million fell just 1% year over year since July, after rising 7% between April and June.

Of course, the pied-à-terre tax can’t shoulder all the blame for the slowdown. The luxury market was already showing signs of weakness before the city began sending tax notices, and rising mortgage rates have added another headwind.

Brokers, meanwhile, say some second-home buyers are approaching purchases with greater caution.

"I don't think this means wealthy buyers are suddenly abandoning New York. They still want to be here," Brennan says. "But they're looking much more closely at the numbers."

Peters expects some prospective second-home buyers to delay their decisions or consider renting until they have a better understanding of their potential tax bills.

"What concerns me more is how the additional cost could influence decisions before buyers even start looking," she says.

The first surcharge payments are due on Jan. 1, 2027, and the larger market response—if there is one—will likely take even longer to untangle from the myriad other factors affecting buyer and seller behavior.

But for now, two things are clear: Thousands of potentially affected properties sit close to tax thresholds with huge financial consequences, and new luxury condo and co-op listings have slowed considerably since the city began notifying owners.

Whether those developments are connected—and whether the tax ultimately changes the number of second homes in New York City—remains to be seen.

Allaire Conte is a senior advice writer covering real estate and personal finance trends. She previously served as deputy editor of home services at CNN Underscored Money and was a lead writer at Orchard, where she simplified complex real estate topics for everyday readers. She holds an MFA in Nonfiction Writing from Columbia University and a BFA in Writing, Literature, and Publishing from Emerson College. When she’s not writing about homeownership hurdles and housing market shifts, she’s biking around Brooklyn or baking cakes for her friends.

"Maybe not for Saturday, but I hope for the next week."

The Reds chief commented: "At the moment he is still training with 21s. But when he trains well and I have a talk this week with him, maybe he is back in the next time."

It’s led to Andoni Iraola deploying the on-loan Ronald Araujo – conventionally a centre-back – on the right-hand side of defence, and thankfully the Uruguayan has been a sturdy presence in the Reds’ back four so far this season.

Nearly a year on from agreeing a deal to sign Jacquet, Liverpool could once more turn to Ligue 1 for a breakthrough defensive prospect.

What happens with Araujo’s future may well have a big bearing on whether or not the Reds go all-out to try and sign Ouziad. If the Uruguayan turns his loan move into a permanent transfer, there would be no great need for right-sided defensive reinforcements at Anfield.

However, if he goes back to Barcelona next summer and Frimpong were to depart amid rumours of Saudi Arabian interest, then Julian Ward might push hard in his attempts to snap up the Le Havre starlet.
