The Giggler: The forgotten serial killer who haunted Boston’s South Station

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Boston’s South Station was less than 70 years old in 1969 but seemed much older. It certainly lacked the glamour of nearby North Station, home to the Boston Garden, where the city’s basketball and hockey teams played and where the Rolling Stones and James Brown performed for screaming teenagers. In comparison, the South Station stop on 700 Atlantic Avenue was a dreary place for tired daily commuters on their way to the suburbs of the South Shore. With its high-domed ceiling and rickety escalators built during the Depression era, South Station managed to be both enormous and decrepit, a sickly old giant in need of urgent care. As the Boston Globe said a few years later, the once thriving railroad stop was “now staggering along in the last stages of neglect.”

Yet even as it faced the threat of the wrecking ball, the station remained busy, particularly at holiday time. On Friday, December 26, a 9-year-old Dorchester boy named Kenny Martin wandered into the station with a friend, Thomas Lydon. It was the day after Christmas and Kenny, who had come into Downtown Boston to buy some Boy Scout equipment, spent the afternoon horsing around with Lydon at the station. They’d chased each other up and down the escalators, running through the crowd of postholiday shoppers. As the afternoon came to an end, Lydon departed, while Kenny planned to take the train back home.

In his brown-checked jacket with fur collar, blue bell-bottom pants, and orange work boots, Kenny was a typical Dorchester boy, coming home from a day of fun. He was the youngest of four children, small for his age at less than 50 pounds. It might seem odd for a boy so young to be alone in the city, but Boston kids learned to use the subways early in those days. They often traveled on their own from one side of the city to the other.

The platform where he would’ve waited for the Dorchester train was cold and drafty, for there are few places colder than a Boston subway platform in late December. One could only stand and shiver, waiting for the sound of a faraway bell to warn people of approaching trains. Perhaps Kenny looked down the long, dark tunnel for the sign of a subway car’s light, usually visible long before the car was in the station. The tunnels were foreboding; there were stories of people accidentally getting electrocuted on the infamous “third rail” and of huge rodents living in the underground network. Meanwhile, pigeons, bats, and owls roosted in the rafters, occasionally swooping down to frighten the commuters.

There was no shortage of urban legends around South Station, of how people committed suicide by jumping in front of moving trains and how homeless men wandered in the dark tunnels to sleep underneath cardboard coverings. In time, stories of vagrants evolved into stories of trolls living in the tunnels, trolls who only came out after midnight when the station was closed. This was fantasy stuff, tales told by older kids to tease younger ones.

Of course, the station had its good points. It was a historical landmark, after all, opening for business in 1899 as the largest station in the country, a majestic building made of pink granite. There was also the railroad chapel, Our Lady of the Railways. Built in the 1950s, it was the first of its kind in America. Organ music played during every Sunday service, and Boston’s Archbishop Cushing held special masses and Easter vigils, perfect for travelers who couldn’t get home in time to worship. There was a lunch counter, a newsstand, the Sidecar Bar-Grill, a bakery, and a barbershop. A Paramount film crew had been to South Station earlier in the year to shoot a scene for a Jack Lemmon movie, The Out of Towners. For a few days, the dilapidated old station was enlivened by the buzz of Hollywood energy and curious crowds. Despite these occasional bright spots, the atmosphere remained glum and ramshackle. When the crowds thinned out after the rush hour, all that remained were boarded-up areas that always seemed to be “under construction” and weary cleaning crews wiping out the messes of the day.

At some point in the afternoon of December 26, Kenny met a pudgy, dark-haired man with a round face. The man seemed different from the other people hurrying around the station. He was rumpled. He needed a shave and a bath. It turned out the man was also named Kenneth. What they talked about, or how they spent their next few hours together, is unknown. History would reveal that the unshaven man knew all about the secret tunnels in South Station and knew all the special places where people could hide. He may have offered to show Kenny around the old building, an irresistible prospect for a 9-year-old boy.

Kenny’s body was found eleven days later. He’d been strangled to death. His body was hidden in one of the station’s underground passageways. Someone had wrapped it in a canvas bag and covered it with debris. A search team consisting of police from two districts, as well as city engineers, had spent the day exploring sewers and tunnels around the station. The search ended in an abandoned transit tunnel, a ghostly location that had once been used to transport cargo to the surface cars. It was a dingy place for a little boy to wind up, cold and dark and airless.

He could’ve been lured there only by one of the station’s trolls.

Security guards patrolled South Station and routinely tossed out vagrants and drunks, shoving them out the door onto Atlantic Avenue. These scenes were unpleasant, but the guards were under orders to keep the winos and grifters out. The raggedy station only looked worse if pitiful old drunks were lounging on the wooden benches. Yet Kenneth Harrison, the pudgy man, had been living in the place for nearly a year, like a hermit hiding in a cave. He didn’t have to compete for a bed at one of the nearby shelters, not when he could hunker down in one of the abandoned offices beneath the station.

The morning after he’d killed Kenny Martin, Harrison slouched out into the daylight, searching for a rooming house where he could find a bed and lie low. The sidewalk was still slippery from the previous night’s rain and sleet. It would have been a nice day to hang out at the station, yet Harrison felt it was time to go. Hanging around was too risky, even if the dead boy was buried deeper than an old bone.

Harrison had grown up in Roxbury’s Mission Hill neighborhood and had dropped out of school after the eighth grade. Now, at 31, he was an unemployed restaurant worker, living a vagabond’s existence and spending what little money he had on alcohol. He’d become one of life’s losers, a degenerate barfly with no future. He was virtually indistinguishable from dozens of others in his situation. The difference was that Harrison murdered children.

Kenny Martin, whose face would soon be featured in local newspapers, was Harrison’s second young victim. Two-and-a-half years earlier, Harrison had killed a 6-year-old girl named Lucy Palmarin. She’d been cashing in an empty tonic bottle at a store. Witnesses saw her climbing into the car of an unknown man. She was found floating among the garbage in South Boston’s Fort Point Channel, a mile from her home. Since the medical examiner found no signs of injury or foul play, Lucy’s death was ruled an accidental drowning. Lucy’s disappearance created a flurry of activity in South Boston. Posters with her picture were distributed throughout the community. Her poor mother waited up nights, clutching a book of old photographs of the missing girl. Three weeks into the search, the Boston police called in the FBI for assistance. They suspected a kidnapping, but the truth was worse.

On April 15, 1967, Harrison was driving a cab, among his many odd jobs. He would later tell a judge that he had dropped off a fare near the D Street housing project where Lucy lived. He saw her walking and offered to give her a ride. Instead of taking her home, he drove to the channel where he tossed her in the city’s notoriously dirty water.

In another version of the confession, Harrison said the little girl asked him for a quarter, which led to him giving her a piggyback ride. Once she was up on his back, he easily pitched her into the water. So he said. His confessions sounded ludicrous. Lucy’s family was Puerto Rican and new to America. The girl didn’t speak English. In between the two child murders, Harrison had killed two adults. There’d been a barroom beef with a fellow named Joseph Breen, an ex-marine and water company employee. Harrison and Breen had been drinking at the Novelty Bar in Boston’s Combat Zone, a dank neighborhood of nudie bars and X-rated theaters.

Later, after an argument over money, Harrison beat Breen to death with a rock.

Inexplicably, Harrison called the police that night, telling them they could find Breen’s body floating in an excavation pit on Kneeland Street near Chinatown. When the police asked who was calling, Harrison felt a laugh coming up his throat, like an eruption.

It was a time when killers were becoming celebrities, with memorable names and trademarks, like the Boston Strangler or the Zodiac in California. Why couldn’t Harrison be “The Giggler?”

Harrison must’ve felt as elusive as the Zodiac, because he’d actually returned to the Novelty a short time later. A couple of Breen’s friends were there and recognized him as the man who had been with Breen the night he was killed. Breen’s friends called the police, but by the time they arrived, Harrison was gone again, no doubt back at the train station, hiding in plain sight.

Then, just a month before killing Kenny Martin, Harrison had murdered an old woman, 75-year-old Clovis Parker of South Boston. As he’d done with the little girl, he left Clovis floating in Fort Point Channel, a mere five-minute walk from Harrison’s South Station hideout. He’d tell investigators that he had met the old woman on a bridge, and she’d asked for help getting across. Without giving a reason, Harrison said that he’d simply kicked Clovis Parker’s cane from under her and then tossed her over the guardrail. The unfortunate woman’s death hadn’t even made it to the newspapers.

A few days after killing Kenny Martin, Harrison called his sister, Eileen. He confessed that he had killed a boy and told her where the body was hidden. On January 6, the day Kenny’s body was found, Harrison boarded a train at South Station and left Boston.

Eileen consulted a lawyer and then called the police. They followed her tip to find the body, and then a quartet of detectives armed with an arrest warrant traced Harrison to Providence. They arrested him without incident and held him without bail in Suffolk County.

While detectives questioned him about Kenny Martin’s death, Harrison interrupted.

“As long as I’m here,” he said, “I might as well tell you about a few more.”

He told them about Lucy Palmarin, Joseph Breen, and Clovis Parker. Investigators were stunned. Harrison was confessing to four murders committed over a span of thirty-two months.

When he talked about Kenny Martin, Harrison said he’d had “an urge to kill” but didn’t remember doing anything. He merely woke up the next morning and saw the boy with a rope around his neck.

Harrison showed little emotion as he spoke, but he seemed comfortable talking about his crimes. He even took a liking to the detectives interrogating him. Later, when he learned that one of the detectives had retired, Harrison sent a card.

Yet his frankness about the murders and his friendship with the detectives masked a fragile mind.

Featured image: South Station, 1970, Daniel Brody.

Don Stradley is an award-winning journalist and author of several books. Boston Tabloid: The Killing of Robin Benedict (2022) was a top seller on Amazon Kindle and The War was a #1 seller in its category, praised in the Times of London, and selected as a Favorite of 2021 by The Progressive Magazine. His earlier titles Berserk (2019), A Fistful of Murder (2020), and Slaughter in the Streets (2020) focused on how the sport of boxing was touched by the world of crime. He has also written for various movie-streaming companies, including Fandor, The Film Detective, and Film Masters. He has appeared on several true crime podcasts.

40 Years Later, Top Gun's Most Famous Line Is Still The Greatest '80s Movie Quote

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

What makes an '80s movie unique? The decade gave humanity lots of horrors that still plague us today, from rampant capitalism, corporations running wild, the rise of the religious far-right, and an obsession with consumerism that is still dominating our lives. But it was also a time of impeccable pop culture, whether it was video games, music, or movies.

Indeed, the 1980s were a wild ride for film fans, with the rise of blockbusters and high concept genre movies with big visual effects paving the way for the IP dominance of the box office. The best '80s movies show the versatility of the medium of cinema, creating strong iconography and delivering one-liners that are remembered decades later.

That's why it's hard to think of a movie that's as quintessentially '80s as Tony Scott's "Top Gun." This is a maximalist movie that exudes iconography, with every exterior day scene being set at sunset, a loud pock-rock soundtrack, plenty of leather jackets, a rousing love of Americana, and old-fashioned American patriotism wrapped in incredibly vague Cold War-inspired politics. This is also a movie so filled with unbridled homoeroticism that it became a gay classic, thanks to a warm story about male friendships that's also so steamy hot that sweaty muscled men are constantly talking about wanting each other's asses while standing half-naked.

Is "Top Gun" the best '80s movie? Probably not, but it is representative of the decade. This movie is an exercise in over indulgence, a movie that's endlessly entertaining and also dumb as hell. The overt military propaganda makes "Top Gun" essentially the movie version of the "Yvan eht nioj" music video from "The Simpsons."

Though the movie has plenty of great lines, one stands out as the most '80s movie quote.

The quote comes early in the movie, when Lt. Pete "Maverick" Mitchell (Tom Cruise) and his wingman LTJG nick "Goose" Bradshaw (Anthony Edwards) are walking down the tarmac on the way to their jets for a new assignment. When a fellow student tells Maverick that his rival Iceman (Val Kilmer) has won yet another contest, the cocky Maverick puts on a fake smile and just replies "I feel the need..." before Goose chimes in and the two say in unison, "The need... for speed!" before doing a high five.

It's a ridiculous moment, a cheesy line with an even cheesier delivery from Cruise and Edwards, but it captures the tone of "Top Gun" — loud, proud, and willing to have fun.

In the context of the movie, however, the line is also important to understanding Maverick. As his call sign implies, Maverick is reckless, and his impulsive behavior makes him a dangerous liability in the eyes of the academy and the military. Sure, Maverick brings results, but he is unreliable, especially compared to Iceman, who is just as good while also being able to follow commands.

"I feel the need, the need for speed" encapsulates not only Maverick's love for danger and for speed but also his insecurities and competitiveness. It's the ultimate expression of American individualism in "Top Gun," the belief of pushing yourself beyond your limits or not giving up when someone surpasses you. It's a declaration of purpose and bravado.

"You are still dangerous. But you can be my wingman any time." — Iceman

"Son, your ego is writing checks your body can't cash." — Stinger

"I don't like you because you're dangerous." — Iceman

"God, he loved flying with you Maverick. But he would've done it anyway... without you. He'd have hated it, but he would've done it." — Carole

"I want somebody's butt, I want it now, I've HAD IT!" — Air Boss Johnson

"You're not going to be happy unless you're going Mach 2 with your hair on fire." — Charlie

Snowbound suspense: 5 thrillers set during snowstorms

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

A glorious snowfall can conjure up warm thoughts of hot cocoa, kids tossing snowballs and building forts, the holidays, a still, peaceful white landscape. But it can also create a darker, dangerous flipside. Getting trapped, losing power, being exposed to the elements. I’ve always loved thrillers that take advantage of a bad snowstorm–of bringing cold, heartless motivations to frozen landscapes.

In my latest novel, The Cold Read, I take full advantage of the chilling nature of a snowy locale by setting my book at a remote, abandoned ski resort, where the elements quickly take on a very sinister nature.

Here are a few other books that do the same. If you want to shiver your way through a good mystery without actually having to worry about frostbite, you’ll want to pick up these other snowbound stories as we head into the winter season.

This novel, involving a group of co-workers setting off on a retreat, would be a nightmare on its own even without the thriller elements. Traveling with coworkers! Skits! Presentations! The terror! To make things much, much worse, however, this work retreat involves an avalanche, power loss, and the little matter of the group disappearing and dying off.

The characters make this book the tense, twisty thriller it is. It’s an enjoyable puzzle to piece together as you read.

A group of competitive snowboarding friends reunite in the French alps at the ski resort where their frenemy mysteriously disappeared some years before. But weird things immediately start to happen. Their cell phones disappear and they are cut off from the rest of the world when the  cable cars stop working, leaving them stranded on a dangerous mountain. Tensions abound as past secrets and suspicions start to emerge.

Told in dual timelines, the book does a great job of showing how fierce friendships can be both invigorating and toxic.

Martin Edwards, Miss Winter in the Library With a Knife

Six writers and editors are invited to an exclusive Christmas retreat to play a murder mystery game. The setting is remote, the weather is bad, and the guests all have had recent terrible luck in their careers and relationships.

In a classic Golden Age style with an homage to Clue, the novel sets the reader as another player in the game, complete with a “clue finder” guide and puzzles to solve. When the game becomes reality and people start getting bumped off, all bets are off and the competitive guests have to start playing as though their lives depended on it–because they do.

When a woman and her son arrive on a bus during a snowstorm in New York, escaping an abusive situation, service worker Mattie breaks the rules of her job and brings them to her own house instead of the local shelter. This mistake proves costly as the storm intensifies, an intruder begins to stalk them, and strange things begin happening.

Tightly plotted and claustrophobic, I had a tough time reading this one late into the evening.

The Winter Over follows a small group of Antarctic research scientists setting up for the winter over at Shackleton South Pole Research Facility in Antarctica. They are supposed to be tested physically and psychologically to prepare for the conditions and the loneliness–nine weeks of complete isolation, constant darkness, and extreme cold.

As Cass, a newbie to the job, starts to settle in to her work, a suspicious death of one of the crew makes her wonder if someone on the team might’ve snapped. Trapped at base with a killer and nowhere to go, the team doesn’t know who to trust. What will kill them first? The extreme conditions or the humans on base?

Tara Laskowski is the award-winning author of the suspense novels The Cold Read, The Weekend Retreat, The Mother Next Door, and One Night Gone. She also wrote two short story collections, Modern Manners for Your Inner Demons and Bystanders. She has won the Agatha Award and Thriller Award for her short fiction and was the longtime editor of the online flash fiction journal SmokeLong Quarterly. She lives in Virginia with her husband, crime writer Art Taylor, and their son Dashiell.

Huddersfield Town’s Drury’s freak injury as Radinio Balker calls for no more 3-week international breaks

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

As the Terriers only needed to avoid a big defeat to guarantee their place at the top of Group A, it was no great shock to see Drury watching from the stands but the reason he gave afterwards was a surprise.

"I've got two fractured ribs from shouting in training," revealed the head coach. “I've been told to try not to shout for a few days so I took a step back (on Tuesday) with full confidence that everything would be in the right place.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

"Jon (Stead) and Dale (Tonge) took the group and everyone's been excellent – as they have all season.

"And full credit to the players to respond from Saturday (when the highest-ranked team in the competition went out of the FA Cup to Exeter City), which was disappointing."

Tonge is an experienced coach, with spells at former clubs Barnsley and Rotherham United included on his CV, but has never been a manager. Former Towns striker Stead was co-manager with Drury when the pair took interim charge last spring, but became the assistant when the pair were kept on in the summer.

Drury will at least get some rest at the weekend, with Huddersfield's scheduled game at Wycombe Wanderers postponed due to international call-ups.

"I've just got to stop shouting," he said. "I'm going to try and get some pain relief injections over the next few weeks."

Meanwhile, defender Radinio Balker hopes this season's three-week international break will be the last of its kind, saying it is bad for players. This month reverts to the more normal two-week break.

Balker, who is in the Suriname squad for Thursday's friendly in Lithuania, came home early in the last break to play at Burton Albion, but still felt his time with his country caused the adductor injury he then picked up on his return.

"I wasn't away for the 16 days that my other team-mates had, but it was a long time," he commented. "You're gone from your normal situation where you have your routine and everything.

"I was only gone for eight days, or nine days, I think. To me, that sounds more realistic, because I came back and I think the injury I endured was probably because of all the travel and being away from my normal routine.

"I can only imagine that my team-mates had similar situations back in Suriname when they came back."

The break wreaked havoc with the League On fixture list, with games organised for both weekends, but postponed if clubs had three or more international call-ups. In the first weekend, that caused call-offs for all five Yorkshire teams in the division, who have had to find new dates for those fixtures.

"I would say two weeks or ten days is probably best," said Balker. "I did understand why they did it this year, because they just had the World Cup, of course.

"So I don't think they will do it more often than this, because there was a lot of complaining about it, but, yeah, the two weeks that is coming is probably better, for sure."

Deep cuts: The vampire stories that inspired Caitlin Starling’s new novel, ‘Milkteeth’

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

You’ve watched or read (or both!) Interview with the Vampire, Dracula, Sinners, and all the other heavy-hitters, you’ve dabbled in playing Vampire: The Masquerade…now what?

You could always check out Milkteeth, my newest novel, which asks the burning question: what if vampires had to breastfeed?

…I promise it makes more sense in context.

More seriously, it’s about Beatrice, a vampiric broodmother; she raises the fledgling children of other vampires through the first three years of their transformation, until they are both mentally mature and able to consume human blood safely. Beatrice is very good at what she does, but she’s looked down on with a certain amount of disgust by the vampires who rely on her.

When she takes on a challenging new charge and her body begins to change in inexplicable ways, she has to figure out a solution before everything can be taken for her—or worse, she devours her own brood.

It’s equal parts domestic and rancid, filthy and tender, violent and intimate. And if that’s less your speed, or you want to see some of the works that are responsible, at least in part, for Milkteeth’s existence, here are some lesser-known vampire stories that I’ve enjoyed.

If you’ve heard of anything on this list, I’d wager it’s this show. Running three seasons on Showtime, Penny Dreadful brought together a broad variety of Victorian pulp horror into an atmospheric, brutal, and occasionally messy symphony. While there are a lot of moving parts, Eva Green’s character, Vanessa Ives, finds herself embroiled on a take on Dracula that is lushly horrific.

The combination of corrupted filth and glittering set pieces across the span of the series epitomizes one of my favorite things about vampire stories: how the seduction and the rot are one and the same, and are inextricably linked.

Buehlman’s novel, set in the 1970s Manhattan subway system, is horrifically, wonderfully rancid, with an all-consuming voice and style. His vampires are monsters in every sense of the word, and their charisma comes from their monstrosity, not in spite of it. He also poses a question that I loved, and kept in mind when writing Milkteeth: what would scare a vampire?

Tom Hiddleston and Tilda Swinton star as Adam and Eve, two vampires who have loved each other for centuries, but often spend long periods apart. When Adam descends into a deep depression, Eve comes home to visit.

The movie is a meditation on music, art, and, ultimately, faith in the coming and going of cycles, and the ability of even the unchanging undead to reinvent themselves on occasion. It’s also occasionally hilarious. Seeing this in a little independent theater with a glass of red wine indelibly shaped the writer I grew into.

Speaking of formative vampires, I saw Hellsing for the first time shortly after it was translated into English. It’s on the dark and brutal side of vampire stories, filled with vicious killers, relentless cravings for blood, and a large helping of ominous and cruel bureaucracy. Alucard, bound to serve the descendants of Abraham Van Helsing, seeks out and destroys other undead monsters, but he’s hardly tame. It’s a great reminder that vampires hunt; it’s definitional.

Hellsing has more than a few adaptations, including Hellsing Ultimate, which stays closer to the original manga source. I think I owe myself a rewatch!

If you prefer your vampires more on the domestic side, Dead Collections is a brilliant, funny, and intimate take on the genre. Sol, an archivist who also happens to be a vampire, and so rarely leaves his place of work, falls in love with the widow of a television writer come to donate the papers of her late wife, and in the process begins to grapple with what his vampirism really means. Like the archive he works in, he’s now immortal…but always at risk of arbitrary destruction.

And while vampire stories often deal with legacy and the hidden costs of immortality, Dead Collections does it all from a delightfully fresh (and queer) angle. In particular, its focus on coming to terms with a changed body echo through Milkteeth.

I read Woman, Eating while editing Milkteeth. The two are quite different, with Woman, Eating centering on a young vampire trying to engage in the larger mortal world, and her experiences with art and isolation, but they both return again and again to that most central vampiric curse: the endless hunger.

Try Woman, Eating if you’re in the mood for something on the literary side that will still manage to unsettle you.

There’s a wide world of vampire-inspired stories out there. If any of these stories capture you, follow where they lead. You may discover a whole new way to look at our favorite thirsty monstrous mirrors.

Caitlin Starling writes horror-tinged speculative fiction of all flavors. Her first novel, The Luminous Dead, won the LOHF Best Debut Award and was nominated for both the Bram Stoker and Locus Awards. She is also the author of the gothic horror tales Yellow Jessamine and The Death of Jane Lawrence, as well as a novella in the Vampire: The Masquerade audio collection, Walk Among Us. Her nonfiction has appeared in Nightmare and Uncanny. Starling also works in narrative design, and has been paid to invent body parts. She's always on the lookout for new ways to inflict insomnia.

Katie Collom on exploring her relationship with true crime in a suspense novel

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

My first encounter with the genre was during an annual trip to visit my grandma. Each year, I’d dive into the pile of Reader’s Digest she’d acquired, devouring them cover to cover. It was in one these that I read an article about a criminal profiler who suffered a mental breakdown.

This left a deep impression on my eleven-year-old mind. I couldn’t believe there were human beings who could commit acts so horrific, they actually drove their pursuers insane. More importantly, there were people whose jobs were to figure out how sociopaths think!

I continued to seek out more content about criminals and their crimes. The effect could be…less than healthy. I recall spending a night at a friend’s house and being unable to sleep because it was located somewhat near a railroad track. Just the week before, I’d watched a show about a killer who chose his victims by their proximity to the trains he freight-hopped.

Later as a teenager, my worries became more specific: I’d fret daily about my parents properly locking the windows and doors in our home before we all went to bed. Thankfully we didn’t have a basement or an attic because if we did, I probably wouldn’t have ever gotten a solid night’s sleep.

It wasn’t until I was an adult that these types of articles and shows were given a label. By then, true-crime podcasts and TV series had become so trendy they were their own genre. The sudden abundance of content seemed tailor-made for someone like me: a person who’d been consuming these stories steadily from childhood. Even better, there was a whole community out there, who shared similar obsessions.

(If you’re here, reading this essay, it’s fair to say you know exactly what I mean. You’re one of my people!)

In my new thriller, The Terrarium, my main character is a true-crime obsessed cat sitter named Annie. The isolating nature of her job—going into people’s homes to care for their cats—lends itself to a lot of introspection. For Annie, it manifests in her fanaticism for a podcast called Cuppa Crime.

Her relationship with Cuppa Crime is an emotional crutch. Its hosts, Gail and Gary, Annie’s voices of reason. Whenever she’s anxious, afraid, or bored, she escapes into the tales of heinous crimes that Gail and Gary spin on their podcast. I was interested in seeing how far I could push Annie’s obsession. In turn, this made me stop and study my own relationship with true crime.

Like Annie, I’m a bit of a cliché: a millennial woman who probably consumes just a little too much true crime. It’s no secret that the audience leans very heavily toward women. Additionally, the victims in most popular stories—whether that be books, podcasts, or TV series—also tend to be disproportionately women.

In some ways, this makes it easy to explain away any discomfort I might feel around the genre. I rationalize that I consume it to learn how to protect myself as a woman. Or because I can empathize deeply with the female victims. Or because I want to gain an understanding of how evil people think.

In The Terrarium, Annie drip-feeds herself a constant stream of true crime. Her fascination with it veers into paranoia, and yet, she doesn’t see a problem with it because her overconsumption is for the ‘right’ reasons. Annie’s goal is to unravel the mysteries featured in the Cuppa Crime episodes she listens to. She wants justice and therefore struggles to see when it’s all too much.

Granted, a lot of Annie’s choices are downright chaotic and not things I’d ever do. But I also know how easy it is to cross the line between a healthy amount of true crime and consuming so much that it starts to have a very real effect on your life.

While all my reasons for engaging with the genre ring true, there is a point where too much knowledge becomes a jumble of information with few tangible benefits. Will listening to yet another podcast episode or watching another documentary really teach me how to better protect myself, to empathize, to understand the way the mind of a psychopath works (spoiler: you can’t and never will)?

Since these books, podcasts, and shows are presented as entertainment, it can be easy to consume it the same way you might binge Love is Blind. When the subject matter is so dark, too much too fast can be dicey.

I’m not planning to give up true crime any time soon—like I said, we go way back. But I’m learning to find a balance between prioritizing my mental health and still enjoying a hobby I’ve had since I was a kid.

Real mysteries continue to fascinate me (I am a thriller writer after all) and there’s nothing quite like sinking your teeth into a well-done series. And…if it’s okay to wear shirts with a stranger’s name on them and get way too invested in a bunch of guys throwing a ball around, I think spending your time engaging with true crime is equally valid.

It just doesn’t hurt to be a little more intentional. To pause and consider my reasons for listening to, watching, or reading something in the genre and the impact it can have on me. I remind myself that it’s okay to take a break! These stories will still be there when I’m ready to come back to them.

In some ways, I see myself in the paranoid cat-sitter I’ve created, and I hope you will too. Annie was a fun character to write and, hopefully, a fun character to read in a sort of peek-through-your-fingers way. She certainly has some entertaining ways of coping with her love for true crime, but that’s what makes her so compelling.

Like Annie does with Cuppa Crime, you might just get more than you bargained for when you step into The Terrarium. But don’t worry, you’ll be fine. Just keep your wits about you and (as Gail and Gary like to say) always trust your gut.

Katie Collom is the author of Peter Miles Has to Die. She grew up in Mazatlan, Mexico, and is a life-long expat and world traveler. She spent four years in Texas and has carried a piece of it with her ever since. Currently, she resides in York, England, with her husband and three cats.

Created by Grove Atlantic and Electric Literature

Nvidia stock falls nearly 10% as CEO Jensen Huang announces resignation

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation here.

Nvidia shares plunged nearly 10% on Thursday, sending the company's value below $4 trillion in market cap after Jensen Huang announced he would immediately step down as CEO and a member of the board at the chipmaker.

Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress will serve as interim CEO until a new leader is appointed, Nvidia said in a Thursday statement. Harvey C. Jones, former CEO of Synopsys, will become the first-ever chair of the board.

On Sunday, Huang admitted to having an affair. The CEO was romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at cloud company Megaspeed, according to The Financial Times, who U.S. intelligence officials believe is a spy for China.

The New York Times on Wednesday reported that Huang had another extramarital relationship with Kimberly Powell, vice president of health care at Nvidia. Powell reports to Huang, according to the Times.

Before Huang resigned, several Wall Street analysts told CNBC that this departure could lead the stock to fall 20%, given how critical he has been to Nvidia's success. Nvidia's stock is already down more than 20% over the last month.

Bernstein on Thursday slashed its price target on Nvidia's stock by half from $400 to $200.

Stacy Rasgon, a senior analyst at Bernstein, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Thursday that Huang's alleged affair with a direct report means it is "much tougher for the board to leave him in position."

Huang was central to Nvidia's rise, having co-founded the company in 1993 and was pivotal in transforming Nvidia from a gaming hardware player to the company at the center of the artificial intelligence boom.

Rasgon said it is unclear who could be the "visionary" to lead Nvidia into the future as new CEO.

"Jensen is Nvidia, right? He is the visionary. The reason they're in the position that they're in today is because he made decisions 10 years ago," Rasgon said. "Who is the right person now to make the decisions for the next 10 years? I don't intuitively know who that person would be."

Earlier, U.S. lawmakers had called for Huang's resignation, adding further pressure to the CEO and the company.

Richard Reyle, chief investment officer at Questar Capital Partners, said Nvidia's lack of a succession plan makes the stock "uninvestable."

"Nvidia doesn't have that person in place, and so it's going to be either a choice of keeping Jensen and being faced with months and months of being hauled in front of probably congressional boards and things to explain themselves and all kinds of interrogations," Reyle told CNBC's "Access Middle East."

"Or they're going to have to move on from him, and that's just going to make the stock uninvestable. That doesn't make the AI trade uninvestable, but it certainly makes Nvidia uninvestable."

— CNBC's Caleigh Keating contributed to this report.

Why Is Tim Cook Being Mentioned as Nvidia’s Next CEO?

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Home » Crypto Market News » Why Is Tim Cook Being Mentioned as Nvidia’s Next CEO?

As prediction markets price in a Jensen Huang exit, Yale’s Jeffrey Sonnenfeld names an outside successor.

Former Apple CEO Tim Cook has entered the conversation as a potential successor to Jensen Huang after Yale professor Jeffrey Sonnenfeld floated his name in Time magazine, citing Cook’s unique track record following a major tech founder.

Now serving as Apple’s executive chairman after stepping down in September, Cook is drawing speculative interest on prediction markets as reports surrounding Huang’s personal life and links with alleged Chinese spy spark boardroom scrutiny at the world’s largest chipmaker.

When Sonnenfeld weighed potential outside replacements alongside former Google CEO Eric Schmidt, Cook stood out for several practical reasons that mirror Nvidia’s current position:

Still, there is no indication that Nvidia has approached Cook or that he is actively seeking the job. And to understand why succession is even being discussed, it helps to look at the controversy unfolding inside Nvidia’s boardroom.

Since November 8, media reports have alleged that Huang had an affair with a woman suspected of ties to Chinese intelligence—a relationship he reportedly acknowledged in a letter to staff.

The revelations have raised immediate questions around national security risks and executive succession. In response, Nvidia’s board launched an independent investigation, sending Nvidia shares down more than 8% in after-hours trading.

While the board investigates, prediction market traders are already betting aggressively on how soon Huang could step down—and who might replace him.

On Polymarket, traders are giving an 88% chance that Huang leaves his position by December 31, with the year-end contract drawing more than $4.7 million in volume.

Bettors also place 59% odds (across $2.43 million in volume) on an announcement happening as soon as today, November 12. On Kalshi, 63% of bettors expect Huang to depart by November 15.

Trading is equally active in the successor markets, though insiders currently lead the pack.

On Kalshi current Nvidia CFO Colette Kress leads at 30%, closely followed by Ian Buck, who runs Nvidia’s hyperscale and high-performance computing business, at 29%. Tim Cook sits at 5%.

On Polymarket, Buck holds the top spot at 25%, just ahead of Kress at 22%.

Because Nvidia sits at the center of the global AI trade, sudden leadership uncertainty ripples far beyond a single stock. Alongside Nvidia’s after-hours drop, broader tech equities and AI-linked crypto assets, including Nvidia-linked crypto derivatives and tokenized stocks, have already posted losses as markets brace for what comes next.

LinkedIn brings its B2B ad targeting to U.K. connected TVs

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

In its latest effort to capitalize on growing demand for video, LinkedIn is expanding its CTV ads to the U.K.

The launch follows its staged U.S. rollout, which began in April 2024, before becoming widely available across the U.S. and Canada in 2025. It brings LinkedIn’s professional audience targeting to CTV, allowing advertisers to reach U.K. decision-makers by job role, industry and seniority while they watch ad-supported streaming content. For example, a software company looking to reach senior IT decision-makers could use LinkedIn’s targeting to serve CTV ads to viewers in relevant job roles and industries, rather than relying solely on broader demographic or household-level targeting.

So far, the platform has partnered with LG and Titan OS for its U.K. CTV ads offering, and it’s available to all advertisers in the market.

“When you think about professional identities and how you want to reach and engage with them, we’re a platform where people tell us who they are,” said Jae Oh, senior director of product management at LinkedIn. “They get verified for the jobs that they have, and we’re able to support a higher fidelity of reach when it comes to how you reach those audiences at scale.”

LinkedIn is positioning CTV as more than just an awareness play, arguing that its professional audience targeting can help advertisers reach decision-makers earlier in the buying process and influence them further down the funnel.

“For so long CTV has been seen as like you naturally think it’s an awareness play, but actually where we’ve seen a real success is when clients use it as part of a full-funnel strategy,” said Milka Privodanova, vice president of LinkedIn Marketing Solutions, large customers — international at LinkedIn.

The company claims that people exposed to a brand’s CTV ad cost 30% less to convert into sales leads. Cisco, which has already tested the targeting update, saw 52% incremental reach beyond its LinkedIn in-feed activity, 141% higher lead form completion rates and 35% higher click-through rates among audiences exposed to its CTV ads, according to its LinkedIn case study.

But the proposition comes with questions over cost and targeting accuracy. Accuracast, for example, evaluated LinkedIn’s CTV offering when it was only available in the U.S. and found its pricing significantly higher than other direct CTV providers, alongside high minimum spend requirements.

“There’s definitely a business case for it — our B2B clients would love to get more reach and eyeballs on connected TVs — but if we’re going to pay LinkedIn’s usual premium pricing, we’d want more clarity on how LinkedIn plans to accurately target their users on these devices,” said the agency’s group CEO Farhad Divecha.

The idea is that advertisers can use CTV to introduce their brand to decision-makers, retarget and nurture those audiences through video and other LinkedIn ad formats to encourage them to take action. The platform is also looking to improve how advertisers measure CTV’s contribution to business outcomes beyond clicks and lead form completions. Oh said LinkedIn is also investing in revenue and pipeline attribution, alongside incrementality testing, to help advertisers understand CTV’s contribution to business outcomes.

“You will see deeper investment when it comes to incrementality testing or experimentation to prove that these different tactics you’re employing work for you,” Oh said.

The U.K. CTV expansion gives LinkedIn another way to tap into the demand for video, by  extending its video offering beyond the platform and onto the TV screen.

Video advertising on LinkedIn is growing 25% year-over-year, according to Privodanova, though she declined to share how much CTV contributes to that growth.

LinkedIn has been stitching together the pieces of its CTV business since 2024 when it lined up Roku and Samsung Ads as supply partners and launch LinkedIn Premier, a managed product built on NBCUniversal’s streaming inventory, A deal for premium Paramount inventory followed in mid-2025, along with integrations with Innovid and Sprinkly meant to make CTV campaigns easier to run.

“As a premium advertising platform, LinkedIn will need to demonstrate that its audience data can translate into meaningful reach and measurable impact,” said Andy Hunt, head of paid media at agency Brave Bison. “Targeting professionals through household devices presents its own challenges around signal quality, accuracy and attribution. Advertisers will want to understand how effectively CTV exposure can be connected to wider campaign performance, rather than simply adding another awareness metric.”

Laser Digital just won Japan’s first crypto license in 4 years – Nomura might sell it

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Nomura Holdings, one of Japan’s largest financial services companies, is reportedly considering selling its cryptocurrency subsidiary, Laser Digital, amid continued financial losses in its European operations.

According to Bloomberg, Nomura has held discussions with potential buyers, including financial services firm Marex Group. However, the company has not made a final decision yet and could still choose to retain the business.

This followed Nomura’s European operations reporting a pretax loss of 7.4 billion yen ($46.5 million) in the third quarter of 2026, marking their fourth consecutive quarterly loss. However, it’s not yet confirmed by Nomura whether Laser Digital was responsible for any part of the financial setback.

For background, Laser Digital was established in 2022 and provides institutional crypto trading, asset management, and investment services. Despite the possibility of a sale, the subsidiary has continued expanding its operations.

In fact, in August 2026, Laser Digital Japan secured the country’s first new crypto exchange registration in approximately four years, allowing it to offer institutional trading and liquidity services.

It also received preliminary approval from the US Office of the Comptroller of the Currency in May to establish a national trust bank.

But it looks like market volatility and financial pressures may have prompted the company to reconsider its investment.

This is because the global crypto market’s trading volume has fallen to $64.513B after a drop of 21.3% in the past 24 hours. Meanwhile, the Crypto Fear and Greed Index also stood at 28 in the ‘Fear’ zone at press time.

At the same time, Japan’s latest inflation data has increased expectations that the Bank of Japan (BOJ) could raise interest rates again in December, potentially creating volatility across global financial markets.

This prediction comes as the producer prices rose 7.1% in October, exceeding economists’ expectations of 6.5%.

Import prices also surged 24.8% in yen terms, largely because the weak Japanese currency has made imported fuel, raw materials and other goods more expensive.

That said, another hike in December would mark the first time since 1989 that the country has raised interest rates three times in one calendar year.

This followed the United States’ reportedly joining Japan in supporting the yen through a coordinated foreign exchange intervention, marking its first such involvement since 1998.

Needless to say, the potential rate increase could also affect Bitcoin [BTC] and US stocks through the yen carry trade. Now, though, Japan has an estimated 5 million cryptocurrency owners, but its crypto adoption remains relatively limited.

According to market estimates, Japan’s cryptocurrency market was valued at $1.69 billion in 2025 and is expected to grow to $1.98 billion in 2026. It could reach $7.12 billion by 2034, representing a compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of 17.32% between 2026 and 2034.

This coincided with a recent report by AMBCrypto, wherein Japan’s major banking groups, including MUFG, SMBC, and Mizuho, are exploring yen stablecoins and cross-border payments.

You Can Get Google’s New Smart Glasses Next Month. I’ve Tried Them Out

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Warby Parker and Gentle Monster's Google and Samsung-created smart glasses have cameras like Meta's, but these have some subtle differences when it comes to AI.

In Gentle Monster’s hallucinatory showroom, I stared up at four robotic heads turning and talking inside floating spheres. I listened to them speak different languages. All of them were translated in my ears, on the fly, as I wore a pair of Gentle Monster smart glasses, “Intelligent Eyewear” codeveloped by Google and Samsung, which will finally arrive Dec. 10. They’ll be Google’s first pair of smart glasses since its Google Glass days more than a decade ago, and they’re landing at a time when public opinions on AI, smart glasses and data privacy couldn’t be more heated.

Google’s return to smart glasses has been promised for nearly two years, but the first waves of actual prescription-ready eyewear are only now rolling in nearly at year’s end, an unusual time for a product launch. Really, it feels more like a kickoff for efforts that will continue next year, as Google also works on AI-enabled glasses that have displays built in.

I’d worn Google and Samsung’s smart glasses in multiple demos before, but this month for the first time I got to try on and test the final versions designed by Warby Parker and Gentle Monster. I also visited two immersive showrooms the companies are opening in New York City to demo how these glasses work. These showrooms will be open by appointment.

The showrooms were like playgrounds, full of installation art to try the glasses in. Warby’s space was a series of library-themed rooms, with travel themes and whimsical sculptures. Gentle Monster’s was a stark, surreal series of rooms, blending industrial design and realistic (sometimes robotic) horses, people, chickens, floating heads, and … a big blue gentle monster.

The glasses I wore give me a strong sense of deja vu after years of reviewing Meta smart glasses and lots of others, but there are also signs of how their deeper-connected Gemini smarts could be a big difference-maker — and likely a sign of how Apple might approach its own glasses expected in 2027.

The various models of smart glasses across Warby Parker and Gentle Monster designs, which start at $395 and $449 respectively, all work the same: The frames do not have displays, just standard glasses lenses that support a range of prescriptions and lens types that match what Warby and Gentle Monster already offer. They have speakers on the arms that project audio, along with four beamforming microphones that filter out background noise and focus on the direction you’re facing. They have a single camera on the left side, and an LED light indicator on the right.

I got to try on all the frame designs and then wore frames with my prescription lenses popped in so that I could actually use the glasses around the exhibits. The glasses feel reminiscent of what Meta’s made for years, with similar-looking frame size and arms compared to recent Ray-Ban Gen 3 models. Most of the frames weigh around 40 grams, which felt pretty easy on my face, but I wear chunky glasses to begin with.

And the frame designs, while mostly clean and somewhat conservative, all looked really good. Warby Parker’s collection of designs came in several understated color shades (I liked the dark green Pine the best), and familiar round and square frames.

All of the Intelligent Eyewear models across both brands have cameras, a feature that Google’s head of XR, Shahram Izadi, still sees as key to having a deep assistive experience, although he’s also open to the idea of future versions that could be camera-free like Meta’s recent Ray-Ban Audio model.

Cameras have been a big flashpoint in smart glasses over the past year, with concerns over harassment and privacy, certain countries and businesses banning them and worries about them being socially inappropriate.

These glasses use the same type of indicator system as Meta’s glasses do, flashing an LED that also auto-adjusts brightness outdoors and playing a sound when taking photos and videos.

I was particularly impressed by how Gemini can just listen to any snippet of language and translate it to what language you need, a trick that makes these glasses feel like a Swiss Army knife for travel. Several demos showed this off, including the surreal room of robotic heads that discussed Gentle Monster’s design philosophy in a handful of languages. I heard them all, sometimes with a short delay or hiccup, over 10 minutes or so.

The glasses can recognize written languages, too, in a much larger range than I’m used to: over 70. Live translation kicks in by pressing and holding two fingers on the glasses temple, and the translated voices change to try to match different speakers in a room as well as varied intonation.

Pressing and holding the side temple turns on Gemini, and once it’s on, it’s on until a significant pause of about 15 seconds or so. That meant that I could keep chatting and asking questions without asking “Hey Google” (or “Hey Gemini,” a new trigger phrase). I could end a session by swiping backward on the arm.

Any of the chats I have can also be turned into a note or summary instantly, hooking into Google Keep. The glasses connect with Google Maps, and Izadi promises that most of Gemini’s key features on phones will carry over to the glasses. On iPhones, the glasses will be able to triangulate across Google apps via the cloud to access Gmail, calendars and more.

I found a few clever demos that showed me some promise. In a pantry installation full of world foods in Gentle Monster’s showroom, I asked Gemini to compare brands of international mayo in front of me. I could point to things and casually ask, “What is this?” And Gemini knew what I was referring to. In another demo, Gemini knew the sheet music I was holding up and played back the matching song. In another demo, I sat down and gave myself a tarot card reading — Gemini could read the cards, regardless of whether the cards were right-side up or reversed.

Other times, Gemini said text I asked it to read was too far away, even though I could read it on a wall. Sometimes it seemed to hallucinate an odd answer to a question. (It responded with “8 minutes” to a question on a wall about whether it was OK to wait 45 minutes for a restaurant table.)

Tapping the temple arm starts and stops music, swiping changes tracks and two-finger swipes change volume (which was a bit confusing). Meanwhile, a top button on the arm takes photos and videos just like Meta glasses do, but in some demo sessions, it seemed like photos got beamed over to a Pixel phone much faster than they do to Meta’s app.

The music playback sounded good based on some quick tries in a crowded room. The audio seemed particularly loud, maybe even a bit more so than Meta’s glasses, but I’ll know more on that when I review.

The real test of Google’s Intelligent Eyewear is in how truly well connected those AI services feel and on the battery life. Meta’s up-to-eight-hour battery life on the Scriber Optics glasses I’ve been wearing the last few months tend to drain battery fast in heavy use. Will Google manage battery better, or similarly?

It’s the AI features and Google app hook-ins that could give Google a leg up. My several hours wearing pairs and wandering through two different immersive public installation spaces only showed me hints, and really, I’m most curious about how the glasses can tap into my entire Gemini history, my Gmail, my Google Docs, or my Google Maps history. How smart and connected can they be, and how good (or unsettling) will that feel?

For now, it’s Google’s approach to privacy on its services for these AI glasses that has a chance to stand apart. Cameras are standard for this first wave of glasses, but Gemini treats camera feeds in AI mode differently than how photos and videos are taken otherwise, Google’s Juston Payne, senior director of project management for XR, told me. Always-on Gemini modes with camera don’t share any photos or videos for AI training or for targeting ads against, and none of the camera captures in AI modes can be seen or shared by the wearer or Google, either. Faces are autoredacted using AI on your connected phone before being sent to the cloud for AI analysis, Payne says.

That’s encouraging, but Google’s already raising questions of AI privacy with targeted ads in Gemini-powered Google search on phones and computers.

Google’s also not enabling any livestreaming from these glasses, a move that leans towards careful compared to Meta’s livestreaming modes on its glasses. “We’ll always take the conservative path right now just to rebuild trust,” Payne said.

The glasses also won’t be able to continuously record audio ambiently to create memory summaries like Apple’s new in-beta Siri Recap feature on its recent watches can or like other smart glasses sometimes do. Also, they don’t have any environmental listening features yet, something Google says is also privacy-focused, but music recognition and other audio awareness features are on the road map. In Gemini-based audio chats with AI, Google also scrubs out voices that might be chatting near you.

But it remains to be seen how much any of this will help make anyone in public feel comfortable with someone wearing a pair of these glasses with camera-enabled AI modes on. It’s already hard to convince people that smart glasses aren’t recording people all the time, and Google’s clearly trying to restore confidence in the idea of smart glasses in public situations.

The Warby Parker and Gentle Monster models are not the only new smart wearables Google has coming: A pair of tethered mixed reality glasses that come with a processor puck and work almost like a portable VR headset, called Xreal Aura, is arriving in the next few weeks. And Google’s also starting to test its display-enabled Intelligent Eyewear ahead of a possible launch next year.

Izadi also told me that more assistive features are coming to these camera-enabled glasses, including what Google calls Guided Vision, something of an extension to the Project Astra tech Google has been working on for years. “You’ll hear more about our plans in the coming months,” Izadi said.

But agentic AI is clearly the road going forward, something both Google and Meta are expanding on for glasses. “We’ll bring more and more agentic and core AI features so that people can depend more on the AI capabilities,” Izadi said. Google’s leaning on these glasses being true phone extensions, as well as watch-connected.

But there could be a lot of cascading hardware pieces in between. Google and Samsung have already promised multiple waves of tethered and wireless smart glasses, ones with displays and ones without, and these glasses are apparently just the start.

I’m curious to see how well Warby Parker and Gentle Monster approach support for the glasses and how easy it’ll be to understand how they work. Dec. 10 isn’t far away.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/finance/duet-nabs-15m-series-plans-launch-first-np-led-aco-2027" hreflang="en">Duet nabs $15M series A, plans to launch first NP-led ACO in 2027</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →
Duet nabs $15M series A, plans to launch first NP-led ACO in 2027" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Nurse practitioner-owned primary care practice network Duet secured $15 million in series A funding to expand its clinic network and grow its partnerships and team.

Kairos led the funding round, with participation from Lerer Hippeau.

Kairos co-founder and co-CEO Alex Fiance said in a statement the company is “one of those rare businesses where everybody wins.”

“It reaches patients in need, helps clinicians become business owners, and brings costs down for the payers and the U.S. government by catching problems early,” Fiance said. “We believe Duet has the potential to reshape care across the country in a highly scalable way and are excited to help fuel their growth.”

Duet is the largest network of independent NP-owned primary care practices in the U.S., the company says. It helps practices earn compensation through its various partnerships with insurers, including Anthem, Cigna and Centene.

Duet Founder and CEO Jonathan Goldberg told Fierce Healthcare the company’s platform “combines practice advisors with AI-powered tools” to offload administrative work and give NPs more time for patient care.

“Our technology identifies people who are drifting out of care and gets them scheduled. It threads together patient data ahead of every visit, so an NP knows who is at risk and what to focus on,” Goldberg said. “And we help extend care into the home for those who need extra attention with remote technology and wraparound support.”

Goldberg continued, “There’s a lot stacked against small practices. Our mission is to help NPs show up for people that the system has overlooked.”

Patients visiting Duet clinics had an overall NPS of 92 across approximately 400,000 visits a year, Goldberg said.

“Patients often comment that this is the first time they felt heard, had someone take the time to read through their chart, or had a condition diagnosed that other clinicians had dismissed,” Goldberg said. “The clinical results follow from that. Practices that join Duet see preventive screenings for conditions like breast cancer and colon cancer rise 30%, and ER visits fall 20%. We also bring back 25% of the patients who are unengaged, and those are the patients who tend to end up worse off.”

The company has raised $22.5 million to date and works with more than 130 clinics across 21 states, executives say.

Goldberg said the company plans to use the fresh capital in three main ways: serve more clinics, deepen its value-based care partnerships with insurers and add engineers to its team. He added that Duet is launching an accelerator program in 2027 to help clinics “start strong.

Alongside the funding round, Duet announced it plans to launch the first-ever NP-led Medicare Accountable Care Organization (ACO) in 2027.

NPs will design and govern the company’s new model, which Goldberg says will allow practice owners to maintain “their autonomy while getting the technology and resources to strengthen their care.”

“NPs already practice prevention first, and this gives them a way to be recognized for it,” he said.

Logitech MX Master 4 Special Edition review: Still the productivity mouse to beat, but should you upgrade?

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Packed with digital art tutorials, inspiration and expert advice from ImagineFX.

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A brilliant, comfortable productivity mouse. The haptics and Actions Ring reward creatives who’ll map shortcuts, but Options+ takes work, and for most MX Master 3S owners or non-creatives, it’s a marginal upgrade.

Comfortable, well-judged weight with real desk presence

Special Edition colourway offers a touch of personality

Haptic feedback adds useful tactile confirmation

Actions Ring speeds up app switching and folder access

MagSpeed scrolling is still class-leading

Needs time in Options+ to get the best from it

Marginal upgrade over the MX Master 3S unless you map shortcuts to the Action Ring

Why you can trust Creative Bloq Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Product: Logitech MX Master 4 (Special Edition)

Sensor & Tracking: Darkfield high precision, 200-8000 DPI

Scroll: MagSpeed electromagnetic wheel plus thumb wheel

Connectivity: Bluetooth and Logi Bolt receiver

Software: Logitech Options+ (Windows and macOS)

Best for: creatives, office workers and anyone with long desk sessions

The MX Master 4 Special Edition takes Logitech's excellent black mouse and spruces it up with a new white colourway and red-and-grey accents. Inside, it's the same MX Master 4, and like the 2025 version, it has a tough act to follow. Its predecessor, the MX Master 3S, is already one of the best productivity mice you can buy, so Logitech never intended to reinvent the wheel. Instead, we get more customisation, haptic feedback, and a handful of design tweaks, and in this Special Edition, a more personalised colour choice.

These are all the developments I’d expect from a follow-up to the excellent MX Master 3S, one of the best mice you can buy, but they make this a nicer-looking version of a mouse that was a smart evolution rather than a must-have upgrade, unless, I'd argue, you're a creative; then the Actions Ring becomes a bigger thing than you may expect and is new to MX Master 4, Special Edition or not.

The shape is the same as the MX Master 3S, with its sculpted ergonomic design, deep thumb rest, and plenty of controls to keep your hands off the keyboard. It’s comfortable for long workdays, and the weight is spot on. It has enough heft to feel planted on the desk without feeling clumsy. It’s big, though, and it won’t suit everyone. This is a mouse for your desk, not your laptop bag.

• For more control consider one of the best drawing tablets

The MagSpeed wheel is as good as ever. Flick it, and it free-spins through a long spreadsheet or a 40-page document, then ratchets back to click-by-click scrolling when you need precision. Switching between the two is automatic, which is why it’s so easy to take for granted.

The finish is where things have changed. This is the Special Edition of last year’s MX Master 4 release, so the design changes over the MX Master 3S remain. Logitech has swapped the soft, rubberised coating of older models for something harder, but it’s not slick or glassy. The cover has a nice bit of tooth, and different areas have subtly different textures, so your fingers always know where they are without you looking. It feels different from the old MX Masters. A colleague wasn’t keen on it when they tried [2025’s MX Master 4], but I got used to it quickly, so your mileage may vary, and existing owners may need a few days to adjust.

Those alterations aside, the Special Edition replaces flat blacks with a soft grey-white colour and red accents. The dark grey Actions Ring thumb rest pops in this colourway, and I far prefer it to the faceless black PC-mouse aesthetic of the base model. There’s no price difference between black and Special Edition.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

The thumb rest gets the biggest rethink. It’s usually the most wasted area of a mouse, a bit of plastic to rest your thumb on. Here, it’s the way into the new virtual dial, called the Actions Ring, which makes it feel like part of the design rather than an afterthought.

The haptics are a lovely touch. They kick in when you make a selection and when you open and cycle through the Actions Ring, so I can keep my eyes on the screen, click and scroll, and still feel a little resistance and feedback. This also helps with gesture control, too, if you go as far as needing that kind of input. It’s subtle, but it makes the mouse feel more connected to what you’re doing.

As you may have guessed, the Actions Ring is the real star of the new features. It’s an on-screen dial that expands what the mouse can do, making it easy to open folders or switch between apps without dropping into the Windows menus. You can tailor the ring to the job, from something as basic as Google to Photoshop or DaVinci Resolve. Support isn’t universal, though. The best digital art and design apps like Krita, Affinity Photo and CorelDRAW are covered, but smaller apps like Escape Motions Rebelle aren’t a default option, so you’ll need a workaround: assign function keys in Options+, then match them to the app’s own shortcuts for undo, clear layer or brush toggles. It works, but you need to know your keys or set them up ahead of time.

This also means getting to grips with Options+ and its app features, which is a lot of learning for a mouse. I’d like it to work more without that software running in the background. But if you persevere, it adds real depth and makes the mouse faster to use and more flexible. Once it’s set up, you’ll love it. You can tweak everything from haptic strength to scroll speed, and I’d probably be moaning if I couldn’t fiddle.

The haptics, gestures, and Actions Ring are a step beyond the MX Master 3S, and creatives who map shortcuts to the ring will get the most from this mouse.

So, should you buy the MX Master 4 Special Edition? If you’re coming from a basic mouse, absolutely. The ergonomics, scrolling and customisation make it a brilliant tool for anyone who works at a computer all day, whether in creative apps or simply navigating menus and Google.

If you already own an MX Master 3S, it comes down to how you work. The haptics and Actions Ring are genuinely useful and, for creative apps, a new, easier way to work, so it’s worth it if you’ll map shortcuts to the ring or, like me, hate sorting through Windows menus and would rather one-click into the regular apps and files. For everyone else, they’re not enough to make a perfectly good mouse obsolete.
